**Model answer, Q7:** "Four things, in order. Is the process running as a user that owns the directory or belongs to its group — `ps -o user,group,pid -p <pid>` or just check the service's unit file. Is the write bit actually set for that class. Is there an immutable attribute or a read-only mount in the way. And if it's SELinux or AppArmor, whether the policy allows the write, which `ausearch` or the audit log will tell you. I'd check ownership before permission bits, because that's the more common cause."
**What they evaluate**
- **Reading `755` fluently**, without counting on your fingers.
- **Understanding that directory permissions mean something different** than file permissions — this is the actual test in the question.
- **Least-privilege instinct**, rather than reaching for `777`.
**Probe:** "Your service writes as `www-data` but the directory is owned by `deploy`. What's the cleanest fix?" Or: "Why is `chmod -R 777` never the answer?"
**Common wrong answers:** `777` as a fix for anything; thinking the execute bit on a directory means "run it"; using `sudo` to solve a permissions problem without checking ownership; believing 644 is safe for a file containing a secret; forgetting that `chmod -R` follows into `.git`.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Group-based permissions vs per-user:** groups let several people share access without adding every user, at the cost of making "who can read this?" harder to answer without checking group membership.
- **Numeric vs symbolic `chmod`:** numeric is compact and diff-friendly in scripts; symbolic is self-documenting and less likely to be wrong by a digit.
- **Running as root vs a service account:** root removes permission friction and removes your ability to contain a compromise. Services win.
**Mistakes:** `chmod 777` as a first response; `chmod -R` on a broad path; leaving `.env` at 644; creating files as root with `sudo` and then being unable to edit them as your user; forgetting that a private key with loose permissions is refused by `ssh` on purpose.
**Mini task:** Create a directory and a script, then reproduce the SSH failure by loosening a key to 644 and reading the error. Then write a `umask`-based one-liner that makes new files private by default.
**Resources:** `man chmod`, `man umask`; the Linux Filesystem Hierarchy standard for the ownership conventions under `/etc`, `/var` and `/home`; your web server's user documentation.
**Self-check:** Can you look at any permission string and say what each character means, and explain why directory permissions are read and execute rather than read and write?

## 2.2.3 Pipes, Redirection, and Text Tools
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Composing commands by connecting their standard streams — stdout of one into stdin of another — and redirecting those streams to and from files.
**Why interviewers ask:** This is the Unix philosophy in one question: small tools that compose. The interviewer is checking whether you can debug in an unfamiliar environment with nothing but coreutils, which is exactly what a first ten minutes on a production box looks like.
**Prerequisites:** 2.2.1.

**Key concepts**
Streams: **stdin (0), stdout (1), stderr (2).**

- **`>` truncates.** This is the mistake everyone makes once: `grep foo access.log > access.log` empties the file before `grep` ever reads it.
- **Order matters in `2>&1`:** `cmd > out 2>&1` points stderr at wherever stdout is *currently* pointing, so it works; `cmd 2>&1 > out` points stderr at the terminal and only then redirects stdout, so stderr escapes.
- **Pipes connect stdout only.** `cmd1 | cmd2` does not send `cmd1`'s stderr anywhere, which is why you see `2>&1` chained into pipes so often — and why a pipeline can appear to hang when the real error is scrolling past on the terminal.
- **`&&`, `||`, `;` are about exit status, not output.** `&&` runs the next command only if the last one exited 0.
- **Exit codes are the API.** `0` means success; anything else means the tool is telling you it failed, and that's what a script and a CI step act on.

```bash
cmd > file        # stdout to file (overwrite)
cmd >> file       # append
cmd 2> err.log    # stderr to file
cmd > out 2>&1    # both streams to one file
cmd1 | cmd2       # stdout of cmd1 becomes stdin of cmd2
cmd < input.txt   # stdin from file
cmd1 && cmd2      # run cmd2 only if cmd1 succeeded
cmd1 || cmd2      # run cmd2 only if cmd1 failed
cmd1 ; cmd2       # run both regardless
```

**Text tools**
```bash
grep -rniE "error|fail" ./logs        # recursive, line numbers, case-insens., regex
grep -v "healthcheck" access.log      # invert match
find . -name "*.log" -mtime +7        # files modified more than 7 days ago
find . -type f -size +100M            # large files
find . -name node_modules -prune -o -name "*.js" -print
sed 's/foo/bar/g' file                # substitute (use -i to edit in place; macOS needs -i '')
awk '{print $1, $9}' access.log       # print columns 1 and 9
awk -F, '{sum += $3} END {print sum}' sales.csv
sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head      # frequency count, top N
wc -l file                            # count lines
cut -d, -f1,3 file.csv                # extract CSV columns
xargs                                 # build commands from stdin
jq '.items[] | .name' data.json       # JSON processing
```

**Classic one-liner (log analysis): top 10 IPs hitting your server**
```bash
awk '{print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head -10
```

**Count 500 errors in a log (nginx combined format, status is column 9)**
```bash
awk '$9 == 500' access.log | wc -l
```

**Questions**
1. What are stdin, stdout and stderr, and what are their file descriptors?
2. `>` vs `>>` vs `2>` vs `2>&1`?
3. Why does `grep foo app.log > app.log` empty the file?
4. Explain `awk '{print $1, $9}' access.log`.
5. Count 500s in an nginx access log.
6. Find the ten IPs hitting your server most.
7. `&&` vs `||` vs `;`?
8. A pipeline hangs and prints nothing. How do you debug it?
9. Why is `cut`/`awk` preferred over `sed` for CSV, and what breaks when you use `sed`?

**Model answer, Q3:** "The shell opens and truncates `app.log` for the `>` redirection before it runs `grep`. `grep` then reads an empty file and matches nothing. The fix is to write to a different file and move it, or read and write in one pass with a tool that can do both, like `sed -i` or `awk`."
**Model answer, Q4:** "Awk reads a line at a time and splits it into fields. With the default whitespace separator, `$1` is the first field and `$9` is the ninth, and the surrounding single quotes stop the shell from expanding them — which is essential, because otherwise `$1` would be replaced by the shell's first positional argument, which is empty in an interactive shell. In an nginx combined log the ninth field is the status code, so that prints the client IP and the status for every request."
**Model answer, Q8:** "First I'd check whether the left-hand command is producing output at all, by running it alone and looking at stderr separately, because a pipe only carries stdout. A very common cause is a program blocked writing to stderr that nobody is reading, or a program waiting on stdin that never gets EOF. I'd break the pipeline in half, then add `2>&1` to see the errors that were going to the terminal, and check with `-o pipefail` whether an upstream stage actually failed while a later stage exited 0."
**What they evaluate**
- **Whether you can build a pipeline,** not whether you memorised flags.
- **Understanding that pipes carry stdout only** — the single most common practical misunderstanding.
- **Quoting discipline:** knowing why `"$1"` inside awk must survive the shell.
**Probe:** "Why does `cmd 2>&1 > out` not capture stderr?" Or: "How would you count distinct user agents in an access log?"
**Common wrong answers:** thinking pipes carry stderr; writing `> file` on the file you're reading; `awk '$8'` for nginx status (it's `$9`); forgetting quotes around awk programs and silently getting an empty result; treating `;` as a conditional.
**Trade-offs:**
- **`grep` vs `awk` vs `jq`:** grep for "does this string appear", awk for columns and arithmetic, jq for structured data. Using the wrong one is slower and harder to read than the pipeline you skipped.
- **Streaming vs loading:** these tools stream, which is why they handle gigabyte logs on a small box. The trade is that a stream can't easily do a second pass without `tee`.
- **Pipelines vs a script:** a one-liner is fast to type and impossible to review six months later. Anything you'd have to explain to someone else belongs in a named script.
**Mistakes:** `>` on the source file; omitting quotes so the shell eats `$1`; assuming `grep -r` skips binary files gracefully; using `sed` on CSV and breaking fields that contain commas; `sort | uniq` without `-c` and wondering why every line is unique.
**Mini task:** From a real nginx log, produce: total requests, requests by status class, the ten busiest IPs, the slowest endpoint by average bytes, and every request that took over 5 seconds. Write each as a named one-liner with a comment.
**Resources:** `man awk`, `man grep`; the GNU Awk User's Guide; "Faster filters" and shell scripting chapters in *The Linux Command Line*.
**Self-check:** Can you write, unaided, the pipeline that counts errors by status code from a log, and explain why the quotes matter?

## 2.2.4 Environment Variables
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Configuration passed to a process from outside the process, inherited by its children, and read at runtime instead of being hard-coded.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because it is where the line between configuration and secrets gets drawn, and candidates routinely get it wrong in both directions. Putting a database password in source control is a junior mistake; refusing to put *any* configuration in an environment variable and hard-coding it is a different kind of mistake that interviewers also enjoy catching.
**Prerequisites:** 2.2.1.

**Key concepts**
- Key-value pairs inherited by child processes. Used for configuration and secrets (12-factor).
```bash
export API_KEY=abc123          # set for this shell and its children
printenv API_KEY               # read
echo "$PATH"                   # directories searched for commands
API_KEY=abc node app.js        # set for one command only
env | grep NODE                # inspect
```
- **`PATH`:** why `command not found` happens: the binary isn't in a directory listed in `PATH`.
- **`.env` files:** loaded by tools (`dotenv`, Docker Compose, Vite). **Never commit them.** Provide `.env.example`.
- **Persistence:** add to `~/.bashrc` / `~/.zshrc` (shell-wide) or use a secrets manager (production).
- **Node:** `process.env.API_KEY` is always a *string*, so `"false"` is truthy.
- **Frontend caution:** env vars bundled into client code (`VITE_*`, `NEXT_PUBLIC_*`) are public. Never put secrets there.

- **Precedence is a real debugging skill.** A shell export, a `.env` file, a container env, a CI secret and a platform dashboard can all set the same name, and the winner is usually "whatever ran last". Being able to state the order for your stack is worth more than memorising the syntax.
- **The four-factor test for whether something belongs in an env var:** does it differ per environment, does it contain anything sensitive, does it change without a deploy, and would a build cache poison it if it were baked in?
- **Frontend vars are a public channel, not a secret store.** The build tool inlines them into the bundle. This is not a flaw to work around; it is the design, and the only correct response is to never put a secret behind a `PUBLIC_` prefix.
- **Config is not the same as secrets, but they share a mechanism.** A feature flag is config; a database password is a secret. The operational difference is that config is often checked in as a template while secrets are injected at deploy time and never written to disk.
- **Validation at startup, not at use.** Reading `process.env.PORT` and getting `undefined` deep inside a request handler turns a config error into a mystery 500. Parse and validate config once when the process boots, and fail loudly.

**Questions**
1. What are environment variables, and how do they reach a child process?
2. Why `command not found`? What does the shell actually search?
3. How do you make a variable available to every future shell? *(and why is that a bad place for app config)*
4. `.env` files — how do they work, and why is `.env.example` the thing you commit?
5. Why is `process.env.FEATURE_ENABLED === "false"` still truthy in Node?
6. Are `VITE_API_KEY` / `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` secrets? Explain.
7. How do you pass config into a Docker container, and how does that differ from baking it into the image?
8. What's the precedence order when the same variable is set in five places?

**Model answer, Q3:** "I'd put it in `~/.bashrc` or `~/.zshrc` for shell-level things like an editor or a language version, but not for application configuration. A dotfile applies to every shell you open on that machine, which means the value silently follows you to other projects and you lose track of which project expects which value. App config belongs in the project's `.env` file, a secret store, or an injected environment variable at run time, where it's scoped to one process."
**Model answer, Q5:** "Because environment variables are always strings — the OS has no types. `'false'` is a non-empty string, and in JavaScript any non-empty string is truthy, including `'false'` and `'0'`. So I compare explicitly: `process.env.FEATURE_ENABLED === 'true'`, and better still I parse it once at startup into a real boolean so the mistake can only be made in one place."
**Model answer, Q6:** "No. `VITE_*` and `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` are inlined into the JavaScript bundle at build time, so they end up in the browser for anyone who opens DevTools. They're a configuration channel for the client, not a secret store. Anything sensitive has to stay server-side, and the test is simple: if you would be uncomfortable pasting its value into a public GitHub issue, it doesn't belong in a `PUBLIC_` variable."
**What they evaluate**
- **Knowing the mechanism** — the process environment is inherited, and children get a copy.
- **The typed-string trap,** which shows you have actually run this in production.
- **Distinguishing config from secrets,** and understanding that client-side env vars are public.
**Probe:** "Your app reads `API_URL` and it works locally and fails in production. How do you find out what it actually resolved to?" Or: "Why do secrets in env vars still need rotation?"
**Common wrong answers:** treating env vars as a place to put long-lived config and then being surprised it differs per environment; committing `.env`; assuming `NEXT_PUBLIC_*` is private because the name looked internal; forgetting `"false"` is truthy; putting API keys in frontend env vars.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Env vars vs a config file:** env vars are container-friendly and end up in process listings and CI logs if you're careless; a checked-in config file is visible and versioned but can hold a secret. The usual answer is env vars for values and a versioned file for structure.
- **Build-time vs run-time config:** baking values into the image makes them immutable and cacheable, and forces a rebuild to change them. Reading at run time makes the image portable across environments, which is why containers are built once and deployed many times.
- **Fail fast vs fail silently:** validating config at boot turns a deploy into a loud failure, at the cost of refusing to start on a missing optional value. Validate required values, default the optional ones.
**Mistakes:** committing `.env`; printing secrets in startup logs; using `VITE_` for anything sensitive; comparing an env var to a boolean instead of a string; setting config in `~/.bashrc` where it leaks across projects.
**Mini task:** Build a tiny app that reads four variables, validates them at startup with a clear error message, and prints which variables were resolved and from where. Then run it with one missing and confirm it fails loudly instead of at the first request.
**Resources:** `man environ`; the twelve-factor app's config section; your framework's env-var docs (Next.js, Vite) — specifically the sections on which vars are exposed to the client.
**Self-check:** Can you name four things that change per environment, say which of them are secrets, and explain why a `PUBLIC_` prefixed variable is not one?

## 2.2.5 Processes and Signals
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** A running instance of a program, and the OS mechanism for telling it to stop — including what happens when it refuses.
**Why interviewers ask:** "How do you find which process is using port 3000 and kill it" is a genuinely common practical question, and the follow-up — "why does `kill -9` break my database connection pooling?" — separates people who have deployed from people who have only read. Graceful shutdown is a backend engineer's responsibility, and knowing about it puts you ahead.
**Prerequisites:** 2.2.2.

**Key concepts**
```bash
ps aux | grep node                # list processes
top / htop                        # live resource usage
lsof -i :3000                     # who is using port 3000? (macOS/Linux)
ss -tulpn | grep 3000             # Linux alternative
kill 1234                         # SIGTERM (15): polite request to terminate
kill -9 1234                      # SIGKILL (9): cannot be caught, no cleanup
kill -HUP 1234                    # SIGHUP: often "reload config"
pkill -f "node server.js"
cmd &                             # run in background
jobs; fg; bg                      # job control
nohup cmd > out.log 2>&1 &        # survive terminal close
```
- **Signals:** `SIGINT` (2, Ctrl+C), `SIGTERM` (15, default `kill`, what Docker and Kubernetes send on stop), `SIGKILL` (9, forced).
- **Graceful shutdown (important for backend):** handle `SIGTERM`, stop accepting requests, finish in-flight work, close DB connections, then exit.
```js
process.on('SIGTERM', () => {
  server.close(async () => {   // stop accepting new connections
    await db.end();            // release resources
    process.exit(0);
  });
});
```
- **Exit codes:** `0` = success, non-zero = failure. `$?` holds the last exit code. CI uses this to pass or fail a step.
- **Daemon/service:** a long-running background process, usually managed by `systemd` (`systemctl status|restart nginx`, `journalctl -u nginx -f`).
- **Zombie process:** finished but not reaped by its parent. **Orphan:** parent died, adopted by init.
- **`kill -9` cannot be caught, blocked or cleaned up after.** The process gets no chance to flush, close sockets or finish a transaction. It is a last resort, and using it routinely hides the bug that made the process refuse to stop.
- **The full set of signals worth knowing:** `SIGHUP` reload config, `SIGINT` interactive interrupt, `SIGTERM` polite shutdown request, `SIGKILL` uncatchable, `SIGSTOP`/`SIGCONT` suspend and resume, `SIGPIPE` write to a closed pipe (which is why programs often ignore it).
- **Why a port stays occupied after `kill`:** the socket is in `TIME_WAIT` because the side that closes first pays for it. `ss -tulpn` shows it; a server with `SO_REUSEADDR` (the usual default) can rebind anyway, so "address already in use" after a kill usually means a *different* process still holds it, or you're looking at a lingering child.
- **Process groups matter in containers.** `docker stop` sends `SIGTERM` to PID 1 only; if your shell script is PID 1 it must forward the signal to its children, or the orchestrator waits out the full grace period and then sends `SIGKILL`.

**Questions**
1. How do you find which process is using port 3000 and kill it?
2. `kill` vs `kill -9`? What can each actually do?
3. What is a zombie process?
4. How would you gracefully shut down a Node server, and why does it matter in Kubernetes?
5. What is `SIGTERM` and what does your orchestrator send on deploy?
6. My process ignores `SIGTERM`. What do I do?
7. What are exit codes and why does CI care?

**Model answer, Q1:** "`lsof -i :3000` on macOS or Linux, or `ss -tulpn | grep 3000`. That gives me the PID. Then I'd try `kill <pid>` first, which sends `SIGTERM` and gives the process a chance to close connections cleanly, wait a few seconds, and only escalate to `kill -9` if it's still there. On a shared box I'd want to confirm the process is mine before killing it, and in a container or under a process manager I'd use the manager instead, so it can restart the service properly."
**Model answer, Q4:** "I'd register a handler for `SIGTERM` that stops accepting new connections, lets in-flight requests finish, closes the database pool and any queues, then exits 0. It matters because Kubernetes and Docker both send `SIGTERM` on deploy and then wait for a grace period before `SIGKILL`. If the app dies immediately, in-flight requests are cut off and clients see errors; if it ignores the signal, every deploy burns the full grace period and then gets hard-killed, which can leave connections and transactions half-open. The same pattern applies to draining consumers before letting the process exit."
**What they evaluate**
- **Escalation discipline:** `SIGTERM` before `SIGKILL`, always.
- **Whether you know graceful shutdown is your job**, including the orchestrator's grace period.
- **Practical tool knowledge** for identifying a process by port.
**Probe:** "Your app takes 30 seconds to shut down and the orchestrator gives you 10. What happens, and what would you change?" Or: "Why is `kill -9` on a database dangerous?"
**Common wrong answers:** going straight to `kill -9`; `kill -15` and `kill -9` described as the same thing with different numbers; thinking signals are "messages" the process can always read; confusing a zombie with an orphan; forgetting that `kill -9` skips cleanup entirely.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Graceful vs immediate shutdown:** graceful shutdown protects in-flight work but consumes the grace period and needs a timeout; hard kill is instant and leaves state inconsistent. Real systems need both, with a bounded wait.
- **Foreground vs daemon/systemd/nohup:** foreground is debuggable and visible; backgrounding survives your terminal but hides output unless you redirect it. systemd gives you restart-on-failure and logs for free.
- **Signals vs a control endpoint:** signals are universal and need no port or auth, but they can't carry data. A shutdown endpoint can, at the cost of a new attack surface.
**Mistakes:** `kill -9` first and always; killing a PID found by `grep` without checking it's the process you meant; running PID 1 in a container without forwarding signals; no exit code on failure so CI passes a broken build; leaving orphaned processes after killing a parent.
**Mini task:** Write a Node script that handles `SIGTERM` with a drain timeout, then prove it by starting it, sending a request, killing it, and showing the request completed rather than being cut off. Then run it under Docker and watch the grace period.
**Resources:** `man 7 signal`, `man kill`, `man systemd`; the Docker docs on `STOPSIGNAL` and signal handling; Kubernetes docs on pod termination and `terminationGracePeriodSeconds`.
**Self-check:** Can you explain, in one sentence each, why `kill -9` skips cleanup, why a graceful shutdown needs a timeout, and what an orchestrator does while it waits?

## 2.2.6 SSH Basics
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** Encrypted remote shell access over port 22, using public-key cryptography instead of passwords, plus local port forwarding for reaching services that aren't exposed.
**Why interviewers ask:** It is a cheap question that still distinguishes people who've administered a server. The follow-ups — "how would you harden this?" and "how do you reach a database that isn't publicly exposed?" — are real operational concerns that most junior candidates can't answer.
**Prerequisites:** 2.2.2 (file permissions matter here).

**Key concepts**
```bash
ssh-keygen -t ed25519 -C "you@example.com"   # generate a key pair
ssh user@host                                 # connect
ssh -i ~/.ssh/key user@host
scp file user@host:/path                      # copy over SSH
rsync -avz ./dist/ user@host:/var/www/app/    # efficient sync
ssh -L 5432:localhost:5432 user@host          # local port forward (tunnel to remote DB)
```
- **Key-based auth:** private key stays on your machine (`chmod 600`), public key goes in the server's `~/.ssh/authorized_keys`. Never share or commit the private key.
- **`~/.ssh/config`** for aliases:
```
Host prod
  HostName 203.0.113.10
  User deploy
  IdentityFile ~/.ssh/id_ed25519
```
- **Hardening:** disable password login and root login, use a firewall, and use fail2ban (Phase 8).
- **GitHub uses SSH keys too** (`ssh -T git@github.com`).
- **The handshake is asymmetric.** The server proves its identity with a host key; your client proves yours with a public key whose private half never leaves the machine. That is why "man in the middle" is a real concern on a shared network, and why the `known_hosts` file exists.
- **`ed25519` over RSA** is the current default for a reason: same security with much smaller keys and faster handshakes on modern hardware.
- **Forwarding has three modes:** `-L` opens a local port that tunnels to the remote side (reach a private DB), `-R` opens a port on the remote machine pointing back at your local one, and `-D` is a SOCKS proxy. `-L` is by far the most common and is what you use to reach a database with no public IP.
- **`known_hosts` verification is the feature, not the annoyance.** "REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED" is either a rebuilt server or a man in the middle, and the safe response is to verify out of band, not to delete the file.
- **Hardening checklist:** key-only auth, no root login, a non-root deploy user with only the sudo it needs, a firewall, automatic security updates, and audit logging — plus removing old keys, which is the step everyone forgets.

**Questions**
1. How does SSH key authentication work, and why is it safer than a password?
2. Why does an SSH private key need `chmod 600`?
3. How do I reach a Postgres database on a server with no public IP?
4. What does `ssh -L` do exactly, and how is it different from `-R`?
5. How would you harden an SSH server?
6. What is `~/.ssh/config` and why should you use one?
7. "REMOTE HOST IDENTIFICATION HAS CHANGED" — what happened and what do you do?
8. How do you copy files to a server, and when is `rsync` better than `scp`?

**Model answer, Q1:** "The server holds a host key and proves its identity with it during the handshake; my client proves mine by signing a challenge with the private key that matches the public key in `authorized_keys`. The private key never leaves my machine and never goes over the wire — only the signature does. Passwords are worse because they're shared, reused, phishable, and brute-forceable; keys are long, can't be guessed, and can be individually revoked without rotating everyone's password."
**Model answer, Q3:** "Local port forwarding. `ssh -L 5432:localhost:5432 user@host` opens port 5432 on my laptop and forwards it, over the encrypted connection, to port 5432 on the server. So I connect to `localhost:5432` locally and reach the private database. The alternative is opening the database to the internet, which I'd rather not do — the tunnel means the service never needs to listen on a public interface."
**Model answer, Q7:** "The host key I have on file doesn't match the one the server presented now. Either the machine was rebuilt or re-imaged, which is the normal explanation, or the connection is being intercepted. I don't just delete the `known_hosts` entry to make the warning go away — I verify the new fingerprint out of band, through the provider's console, and only then accept it. If I can't verify it, I treat it as an attack."
**What they evaluate**
- **Key-only auth and non-root login** as the two hardening steps everyone should name first.
- **Whether port forwarding is understood as "don't expose the database",** which is the security instinct the question is really testing.
- **Not treating a changed host key as a nuisance to suppress.**
**Probe:** "Your teammate needs the same access. How do you give it without sharing a key?" Or: "How would you reach a service in a private subnet with no bastion host?"
**Common wrong answers:** pasting the private key into a chat or a repo; "SSH encrypts everything so it's secure" with no mention of key management; exposing a database publicly and calling it a firewall; deleting `known_hosts` on a warning; using `root@` for day-to-day work.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Key-based vs password auth:** keys are far stronger and revocable individually, but managing them across a team and a laptop fleet is real work. That's what a bastion or an identity provider is for.
- **Jump host vs flat access:** a jump host is a single audited entry point, at the cost of a hop and a second thing to secure.
- **`scp` vs `rsync`:** `scp` is trivial, `rsync` only transfers differences and can be made resumable — worth it for large deploys, overkill for a single file.
**Mistakes:** committing an `id_rsa`; `chmod 777` on a key to make the error go away; leaving `PasswordAuthentication yes`; logging in as root; running a database on `0.0.0.0` when a tunnel would do.
**Mini task:** Set up a throwaway VM, create a non-root user with a key, disable password and root login, and reach a locally-bound service through a tunnel. Verify each change by trying to log in the old way and failing.
**Resources:** `man ssh`, `man ssh_config`, `man sshd_config`; the OpenSSH hardening guides from the major cloud providers; the "Sites" chapter of *The Linux Command Line*.
**Self-check:** Can you explain what `ssh -L` does in one sentence, why the private key needs `chmod 600`, and what your response to a changed host key should be?

## 2.2.7 Shell Scripting Basics
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Writing small, reliable programs in the shell that take arguments, branch on conditions, loop, call functions, and — above all — fail loudly instead of silently.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because a shell script is often the *first automation you deploy*, and because `set -euo pipefail` is the single highest-value line you can put at the top of one. Interviewers also want to see that you can tell a script written for a human to read from a script written to be trusted by a pipeline.
**Prerequisites:** 2.2.3 (pipes and redirection are assumed).

**Key concepts**
```bash
#!/usr/bin/env bash
set -euo pipefail            # exit on error, unset var, and pipe failures

APP_DIR="${1:-/var/www/app}" # first arg, with default
LOG="deploy.log"

log() { echo "[$(date +'%F %T')] $*" | tee -a "$LOG"; }

if [[ ! -d "$APP_DIR" ]]; then
  log "Directory $APP_DIR not found"; exit 1
fi

cd "$APP_DIR"
git pull --ff-only
npm ci --omit=dev
npm run build

for svc in api worker; do
  log "Restarting $svc"
  systemctl restart "$svc"
done
log "Done"
```
Concepts: shebang, variables (`"$var"`), arguments (`$1`, `$@`, `$#`), conditionals (`[[ ]]`), loops, functions, exit codes, `trap` for cleanup, and `set -euo pipefail` for safety.

- **What each flag in `set -euo pipefail` buys you.** `-e` exits on an unhandled failure, `-u` treats an unset variable as an error instead of an empty string (this is what saves you from `rm -rf "$DIR/"`), and `-o pipefail` fails the pipeline if *any* stage fails rather than just the last one. Together they convert silent corruption into a visible failure.
- **Quote every expansion.** Unquoted `$var` word-splits on whitespace and glob-expands, which turns a filename with a space into two arguments and a filename with `*` into a list of files. `"$@"` is the correct way to forward arguments to another command.
- **`[[ ]]` over `[ ]` in bash:** no word splitting, `&&` inside the condition, and a regex operator. `[ ]` is the POSIX form and behaves differently enough to be worth avoiding in new scripts.
- **`trap` for cleanup.** Register `trap cleanup EXIT` and put your teardown in one place, so the script behaves the same whether it succeeded, failed, or was interrupted.
- **Exit codes are your only reporting channel.** A script used in CI must `exit 1` on failure; a script used as a pipeline stage must propagate the failing code rather than always exiting 0.
- **Idempotency is a property of deploy scripts.** Re-running the script should be safe, which means `mkdir -p`, `git pull --ff-only`, and `npm ci` rather than anything that appends or regenerates state blindly.
- **Prefer the platform's tool where one exists.** A five-line shell script that grows to two hundred lines of `sed` is a sign it wants to be a small program with real error handling — or a Makefile, or a CI job definition, or a proper deployment tool.

**Questions**
1. What does `set -euo pipefail` do, flag by flag?
2. Why quote `"$var"`? Give a concrete failure.
3. `==` vs `=` — when does it matter?
4. How do you pass all arguments through to another command?
5. How do you make a script clean up on failure or Ctrl+C?
6. Why does a CI script need an explicit exit code?
7. Why `npm ci` instead of `npm install` in a deploy script?
8. When should a shell script become something else?

**Model answer, Q1:** "`-e` exits immediately if a command fails, so the script stops at the first problem instead of ploughing on with broken state. `-u` treats an unset variable as an error rather than an empty string — that's the one that stops `rm -rf \"$DIR/\"` from becoming `rm -rf /`. `-o pipefail` makes the pipeline return failure if *any* stage fails, not just the last, so a failing `curl | jq` doesn't look successful because `jq` exited 0. The caveat I'd mention is that `-e` has surprising interactions in conditionals and pipelines, which is why I'd still check the things that actually matter."
**Model answer, Q2:** "Unquoted expansions get word-split on whitespace and glob-expanded. A file named `my report.txt` becomes two arguments. A file named `*.log` becomes every matching file. And an empty variable disappears entirely, so `rm -rf $DIR/` with an unset `DIR` becomes `rm -rf /`. Quoting `\"$var\"` keeps it as exactly one argument. The one exception is `\"$@\"`, which is the correct way to forward all of a script's arguments."
**Model answer, Q6:** "Because the shell reports success or failure through the exit status, and `0` means success. A script that runs `npm ci` and then reaches the end without an explicit failure path exits 0, so a green CI build tells you nothing. I'd capture each critical step's status or use `set -e` with an explicit `trap` that exits non-zero, and always `exit 0` at the end of a successful path so a stray failure in a cleanup step doesn't fail the build."
**What they evaluate**
- **Whether `set -euo pipefail` is reflex,** not recited.
- **Quoting discipline,** including knowing why `"$@"` is the exception.
- **Knowing the limits** of the shell — a script that has outgrown its job should become a real program.
**Probe:** "Why is `-e` not enough on its own?" Or: "Your script works by hand but fails in CI. What are the three most likely differences?"
**Common wrong answers:** `set -e` alone as "safety"; forgetting `pipefail` and trusting the last command's status; using `[ ]` and `[ "$a" == "b" ]` with unquoted variables; echoing secrets into logs; a script that always exits 0.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Bash vs POSIX `sh`:** bash gives you arrays, `[[ ]]` and `pipefail`; `sh` is portable but you give those up. Portability is worth it only if the script really does run on more than one kind of box.
- **A shell script vs a program in Node/Python:** a script is fast to write and has no runtime dependency, but it has no real error handling, no tests and no structure. Roughly a hundred lines is the point where I'd rewrite it.
- **Inline one-liners vs named scripts:** one-liners are faster for a single investigation; named, commented, logged scripts are what you want for anything that runs on a schedule or in CI.
**Mistakes:** no `set -euo pipefail`; unquoted variables; `cd` without checking the directory exists; not using `set -a`/proper loading for env files; logging credentials; treating a script as idempotent when it appends; running as root because it was easier.
**Mini task:** Write a deploy script for a static site that: validates its arguments, checks the target exists, refuses to run without a confirmation flag when the target is production, logs with timestamps, cleans up a temp directory via `trap`, and exits non-zero on any failure. Then break it three ways and confirm each failure is loud.
**Resources:** the Bash Pitfalls wiki page (read it once); *The Linux Command Line* by William Shotts, free; Google Shell Style Guide for conventions; `man bash` for `[[`, `trap` and parameter expansion.
**Self-check:** Can you explain all three flags in `set -euo pipefail`, name the one case where quoting is wrong, and say when a shell script should stop being a shell script?

## 2.2.8 grep/find/sed/awk basics
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · the four tools you are expected to be fluent in on any machine you own

**Definition:** The core text-processing toolkit on any Unix-like system: `grep` selects matching lines, `find` locates files by metadata, `sed` edits lines in place, and `awk` computes over columns and rows.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because they are how real work gets done on a server, and because "how would you find every place this string is used" is a question that separates people who have actually maintained a codebase from people who only use the GUI. It is also the fastest way to look competent in a pairing session — an interviewer watching you reach for `grep -rn` instead of scrolling files will read that as real experience.
**Prerequisites:** 2.2.1, 2.2.3.

**Key concepts**
- **`grep` searches *content*; `find` searches the *filesystem*.** Confusing the two wastes most beginners' time.
```bash
grep -rn "getUserById" src/          # recursive, line numbers, in a directory
grep -rn --include='*.ts' "TODO" .   # restrict to a file type
grep -v "skipped" access.log          # invert: everything that does NOT match
grep -c "ERROR" app.log               # count matching lines, don't print them
grep -oE "(GET|POST) \S+" access.log  # print only the matched part (-o) with regex (-E)
```
- **`-E` is POSIX extended regex; `-P` is PCRE,** so lookarounds and named groups need `-P` and are not portable to BSD/macOS `grep`.
- **`find` filters on metadata, not text:**
```bash
find . -name "*.log" -mtime +7          # modified more than 7 days ago
find . -type f -size +100M              # over 100 MB
find . -name node_modules -prune -o -name '*.js' -print   # skip a huge dir, the important trick
find . -type f -exec grep -l "secret" {} +               # acts on each file
```
- **`-prune` is the difference between a fast command and a hang,** because without it `find` descends into `node_modules` and prints a million paths.
- **`sed` is a stream editor.** The four commands are `s` substitute, `p` print, `d` delete, `i`/`a` insert/append. Remember `-i` for in-place editing, and that on macOS it needs `sed -i ''` because BSD `sed` treats the next argument as the backup suffix.
```bash
sed 's/foo/bar/g' file                 # substitute all on each line
sed -i 's/foo/bar/g' file              # in place (GNU)
sed -i '' 's/foo/bar/g' file           # in place (macOS/BSD)
sed -n '10,20p' file                   # print lines 10-20 only
sed '/^#/d; /^$/d' config              # delete comment and blank lines
```
- **`awk` is a field processor,** which makes it the right tool for counting, summing and grouping columns — the thing `grep` and `sed` cannot do.
```bash
awk '{print $1}' access.log | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head   # top 10 IPs
awk -F, 'NR>1 {sum += $3} END {print sum}' sales.csv             # total column 3, skipping header
awk -F: '$3 < 1000 {print $1}' /etc/passwd                        # rows where field 3 < 1000
awk '{a[$9]++} END {for (k in a) print a[k], k}' access.log       # frequency of status codes
```
- **`-F` sets the separator** (default is runs of whitespace, which is why it usually Just Works on log files), and `NR`/`$n`/`END` are the three things worth memorising.
- **`jq` is the JSON equivalent of awk, and you should reach for it instead of parsing JSON with grep,** which breaks the moment the JSON is nested or spans lines.
- **Quote or use `xargs -0`:** filenames with spaces break naive `find | xargs`, and `find -print0 | xargs -0` is the fix.
- **Pipes compose these,** which is the actual point: each tool does one thing, and the answer is a pipeline rather than a script.

**Questions**
1. Difference between `grep` and `find`?
2. How do you search a codebase for a string, recursively, showing line numbers?
3. What does `-prune` do, and why do you need it?
4. Substitute text in a file in place. How does it differ on macOS?
5. Count the 10 most frequent IPs in an access log.
6. Sum a numeric column in a CSV.
7. Why is `grep` the wrong tool for JSON, and what do you use instead?
8. What breaks when you pipe `find` into `xargs`?

**Model answer, Q1:** "`grep` searches inside file contents and returns matching lines; `find` walks the directory tree and matches files by name, type, size or modification time. They're complementary: I use `find` to locate candidate files and `grep` to search them, and I `find -print0 | xargs -0 grep` when I need both."
**Model answer, Q3:** "`-prune` stops `find` from descending into a matched directory. Without it, `find . -name '*.js'` walks `node_modules` and can take minutes and print a million paths. The idiom is `find . -name node_modules -prune -o -name '*.js' -print` — prune the huge directory, and only print files that match the second condition."
**Model answer, Q5:** "`awk '{print $1}' access.log` pulls the first whitespace-separated field, which is the client IP in the combined log format. Then `sort` groups identical lines, `uniq -c` counts them, `sort -rn` orders by count, and `head` takes ten. If the log is JSON I would use `jq -r '.ip' | sort | uniq -c | sort -rn | head` instead."
**Model answer, Q8:** "Filenames containing spaces or newlines get word-split by the shell when passed to `xargs`, so `find` hands `xargs` more arguments than you intended and the commands fail or act on the wrong files. `find . -print0 | xargs -0` uses a null separator instead of whitespace, so filenames survive intact. Same reason `grep -r | xargs` misbehaves on weird paths."

**What they evaluate**
- **Choosing `grep` vs `find` correctly** without hesitating — this is the baseline expectation.
- **Knowing `-prune` and `-print0`/`-0`,** which separates people who have run these tools on real repositories.
- **Composing a pipeline rather than reaching for a loop,** and preferring `jq` for structured data.
**Probe:** "There's a string `user_id` in 40 files. Show me every call site." (Expect `grep -rn` first, and then a question about whether it's a rename, which changes the answer.)
**Common wrong answers:** using `grep` to find files by name; `sed -i` given without the macOS caveat; parsing JSON with `grep`; reaching for a `for` loop instead of a pipeline; forgetting that `find` descends into `node_modules` without `-prune`.
**Trade-offs:**
- **`sed -i` vs an editor:** in-place is fast and scriptable but has no undo and no preview; opening the file is safe but not automatable. Use `sed` without `-i` first to see the diff, then commit with `-i`.
- **`awk` vs a real tool:** `awk` is always available and streams in constant memory, but every non-trivial program becomes a language nobody else can read; `jq`, Node, or `csvkit` is more maintainable and worth the dependency once the transformation grows past a couple of lines.
- **`grep -r` vs an indexed search:** ripgrep respects `.gitignore` and is orders of magnitude faster, but it may not be installed on the server; if the interviewer is on a machine without it, plain `grep` is the portable answer.
**Mistakes:** unquoted regex metacharacters causing unexpected matches or errors; running `sed -i` without checking first and mangling a file; `-name '*.js'` matching directories; assuming log field positions; `grep`ping a minified bundle and drowning in noise; using `sudo` out of habit rather than fixing file ownership.
**Mini task:** Find every file over 1 MB in a repo excluding `node_modules`, then count how many contain a specific string, without writing a loop. Then break the command on purpose by unquoting a variable and explain the error.
**Resources:** `man grep`, `man find`, `man sed`, `man awk` (skim, don't read); the GNU grep manual's "Matching Characters" chapter; the "Breach" (OverTheWire) and Bandit wargames for practice under time pressure.
**Self-check:** Can you write the top-10-IPs pipeline from memory, explain what `-prune` prevents, and name the flag that makes pipelines safe for filenames with spaces?

## Interview Questions (2.2)
**Questions**
1. How do you find which process is using port 3000 and kill it? *(`lsof -i :3000` → `kill <pid>`; try SIGTERM before SIGKILL.)*
2. `chmod 755` vs `644`? Why do SSH keys need `600`?
3. `>` vs `>>`? What does `2>&1` do?
4. `kill` vs `kill -9`? Why prefer the former?
5. What's the difference between a process and a thread? *(see 2.6)*
6. How do you see the last 100 lines of a log and follow new ones, filtered for errors? *(`tail -n 100 -f app.log | grep -i error`)*
7. How would you find all files over 100MB modified in the last week?
8. What are environment variables and why use them over hardcoding?
9. What does `set -euo pipefail` do?
10. How do you run a command that survives closing your SSH session? *(`nohup`, `tmux`/`screen`, or better, `systemd`/a process manager.)*
11. A server's disk is full. How do you investigate? *(`df -h`, `du -sh /* | sort -h`, check logs, Docker images, `lsof | grep deleted`.)*
12. What's the difference between a hard link and a symlink?
13. What do `&&`, `||`, `;`, and `|` do?
14. How do you check a process's CPU/memory use? *(`top`/`htop`, `ps aux --sort=-%mem`.)*
15. What is `sudo` and why is running everything as root dangerous?

**Mistakes:** `sudo` as a reflex; storing secrets in shell history (`export SECRET=...` lands in history, so use a prompt or secrets manager); unquoted variables; running destructive commands without checking the path; no `-p` or confirmation habit.

**Mini tasks**
1. Take a sample access log (generate with a script or download one). Produce: total requests, top 5 IPs, count per status code, and the slowest endpoints.
2. Write a bash script that backs up a directory to a timestamped `.tar.gz`, keeps only the last 5 backups, and exits non-zero on failure.
3. Start two servers on the same port, read the error, then find and kill the first one.

**Resources:** *The Linux Command Line* (William Shotts, free), `man` pages, the "Missing Semester of Your CS Education" (MIT, free), explainshell.com, ShellCheck for linting scripts.

**Self-check:** Can you do the log-analysis one-liner and the port-kill sequence without looking anything up?

---

# 2.3 GIT AND GITHUB

**Prerequisites:** terminal basics. 🟢 → 🔴 · **Must-know**

**Why interviewers ask:** Every team uses Git. Candidates who only know `add/commit/push` struggle with real workflows: conflicts, rewriting history, recovering lost work. Questions here reveal collaboration maturity.

## 2.3.1 The Mental Model
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Git as a content-addressed object store with three places your work can be — the working directory, the staging area, and the repository — and a set of pointers that record which snapshot is "current".
**Why interviewers ask:** Because everything else in this group is a consequence of this model. A candidate who knows that `rebase` creates *new* commit objects doesn't need to memorise that it's dangerous on shared branches — the danger follows. This is also the question that separates people who copy Git recipes from people who can reason about a failure.
**Prerequisites:** 2.2.1.

**Key concepts**
```
Working directory ──git add──▶ Staging area (index) ──git commit──▶ Local repo ──git push──▶ Remote
        ◀──────────────────git checkout/restore──────────────────       ◀──git fetch/pull──
```
- **Git stores snapshots, not diffs.** Everything is content-addressed objects: **blob** (file contents), **tree** (directory), **commit** (snapshot + parent(s) + metadata), **tag**. Identified by SHA hashes.
- **A branch is just a movable pointer to a commit.** `HEAD` points to the current branch (or directly to a commit in "detached HEAD" state).
- **Commits are immutable.** "Amending" or "rebasing" creates *new* commits and moves the pointer. The old ones still exist until garbage-collected, which is why the reflog can save you.
- **Remote** = another copy of the repo (`origin`). **Remote-tracking branches** (`origin/main`) are your last-known view of it.
- **The staging area is a deliberate feature, not a quirk.** It lets you commit part of your work, which is why `git add -p` exists. Being able to say that turns "why do I have to `git add` again" from confusion into intent.
- **A commit is a tree plus parents plus author and message.** The tree references blobs by hash, so unchanged files across commits are stored once. That's why Git is fast and why "snapshots, not diffs" is literally true.
- **The hash is derived from the content**, including the parent hashes. Change anything — content, message, parent — and the ID changes. That is what makes rewriting history *rewrite*: every descendant gets a new ID too.
- **Almost nothing is truly deleted.** A removed branch still exists as an object until garbage collection, and your reflog records where `HEAD` has been. "Lost" commits are unreferenced, not gone.

**Questions**
1. What are the three areas, and what moves data between them?
2. Does Git store diffs or snapshots? What's the practical difference?
3. What is a branch, really?
4. What is `HEAD`, and what is a detached HEAD?
5. Why is rewriting history a bigger deal than it looks?
6. Why is there a staging area at all?
7. Explain `origin`, `origin/main`, `upstream` and `main`.
8. If you `git checkout` an old commit, have you lost anything?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Snapshots. Each commit points to a tree, and the tree references blobs by their SHA, so if a file didn't change between two commits, both commits point at the same blob — it's stored once. That makes Git fast, makes history comparable no matter which branch you're on, and it's why the hash covers the content. The practical difference from diffs is that nothing is ever 'out of date': a commit is a complete picture, so checking out an old one really does restore that state."
**Model answer, Q3:** "A branch is a file containing the SHA of a commit. Creating one writes 41 bytes; switching to one changes what `HEAD` points at; merging one is just creating a commit with two parents. That's why branches are cheap and why deleting one doesn't delete the commits immediately — they stay reachable from the reflog until garbage collection."
**Model answer, Q5:** "Because a commit's hash includes its content, its message, and its parents' hashes. If you change any commit, its ID changes, and so does the ID of every commit after it. So rewriting history doesn't just alter one commit — it renumbers everything downstream, which means anyone who already fetched those commits now has two different versions of the same commit. Their `main` and your `main` look related but aren't."
**What they evaluate**
- **Whether the model is structural,** not a list of commands. Can they derive behaviour from it?
- **Knowing branches are pointers,** which is the basis for every later question.
- **Understanding that history rewriting renumbers descendants.**
**Probe:** "Why is `git checkout <old-sha>` safe?" Or: "If a branch is just a file, what actually happens when you delete it?"
**Common wrong answers:** "Git stores diffs to save space"; a branch as a copy of the whole repo; `HEAD` as the current commit's contents; thinking a deleted branch deletes its commits immediately; treating the staging area as a bug.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Staging area vs staging nothing:** it costs an extra step but lets you commit a coherent unit out of mixed work. Skipping it means every commit is "whatever I happened to have".
- **Content-addressed storage vs deltas:** whole snapshots make history self-contained and diffing cheap; a delta chain makes history undecodable if any link is lost.
- **Loose refs vs packed refs:** thousands of loose branch files slow things down; `git gc` packs them. Purely an implementation trade, but it's why old repos feel slower.
**Mistakes:** thinking `git add` "uploads"; believing `git checkout` of a commit deletes the newer ones (it doesn't); confusing the remote with `origin/main`; assuming a deleted branch's commits are gone immediately.
**Mini task:** In a scratch repo, run `git cat-file -p HEAD` and `git cat-file -t <sha>` to see a commit decomposed into tree, parents and message. Then delete a branch and recover its commit from the reflog.
**Resources:** *Pro Git* chapters 1-2 (free at git-scm.com/book); the Git internals page on the official site; `git help gitrepository-layout`.
**Self-check:** Can you explain, in one sentence each, why rewriting history renumbers commits, why branches are cheap, and why deleted commits aren't gone?

## 2.3.2 Everyday Commands
🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The commands you use dozens of times a day — inspecting state, staging precisely, branching, and syncing with a remote.
**Why interviewers ask:** Not for recall of flags, but because *how* someone inspects before acting reveals their habits. Someone who reaches for `git status` and `git diff` before changing anything is describing a debugging discipline; someone who reaches for `git reset --hard` first is telling you something else.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.1.

**Key concepts**
```bash
git init / git clone <url>
git status
git add -p                       # stage interactively, hunk by hunk (great habit)
git commit -m "feat: add login"
git log --oneline --graph --decorate --all
git diff                         # unstaged changes
git diff --staged                # staged changes
git switch -c feature/x          # create and switch branch (older: git checkout -b)
git switch main
git fetch                        # download remote changes, don't merge
git pull                         # fetch + merge (or rebase if configured)
git push -u origin feature/x
git restore file                 # discard working changes (older: checkout -- file)
git restore --staged file        # unstage
git commit --amend               # modify last commit (only if not pushed/shared!)
```
- **The inspect-before-you-act trio.** `git status` for state, `git diff` for what changed, `git log` for how you got here. Almost every Git mistake is avoided by running those three first.
- **`git add -p` is the highest-value habit in this list.** It lets you stage one hunk at a time so a commit contains one idea. It also means a stray `console.log` never ends up in a commit called "fix login".
- **`switch`/`restore` replaced `checkout` for files and branches** because `git checkout <branch>` and `git checkout <file>` were two different operations wearing one name. The old spellings still work; the new ones are clearer and are what you should write.
- **`fetch` is safe, `pull` is not.** `fetch` only downloads and updates `origin/main`. `pull` fetches *and* integrates, which can create a merge or trigger a rebase in the middle of your work.
- **`git commit --amend` is only safe before you push.** After the commit exists on the remote, amending creates a new commit with the same parent, and you have two divergent versions of the same position.
- **`git restore file` discards work with no undo.** It is the command to be most careful with, because unlike a reset it doesn't even leave the changes in a recoverable object.

**Questions**
1. `git fetch` vs `git pull`? Which is safer, and why?
2. What's the difference between `git diff` and `git diff --staged`?
3. How do you stage part of a file?
4. `switch` vs `checkout`?
5. How do you undo `git add`?
6. When is `git commit --amend` safe?
7. How do you rename a branch and delete one?
8. How do you see what changed on the remote since you last fetched?
9. What's `git push -u` for?

**Model answer, Q1:** "`fetch` downloads the remote's commits and updates your remote-tracking refs like `origin/main`, and stops there. `pull` is fetch plus integrate — it merges or rebases into your current branch. So fetch is strictly safer: it lets you look before you leap, and `git log HEAD..origin/main` tells you exactly what's waiting. I use fetch as the default and pull when I actually intend to integrate."
**Model answer, Q5:** "`git restore --staged <file>` puts it back in the working tree unstaged. Older Git uses `git reset HEAD <file>`, which does the same thing. If I want to keep the changes but stop tracking the file entirely, that's `git rm --cached` plus an entry in `.gitignore`."
**Model answer, Q8:** "`git fetch` then `git log --oneline HEAD..origin/main` for what's new on the remote, or `git diff HEAD...origin/main` for the actual content. The `HEAD..origin/main` form is the one that matters: it means 'commits reachable from origin/main but not from my HEAD' — commits I don't have yet."
**What they evaluate**
- **Whether they inspect state before mutating it.**
- **Knowing that `fetch` and `pull` are different verbs,** and can explain the safety difference.
- **Precision about staging,** which shows they care about readable commits.
**Probe:** "Why is `git commit --amend` dangerous after a push?" Or: "You want to see what a teammate pushed before you pull. What do you run?"
**Common wrong answers:** `pull` and `fetch` as synonyms; `git commit -a` as equivalent to `add` (it misses untracked files); `git restore` as a safe undo; using `git reset --hard` to unstage.
**Trade-offs:**
- **`add -A` vs `add -p`:** `-A` is fast for a clean sweep; `-p` is slower but produces commits that are one idea each. Most teams end up wanting `-p` on anything non-trivial.
- **`pull` with merge vs rebase:** merge preserves the truth and adds a merge commit; rebase keeps history linear but rewrites your local commits. Configure it explicitly rather than inheriting a default.
- **Aliases:** `git config --global alias.st status` saves keystrokes and makes your habits visible to your future self.
**Mistakes:** `git add .` staging secrets and build output; `git pull` mid-conflict and losing track of what's happening; `git commit -a` thinking it catches new files; `git restore` throwing away an hour of work.
**Mini task:** Create a repo and go through the whole loop deliberately: change two files, stage one hunk with `add -p`, commit, then inspect with `status`, `diff`, and `diff --staged`. Repeat until you never need to look anything up.
**Resources:** `git help` for any command (`git help pull` opens the manual); *Pro Git* chapters 2-3; Oh Shit Git for the panics.
**Self-check:** Can you name the three commands you'd run first when something has gone wrong, and what each one tells you?

## 2.3.3 Merge vs Rebase
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Two ways to move commits from one branch onto another. Merge records that two histories converged; rebase replays your commits on top of a different base as new commits.
**Why interviewers ask:** It is the most-asked Git question with a real trap in it, because the naive answer — "rebase for clean history" — is wrong in a way that has cost real teams real work. The interviewer wants to hear where you draw the line, and the golden rule is what they're listening for.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.1.

**Key concepts**
```
Before:       A──B──C  (main)
                 \
                  D──E  (feature)

Merge:        A──B──C─────M   (main)       M = merge commit with two parents
                 \       /
                  D──E──┘

Rebase:       A──B──C──D'──E'  (feature)   D', E' = new commits with new hashes
```

| | Merge | Rebase |
|---|---|---|
| History | Preserves true history, adds merge commit | Linear, cleaner |
| Rewrites commits? | No | Yes (new hashes) |
| Safe on shared branches? | Yes | **No**, never rebase commits others have pulled |
| Conflicts | Resolve once | May resolve per replayed commit |
| Use for | Integrating a finished branch; shared branches | Updating your *local* feature branch with main; tidying commits before a PR |

**Golden rule:** don't rebase public/shared history.

**PR merge strategies on GitHub**
- **Merge commit:** keeps all commits plus a merge commit.
- **Squash and merge:** collapses the PR into one commit on main (clean history, loses granularity).
- **Rebase and merge:** replays commits linearly onto main (no merge commit).

**Interactive rebase** to clean up before a PR:
```bash
git rebase -i HEAD~4    # squash, reword, reorder, drop commits
```

- **A merge commit has two parents, which is the whole point.** It *records* that the two lines of history diverged and rejoined. That's information, not noise.
- **Rebase copies, it doesn't move.** Your original commits still exist in your reflog; you get new ones with new hashes on the new base. This is why `git pull --rebase` repeatedly is safe locally and disastrous on `main`.
- **The danger is precise:** if someone has already fetched your commits and you rewrite them, their copy and yours are now different commits with the same content. Their next `git pull` doesn't fast-forward — it either conflicts or duplicates the work.
- **Squash merges sidestep the whole problem.** That's one reason teams adopt squash-and-merge: it makes the base branch's history linear and makes rewriting your own PR branch uninteresting.
- **Conflicts during rebase are resolved per commit,** so a conflict that was one decision during a merge can become four separate interruptions. Frequent rebases are cheap; one rebase of fifty commits is not.
- **`--force-with-lease` exists because of this.** It refuses to push if the remote moved since you last fetched, turning "I just deleted your work" into a clean failure.

**Questions**
1. Merge vs rebase — when do you use each?
2. Why is rebasing `main` dangerous?
3. What is a merge commit, structurally?
4. Difference between squash-merge, rebase-merge and merge-commit on a PR?
5. Why does rebasing produce new commit hashes?
6. Your branch is 30 commits behind `main`. What do you do?
7. What is `--force-with-lease` and why is it safer than `--force`?
8. When is an interactive rebase worth the trouble?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Both integrate changes; they differ in what they do to history. Merge creates a commit with two parents, so it records the true shape of the work and never rewrites anything — that's why it's safe on shared branches. Rebase replays my commits on top of another branch as new commits, giving a linear history, which is easier to read but rewrites. So: I rebase my own feature branch onto `main` to stay current and to tidy commits before opening a PR, and I use a merge or squash merge to land it. The one rule I never break is rebasing a branch other people have already fetched."
**Model answer, Q2:** "Because a commit's hash covers its content, its message and its parents. If I replay commits onto a different base, every one of them gets a new hash — and so does everything after it. Anyone who already fetched my old commits now has objects that look like mine but aren't, and their next pull doesn't fast-forward; it duplicates the work or conflicts. With `main` that hits everyone on the team, which is why branch protection blocks force pushes there."
**What they evaluate**
- **Where they draw the line,** not which they prefer. "Rebase always" is as wrong as "never rebase".
- **Understanding the mechanism** behind new hashes, so the danger is derived rather than memorised.
- **Awareness of squash merging** as the pragmatic answer many teams land on.
**Probe:** "You rebased your feature branch and now the PR shows duplicate commits. What happened and how do you fix it?" Or: "Your team wants linear history on `main`. What do you configure?"
**Common wrong answers:** "Rebase is always cleaner, so always rebase"; rebase described as moving commits rather than copying them; force push without a lease; rebasing a long-lived shared release branch; confusing squash-merge with rebase-merge.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Merge vs rebase on `main`:** merge keeps the record of how work actually converged, at the cost of clutter; linear history reads better but needs force pushes to maintain.
- **Squash vs no squash on PRs:** squash gives one clean commit per change and trivial reverts, but loses the granularity that helps `git bisect` and code review of intermediate steps.
- **Merge often vs rebase often:** small frequent rebases conflict once and cheaply; one big rebase at the end of a long branch surfaces every conflict you've been avoiding.
**Mistakes:** `git pull --rebase` on a shared branch; `git push --force` without a lease after a rebase; rebasing a branch that has an open PR others have pulled; resolving rebase conflicts by taking "ours" wholesale and losing work.
**Mini task:** Build two branches that both change the same three lines. Resolve with a merge, then redo it with a rebase, and diff the two history graphs. Then rebase one and force push with and without `--force-with-lease` against a colleague's new commit.
**Resources:** learngitbranching.js.org (interactive, the fastest way to build intuition); *Pro Git* chapter 7; the GitHub docs on pull request merge strategies.
**Self-check:** Can you state the golden rule, explain in one sentence why rebasing changes hashes, and say which merge strategy your last team used and why?

## 2.3.4 Conflict Resolution
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** What it means when two changes touch the same lines, how Git marks it, and how you decide what the merged result should be rather than just clearing the markers.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because "delete the conflict markers" is what people say when they've only done it once. The real skill is recognising that Git stops at the *line* level while the actual disagreement is usually at the *intent* level, and that the resolution is a decision you own.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.3.

**Symptom**
```
<<<<<<< HEAD
your version
=======
their version
>>>>>>> feature/x
```
Steps:
1. `git status` to see conflicted files.
2. Open each file, decide the right result (often a combination), and remove the markers.
3. `git add <file>` then `git merge --continue` (or `git rebase --continue`).
4. **Run tests.** A conflict-free merge can still be a *semantic* conflict.
- Abort anytime with `git merge --abort` / `git rebase --abort`.
- Reduce conflicts: small PRs, frequent syncing with main, avoid mass reformatting, and clear file ownership.
- Tools: VS Code merge editor, `git mergetool`. Enable `rerere` to reuse recorded resolutions.

**Key concepts**
- **Git conflicts at the line, humans conflict at the intent.** Two branches that both renamed a variable or changed the same function's behaviour can merge cleanly and still be wrong. That is the failure mode this topic exists to prevent.
- **The two sides are labelled `HEAD` (yours, the current branch) and the other ref.** During a rebase "yours" and "theirs" mean something different from during a merge, which is a genuinely reliable source of confusion.
- **Most conflicts have three outcomes:** keep one side, keep both (usually as a union for imports or lists), or write something new. Taking one side wholesale is the default and it's usually wrong.
- **`merge.conflictstyle` changes the ergonomics,** not the semantics — `zdiff3` adds a third section showing the common ancestor, which turns "pick a side" into "see what both sides changed from".
- **`rerere` remembers your resolutions** and replays them, so the same conflict in the same place stops costing you time. On a long-lived branch that's the difference between merging weekly and merging quarterly.
- **Structure conflicts can be unresolvable by hand.** Two branches each add an import at the top of the same file, or one reformats while the other edits every line. These need a human to restructure, and they're an argument for smaller PRs and separate formatting commits.
- **Tests are the second line of defence.** A merge that passes CI has still not been verified semantically; running the suite locally before you push is what catches the case where both changes were individually correct and jointly wrong.

**Questions**
1. Walk me through resolving a merge conflict.
2. What do the `<<<<<<<`, `=======` and `>>>>>>>` markers mean?
3. What if you pick the wrong resolution?
4. How do you resolve a conflict during a rebase?
5. Why can a conflict-free merge still be a bug?
6. What is `rerere` and when does it help?
7. How do you avoid conflicts in the first place?
8. You've been in a rebase for an hour and it's a mess. What now?

**Model answer, Q1:** "`git status` first, to see exactly which files are unmerged. Then open each one and read the surrounding code, not just the marked lines — the right answer is often a combination of both sides. I delete the markers, `git add` the file, and then `git merge --continue` or `git rebase --continue`. And then I run the tests before pushing, because a merge that resolves cleanly can still be semantically wrong."
**Model answer, Q5:** "Because Git only detects overlapping *changes to the same lines*. Two branches can each correctly change different parts of the same function — one renames a parameter, the other changes the default — and the merge will be textually clean while the code no longer works. That's why 'it merged' is not the same as 'it works', and why I run the tests."
**Model answer, Q8:** "`git rebase --abort` to get back to exactly where I started, then change the approach — sync with `main` in smaller steps, or resolve the mess on a fresh branch. The rule is never to keep pushing through a rebase you've stopped understanding, because the next conflict is applied on top of a resolution you no longer trust."
**What they evaluate**
- **Reading code, not just markers.** The probe is whether they talk about intent.
- **Knowing the rebase labels differ** from merge labels.
- **Prevention instinct** — small PRs, frequent syncs, formatting in its own commit.
**Probe:** "Both sides added a different import to the top of the same file. What do you do?" Or: "What does 'ours' mean during a rebase?"
**Common wrong answers:** choosing "accept all" in an editor; deleting markers and committing without adding the file; `git rebase --skip` to make a conflict go away; treating a clean merge as proof of correctness.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Manual vs `rerere` vs merge driver:** a merge driver can automate structured cases but needs a custom driver to do it well; `rerere` learns from your manual choices without extra configuration.
- **`zdiff3` vs default markers:** three-way output costs more screen space and removes a whole class of mistakes.
- **Resolving now vs rebasing onto a fresh base:** rebasing from scratch is more work up front but avoids stacking resolutions on top of ones you rushed.
**Mistakes:** `git checkout --theirs file` wholesale; committing with markers still in the file; resolving during a rebase by taking the whole file from one side; skipping tests; not using `rerere` on a long branch.
**Mini task:** Create two branches that both edit the same function in a way that is individually correct and jointly wrong. Merge them, watch it succeed, and have the tests catch it. Then merge the same change with `zdiff3` and see the difference.
**Resources:** `git help merge`, `git help rerere`; the `zdiff3` article by Aaron Boharem; VS Code's 3-way merge editor docs.
**Self-check:** Can you explain why a clean merge can still break, and name three things you do to reduce conflicts rather than resolve them?

## 2.3.5 Stash, Cherry-pick, Reset vs Revert, Reflog
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The recovery toolkit — parking work in progress, copying one commit, moving a branch pointer, adding a new commit that undoes an old one, and finding commits that look lost.
**Why interviewers ask:** "I committed a secret" and "I ran `reset --hard` and lost a day" both end careers in a bad way, and this group is the answer to both. The interviewer is testing whether you can undo things *safely*, and the reset-versus-revert distinction is the specific line they're probing.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.1, 2.3.3.

**Key concepts**
```bash
# STASH: shelve uncommitted work
git stash push -m "wip login"      # (-u includes untracked files)
git stash list
git stash pop                      # apply and drop; `apply` keeps it

# CHERRY-PICK: copy a specific commit onto the current branch
git cherry-pick <sha>              # use for hotfixes backported to a release branch

# RESET: move the branch pointer (rewrites history locally)
git reset --soft HEAD~1            # undo commit; changes stay STAGED
git reset --mixed HEAD~1           # (default) undo commit; changes UNSTAGED
git reset --hard HEAD~1            # undo commit; DISCARD changes ⚠️

# REVERT: create a NEW commit that undoes a previous one (safe for shared history)
git revert <sha>

# REFLOG: local log of where HEAD has been, so you can recover "lost" commits
git reflog
git reset --hard HEAD@{3}          # go back to a prior state
git branch rescue <sha>            # safest: make a branch at the lost commit
```

| Situation | Use |
|---|---|
| Undo a commit you haven't pushed | `reset` |
| Undo a commit already pushed/shared | `revert` |
| Need a single commit from another branch | `cherry-pick` |
| Switch tasks with dirty working tree | `stash` |
| "I destroyed my work!" | `reflog` |
| Find which commit introduced a bug | `git bisect` |

**`git bisect`:**
```bash
git bisect start
git bisect bad                 # current commit is broken
git bisect good v1.2.0         # this one worked
# git checks out midpoints; mark each good/bad (or `git bisect run npm test`)
git bisect reset
```
Binary search over history gives O(log n) steps.

**Force pushing:** after rewriting local history you must force push. Use `git push --force-with-lease`, which refuses if someone else pushed in the meantime. Never force push to `main`/shared branches (protect them in GitHub settings).

- **Reset moves a pointer; revert adds a commit.** That single sentence answers most questions about this group. Reset says "this never happened", which is only true if nobody else has seen it. Revert says "this happened and here's the correction", which is always honest.
- **`--soft`, `--mixed`, `--hard` differ only in what happens to the index and working tree.** Soft leaves everything staged, mixed unstages it, hard throws it away. Choosing among them is really choosing how much work you're willing to lose.
- **The reflog is local and short-lived.** It records where `HEAD` has been, and it only exists on the machine where the commits were made. That's why "force pushed and now it's gone" is sometimes unrecoverable and sometimes a five-minute fix.
- **`git branch rescue <sha>` before anything destructive.** Once you've found the commit in the reflog, giving it a name makes it reachable again and immune to garbage collection. Do that first, then figure out what you actually want.
- **Cherry-pick copies, it doesn't move.** The original commit still exists on its own branch, and the copy is a new commit with a new hash. If you cherry-pick the same change twice, you get it twice.
- **`git bisect run npm test` turns a manual search into a command.** With a reliable test that reproduces the bug, bisect finds the offending commit in log₂(n) steps — about 6 steps for 64 commits.

**Questions**
1. `reset` vs `revert`? When do you use each?
2. `reset --soft` vs `--mixed` vs `--hard`?
3. What does the reflog do, and what can't it do?
4. I ran `reset --hard` and lost a day of work. Walk me through recovery.
5. What does `git stash` include by default, and what does it not?
6. When is cherry-picking the right tool?
7. How do I find which commit introduced a bug?
8. I committed a secret. What now?

**Model answer, Q1:** "`reset` moves the branch pointer, which means the commits are no longer on your branch — that's rewriting history, so it's only safe if nobody else has fetched them. `revert` doesn't move anything; it creates a new commit whose effect undoes an old one, which is the only honest option on shared history. So my rule is: reset for anything local that hasn't been pushed, revert for anything already pushed. If I'm not sure, `git log` tells me whether the commit is on the remote."
**Model answer, Q4:** "First I'd stop panicking, because nothing is actually deleted. `git reflog` shows every position `HEAD` has been at, including the commit before the reset. I'd find the SHA I want, and immediately `git branch rescue <sha>` so it can't be garbage collected — that's the safety step people skip. Then I can `git reset --hard` back to it, or `git cherry-pick` the specific work. The recovery only fails if the reflog has expired and nothing else references the commits, which is why I do the `branch rescue` step immediately rather than exploring further."
**Model answer, Q8:** "Rotate the secret first. That's the only step that actually fixes the problem — the moment it was pushed it's compromised, regardless of what I do to the history. Then I tell the team, because they may have clones too. Then I remove it from history with `git filter-repo` or BFG, force push, and have everyone re-clone. And I add it to `.gitignore` and to secret scanning so it doesn't happen again. Cleaning history without rotating is the wrong order and the most common mistake here."
**What they evaluate**
- **The reset/revert line, stated as a principle** about shared vs local history, not as a rule to recite.
- **Knowing that the reflog is local,** which is the honest limit of "it's recoverable".
- **Rotating the secret before cleaning history.** The order is the whole answer.
**Probe:** "You're on a team of five. Can you use `reset` to undo yesterday's commit?" Or: "Why is `git stash pop` riskier than `apply`?"
**Common wrong answers:** `revert` described as deleting a commit; `reset --hard` as a safe undo; expecting `reflog` to recover commits force-pushed by someone else; cleaning history before rotating a secret; `stash` claimed to include untracked files by default.
**Trade-offs:**
- **`reset` vs `revert`:** reset gives clean history and a clean diff; revert gives an auditable record that also works on shared branches. Squashing on a feature branch then reverting on `main` is the common hybrid.
- **`stash pop` vs `apply`:** pop is convenient and destroys the entry if it applies cleanly; apply keeps it, at the cost of a stash list that grows and a habit of forgetting to clean it.
- **Cherry-pick vs merge:** cherry-pick gives a focused, readable change; merge records that the branches really did converge. For a hotfix backport, cherry-pick is what you want.
**Mistakes:** `reset --hard` on a branch others have; cherry-picking a commit that depends on changes not in the target branch; `stash` without `-u` and then "losing" untracked files; not creating a rescue branch before exploring; force pushing without a lease.
