**Mini task:** Do all five of these on a scratch repo and record what each one did to `git log --oneline --graph`: a soft reset, a hard reset then a reflog rescue, a revert of a pushed-looking commit, a stash round trip with and without `-u`, and a `git bisect run` over 30 scripted commits.
**Resources:** *Pro Git* chapters 6 and 10; Oh Shit, Git for the panic cases; `git help reflog`; the GitHub docs on removing sensitive data.
**Self-check:** Can you state the reset/revert rule in one sentence, walk the reflog recovery, and say why rotation comes before history cleaning?

## 2.3.6 Branching Strategies
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** The conventions a team uses for how branches are created, named, merged and deleted — and therefore how much integration pain it accepts in exchange for what.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because the right answer is never "Gitflow is best". The interviewer is checking whether you can match a branching model to a release cadence and a team size, and whether you know what each one costs.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.3, 2.3.7.

**Key concepts**
| Strategy | How it works | Fits |
|---|---|---|
| **Trunk-based** | Everyone commits to `main` (or very short-lived branches, under 1-2 days) behind feature flags; CI is strict | Fast-moving teams with good CI and CD |
| **GitHub Flow** | `main` is always deployable; feature branch → PR → review → merge → deploy | Most web teams |
| **Gitflow** | `main`, `develop`, `feature/*`, `release/*`, `hotfix/*` | Versioned/scheduled releases (mobile, on-prem); heavy for continuous deployment |

**Branch naming:** `feature/login-form`, `fix/cart-total-rounding`, `chore/upgrade-deps`.

- **The choice is really about release cadence and blast radius.** Continuous deployment pushes toward trunk-based; a shipped product with quarterly releases and multiple supported versions pushes toward something with release branches.
- **Branch lifetime predicts conflict pain.** The longer a branch lives, the more it diverges and the more expensive the final integration is. Short-lived branches are the single most effective conflict-avoidance technique, and they cost nothing.
- **Feature flags change the calculus.** With flags, unfinished work can live on `main` without being visible, which lets you merge small and deploy continuously — that's what makes trunk-based viable.
- **Gitflow's cost is ceremony.** Five branch types means remembering which one a hotfix targets, plus merge ceremony on every change. It's a real cost paid daily to solve a release problem that only matters occasionally.
- **Branch protection matters more than the model.** Requiring review, requiring passing checks, and blocking force pushes to `main` gives you most of the safety regardless of which model you claim to follow.
- **Long-lived release branches need backports to be deliberate.** A hotfix lands on `main` and is cherry-picked forward; a team that forgets this ends up shipping a fix to one version only.

**Questions**
1. Trunk-based vs Gitflow — when do you use which?
2. What problem is Gitflow actually solving?
3. Why are short-lived branches better?
4. How do feature flags change your branching options?
5. What is the main benefit of GitHub Flow over trunk-based?
6. How do you ship a hotfix to two supported versions?
7. What should branch protection require, regardless of strategy?
8. How do you name branches, and does it matter?

**Model answer, Q1:** "It comes down to release cadence and how much risk a bad deploy carries. If we deploy continuously and can turn features off with flags, trunk-based fits: everyone works on short-lived branches, merges to `main` often, and `main` is always deployable behind flags. Gitflow fits when we ship on a schedule and support multiple released versions at once — it gives you release branches, which is exactly what you need when a customer on v2 needs a fix that also has to reach v3. The cost of Gitflow is ceremony on every change, so I'd only adopt it if the release process genuinely needs it."
**Model answer, Q3:** "Because a branch's cost grows with how long it lives. Every day it's open, `main` moves further away, the diff gets noisier, the review gets harder and the merge conflicts more. Short-lived branches — under a day or two — mean a small, focused diff, a quick review, and a merge that's mostly a formality. It's the cheapest conflict-avoidance technique there is, and it requires no process change at all."
**What they evaluate**
- **Matching the model to the context** rather than quoting a preference.
- **Knowing that branch lifetime drives conflict cost.**
- **Noticing that branch protection is the real safety net.**
**Probe:** "Your team ships weekly mobile builds to the App Store with two versions in the store. Now what?" Or: "How would you introduce a branching model to a team that currently pushes straight to `main`?"
**Common wrong answers:** Gitflow as the professional default; GitHub Flow and trunk-based described as the same thing; branches named `develop`, `feature`, `final`, `new`; arguing for long-lived feature branches; no mention of branch protection at all.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Trunk-based vs Gitflow:** constant integration and small diffs against release flexibility and a heavier ceremony.
- **Long-lived feature branches vs short-lived ones with flags:** isolation and fewer merges against constant integration pain.
- **Naming conventions:** enforced prefixes make automation and dashboards possible; free-form names are faster to type and impossible to filter on.
**Mistakes:** a `develop` branch nobody can explain the purpose of; branches that live for weeks; no protection on `main`; hotfixes that never get backported; merge conflicts treated as bad luck rather than a symptom of branch age.
**Mini task:** Take a real repo and measure how long branches live between creation and merge. Then deliberately shorten it for a week and see whether conflicts and review time drop.
**Resources:** the Trunk Based Development site; Gitflow's original article (still the clearest statement of the trade); GitHub's protected-branches docs.
**Self-check:** Can you name the release-cadence question you'd ask before recommending a branching model, and say which single GitHub setting you'd turn on regardless?

## 2.3.7 Pull Requests and Code Review (GitHub)
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The GitHub workflow of proposing a change on a branch, describing it, having it reviewed, and merging it — plus the social protocol that makes it actually improve things.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because review is where code quality is decided in most teams, and because "what makes a good PR?" is really a question about engineering judgement: scope, communication, and how you treat other people's time.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.6.

**Key concepts**
- **Good PR:** small (ideally under 400 lines), one purpose, descriptive title and body (what, why, how to test, screenshots), linked issue, passing CI.
- **Reviewer etiquette:** specific, kind, explain why, label nits. **Author etiquette:** self-review first, respond to every comment, and don't take feedback personally.
- **Protection rules:** require review, require passing status checks, block force pushes, and require linear history if wanted.
- **Other GitHub features:** Issues, Projects, Releases and tags, CODEOWNERS, Dependabot, Actions, Secrets, Pages.
- **Forking workflow** (open source): fork → clone → branch → PR from fork to upstream.

- **Size is the strongest predictor of review quality.** Under roughly 400 lines, a human can hold the change in their head. Above it, reviewers start approving what they didn't understand, and the review becomes a formality that exists to satisfy the branch protection rule.
- **One PR, one purpose.** A PR that fixes a bug and reformats a file is unreviewable, because the reviewer can't tell which change is intentional. This is the single most common cause of rubber-stamp reviews.
- **The description is part of the review.** "What changed, why, and how do I verify it" is what lets a reviewer catch a design error before reading a single line of the diff.
- **Comments are categorised on purpose.** Prefix a non-blocking suggestion with `nit:` and make clear it's optional. Mixing required changes into the same comments as taste is how reviews become arguments.
- **Reviewing your own diff first** catches the vast majority of what a reviewer would flag, and it signals that the change is finished.
- **CODEOWNERS makes review mandatory where it matters.** Security-sensitive directories — auth, payments, infrastructure — can require a specific team's approval, which is more reliable than hoping reviewers notice.
- **A reviewer's job is the code, not the author.** Personal feedback and a merge decision are two different conversations, and conflating them is what makes people defensive about review.

**Questions**
1. What makes a good pull request?
2. How big should a PR be, and why?
3. What do you put in a PR description?
4. How do you give feedback that lands?
5. What are branch protection rules and which do you enable?
6. Reviewer says "this is wrong". Your response?
7. How do you handle a 2000-line PR that someone hands you?
8. What is CODEOWNERS for?
9. How do you review your own code before requesting review?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Three things. It's small — ideally under 400 lines, because above that reviewers stop really reading. It does one thing, so a fix and a reformat don't get bundled. And the description explains what changed, why, and how to test it, which is what lets a reviewer catch a design problem before reading the diff. Plus CI green and a linked issue so the context is somewhere other than in the author's head."
**Model answer, Q6:** "I'd first try to understand it rather than defend the code. Often the reviewer has context I don't — a constraint from another system, a past incident — and if they're wrong, that context is exactly what I was missing. If I still disagree after that, I'd say so specifically, explain my reasoning, and offer to pair on it for five minutes. What I wouldn't do is push back vaguely or silently ignore it, because then it lands again on someone else later."
**What they evaluate**
- **Whether they treat review as a process to design,** not a box to tick.
- **Concrete review mechanics** — size, description, `nit:` convention, self-review.
- **Emotional regulation** under criticism, which is a genuine predictor.
**Probe:** "A reviewer leaves 30 comments and you're the author. What's your process?" Or: "Your team requires one approval and everyone's rubber-stamping. How would you fix it?"
**Common wrong answers:** a 1500-line PR described as "small refactor"; a description that restates the diff; taking review comments personally; approving a PR you didn't understand because you're blocked; no branch protection.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Many small PRs vs fewer large ones:** small PRs review well and integrate easily, at the cost of more overhead and more `main` merges.
- **Strict review requirements vs throughput:** requiring two approvals and passing checks raises quality and adds latency and queueing. That trade is worth making for auth and payments, and is more debatable for a docs typo.
- **Nit-level comments vs silence:** giving every optional suggestion builds shared standards but trains reviewers to ignore comments.
**Mistakes:** bundling refactors with fixes; a title like "updates"; pushing a draft PR with no note; leaving comments unanswered; reviewing only the diff and not the tests; merging your own PR without a second pair of eyes when the change is risky.
**Mini task:** Take a recent commit and split it into three PRs the way you'd have to. Write the description for each — what, why, how to test — and ask someone to review only the descriptions. If they can't tell what each does, the descriptions failed.
**Resources:** the GitHub docs on pull requests and protected branches; Google's eng practices on code review; "How to Write a Good Pull Request" style write-ups from your own team.
**Self-check:** Can you state the size limit you'd enforce and why, and describe how you'd deliver feedback that a colleague would thank you for?

## 2.3.8 GitHub Actions Intro (full CI/CD in Phase 8)
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** Running your build, tests and linters automatically on GitHub's hosted runners, triggered by pushes and pull requests, defined as YAML in the repository.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because CI is the mechanism that makes every other quality practice enforceable. If nothing fails automatically, code review becomes the only gate, and code review doesn't scale.
**Prerequisites:** 2.5.2 (package managers), 2.3.7.

**Key concepts**
```yaml
# .github/workflows/ci.yml
name: CI
on:
  pull_request:
  push:
    branches: [main]
jobs:
  test:
    runs-on: ubuntu-latest
    steps:
      - uses: actions/checkout@v4
      - uses: actions/setup-node@v4
        with:
          node-version: 20
          cache: npm
      - run: npm ci
      - run: npm run lint
      - run: npm test
```
Concepts: **workflow** (file) → **jobs** (parallel by default) → **steps**; **triggers** (`on:`); **runners**; **secrets** (`${{ secrets.NAME }}`, never hardcode); **matrix builds** for multiple Node versions; **caching** for speed.

- **The workflow/jobs/steps hierarchy is the mental model.** A workflow is a file, jobs run in parallel unless you declare `needs`, and steps run in order inside a job. Most "why didn't my step see that variable" problems are a job-vs-step confusion.
- **`on: pull_request` is what makes CI a review tool.** It tests the merge result before anyone approves, which is what lets you require a passing check.
- **`npm ci` in CI, `npm install` on your machine.** `ci` installs exactly the lockfile, fails if the lockfile is out of sync with `package.json`, and is faster. Using `install` in CI means you're testing a dependency tree nobody has.
- **Secrets come from the environment, never from the repo.** `${{ secrets.NAME }}` is masked in logs, but the value still lands in the runner's environment, so treat it as exposed on that machine.
- **Pinning matters.** `actions/checkout@v4` is a tag that can be moved; `@<sha>` is immutable. For a third-party action, a moved tag is a supply-chain vector.
- **Caching is the difference between 40 seconds and 4 minutes.** `setup-node` with `cache: npm` uses the lockfile as the cache key, which is why a lockfile is also a CI speed optimisation.
- **A matrix is the cheapest way to prove version support.** Testing Node 18, 20 and 22 costs minutes and turns "we support Node 20" from a claim into a check.
- **`if:` conditions, concurrency groups and timeouts are the three things you add on day two.** Concurrency in particular prevents two pushes from fighting over the same deploy environment.

**Questions**
1. What are workflows, jobs and steps?
2. Why `npm ci` instead of `npm install` in CI?
3. How do you access a secret in a workflow?
4. What is a matrix build and why would you add one?
5. How do you cache dependencies, and what determines the cache key?
6. Why run CI on `pull_request` as well as `push`?
7. Should you pin actions to a tag or a SHA?
8. Your CI passes locally but fails in Actions. Debugging approach?

**Model answer, Q2:** "`npm ci` installs exactly what's in the lockfile and errors if `package.json` and `package.lock.json` disagree. `npm install` is allowed to update the lockfile, which means CI could silently install versions you never committed and never tested. On a laptop that's convenient; in CI it means your build isn't reproducible and your test result describes a dependency tree nobody has. `ci` is also faster because it skips resolution and goes straight from the lockfile to the store."
**Model answer, Q8:** "I'd start by reading the actual failure rather than guessing — the log tells me which step failed, and whether it's the install, the lint or the tests. Then I'd reproduce locally in a clean state: delete `node_modules`, run `npm ci`, run the same script. The most common causes are an uncommitted file that exists on your machine, case-sensitivity differences on Linux, a missing environment variable or secret, and a Node version difference — which is exactly what the matrix is for. If I can't reproduce it, I'd re-run with debug logging enabled on that step rather than adding `echo`s everywhere."
**What they evaluate**
- **The hierarchy,** because it's the thing people get wrong when debugging.
- **Reproducibility discipline** — lockfiles, pinning, `npm ci`.
- **Awareness that CI is a separate environment** with different OS, case sensitivity and available services.
**Probe:** "Your workflow sets an env var in one job and reads it in another. Why doesn't it work?" Or: "How would you make sure a workflow can't leak your deploy key?"
**Common wrong answers:** jobs and steps treated as interchangeable; `npm install` in CI because "it's the same"; secrets committed to the workflow file; a matrix described as parallel jobs; caching without knowing what the key is.
**Trade-offs:**
- **GitHub-hosted runners vs self-hosted:** zero setup and good spec parity, at a per-minute cost and no persistent cache; self-hosted is free and cacheable, but you're patching it and it can be a network pivot for a PR build.
- **Tag pinning vs SHA pinning:** tags are readable and easy to update; SHAs are immutable but need a bot or Renovate to keep current.
- **One workflow vs many:** separate workflows per concern are clearer and can run in parallel, at the cost of duplicated setup and harder local reproduction.
**Mistakes:** `npm install` in CI; committing `.env` because "it's a secret variable"; no caching on a large dependency tree; a workflow that passes on `push` but not on PRs; not setting timeouts so a hung job burns minutes; running untrusted PR code with write permissions.
**Mini task:** Add the workflow above to a real repository, deliberately break the lint step on a branch, and open a PR. Watch it fail, then protect `main` so it can't merge without a passing check. Then add a Node matrix.
**Resources:** the GitHub Actions documentation; "GitHub Actions cheat sheet" by GitHub; the workflow-syntax reference for `if`, `concurrency` and `permissions`.
**Self-check:** Can you explain the workflow/job/step hierarchy, say why `npm ci` is the CI-correct choice, and name three things you'd check when a CI failure won't reproduce locally?

## 2.3.9 Conventional Commits
🟢 Beginner · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** A convention where each commit message starts with a type and optional scope — `feat(auth): add refresh token rotation` — so that history, changelogs and version numbers can be generated from the log.
**Why interviewers ask:** As a proxy for whether you care about the readability of history and whether you'd automate the tedious parts. It also connects directly to release engineering, which is a common senior follow-up.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.2, 2.3.3 (interactive rebase is how you retrofit it).

**Key concepts**
```
<type>(<scope>): <description>

feat(auth): add refresh token rotation
fix(cart): correct rounding in total
docs: update README setup
refactor(api): extract validation middleware
test, chore, perf, ci, build
feat!: drop Node 16 support          # ! or BREAKING CHANGE: footer
```
Benefits: readable history, automated changelogs and semantic versioning (`semantic-release`), easy filtering. Commit message rules: imperative mood ("add", not "added"), subject under about 50 characters, body explains *why*.

**.gitignore essentials:** `node_modules/`, `.env`, `dist/`, `.DS_Store`, coverage output. **Never commit secrets.** If you do, rotate the secret immediately. Removing it from history is not enough because it may already be cloned or scraped.

- **The type is the payload.** It lets a machine answer "what changed?" — `fix` commits are patches, `feat` commits are minor bumps, a `!` or `BREAKING CHANGE:` footer is a major. Without it, every release has to be annotated by hand.
- **Imperative mood because the subject is a command the log will show.** "add rate limiting" reads as something you did; "added rate limiting" reads as a status update. Consistency is what makes `git log --grep` useful.
- **The subject says what, the body says why.** The diff already records the what; the *why* — the constraint, the incident, the alternative you rejected — is the only thing the history cannot reconstruct, and it's the part future readers need.
- **Conventional Commits is a convention, not a tool.** Nothing in Git enforces it. Enforcement is `commitlint` in a pre-commit hook or a CI check, and a team that adopts the convention without enforcement will drift within a month.
- **Retrofitting is an interactive rebase,** and it's worth doing on a feature branch: reword the five commits you made this morning so they read consistently, and leave published history alone.
- **Squash merges and Conventional Commits are partners.** A squash merge produces exactly one commit, so if that one commit follows the convention, the whole team's history does. This is why the two decisions are usually made together.
- **`.gitignore` is preventive, not corrective.** Ignoring a file that was already committed doesn't untrack it — you need `git rm --cached`. And if a secret got in, ignoring it afterwards does nothing about the copies already made.

**Questions**
1. What is the Conventional Commits format, and what is each part for?
2. Why imperative mood?
3. What does the `!` do, and what's the alternative?
4. How does this connect to semantic versioning?
5. How would you enforce it on a team that doesn't follow it?
6. You have five inconsistent commits on a branch. What do you do?
7. Why do squash merges and Conventional Commits go together?
8. You committed a secret. What's the order of operations?
9. I added a file to `.gitignore` but Git still tracks it. Why?

**Model answer, Q1:** "A commit message starts with a type, optionally a scope in parentheses, then a short imperative description — like `feat(auth): add refresh token rotation`. The type tells a tool what kind of change it is, the scope says where, and the description says what happened. That structure is machine-readable, which is the point: it's what lets you generate a changelog, filter history by kind of change, and derive the next version number from the commits alone."
**Model answer, Q8:** "Rotate first. The second it was pushed it's compromised, and no amount of history rewriting changes that — clones and forks and scrapers may already have it. Then tell the team so they can rotate anything derived from it and re-clone. Then remove it from history with `git filter-repo`, force push, and add it to `.gitignore` and to secret scanning. The order matters: rotating is the only step that actually fixes the problem."
**What they evaluate**
- **Understanding the automation payoff,** not the syntax.
- **Knowing that enforcement is the hard part.**
- **Getting the secret-rotation order right.**
**Probe:** "Your team squashes every PR. How would that change your commit message habits?" Or: "Why is `git rm --cached` needed after adding to `.gitignore`?"
**Common wrong answers:** the convention described as a Git feature; enforcement assumed to be automatic; cleaning history before rotating a secret; assuming `.gitignore` untracks a file; past-tense subjects.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Squash vs no squash with a convention:** squash guarantees one conventional commit per change; preserving commits keeps granularity but means every commit in the branch must follow the convention or the automation misreads it.
- **Commitlint in a hook vs in CI:** a hook catches it before the commit exists, which gives fast feedback but can be bypassed with `--no-verify`; CI is unskippable but slower and after the fact.
- **Strict types vs a long list:** a short list people remember beats a comprehensive one they ignore. `feat`, `fix`, `docs`, `refactor`, `test`, `chore` covers most work.
**Mistakes:** type in the past tense; descriptions longer than the subject; no body explaining why; a 14-type taxonomy nobody remembers; no enforcement; assuming a `chore` commit is a breaking change.
**Mini task:** Take your last ten commits and rewrite them with `git rebase -i` to the convention, adding a body to each explaining why. Then run `npx semantic-release --dry-run` (or a `git log --grep` walkthrough) and see whether the version bump it suggests matches what you'd have chosen by hand.
**Resources:** the Conventional Commits specification; the Angular commit conventions (the origin of the style); `git log --oneline --grep="^fix"` as the cheapest way to feel the payoff.
**Self-check:** Can you write a conventional commit with a body that a future reader would find useful, and explain why squash merges make the convention easier to keep?

## 2.3.10 Init/commit/branch/merge
🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · the daily loop, and the commands you should never have to look up

**Definition:** The four actions that make up almost every working day in Git: turning a directory into a repository, recording a checkpoint, creating a line of development, and bringing lines back together.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because it is the entry-level screen. They are checking you can operate the tool before trusting you to design anything, and the tells are specific: whether you know what a commit actually *is* (a snapshot with a parent), whether you stage deliberately or reflexively, and whether you can say what branch you are on without running a command. It is also the question where candidates reveal they have memorised commands without understanding that Git stores objects and refs, which is what every later Git question depends on.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.1.

**Key concepts**
- **`git init` creates the repo,** `.git/` is the whole database, and a repository is just a directory with that folder in it. Cloning an existing repo skips this step entirely.
- **The three places work lives:** the **working tree** (files on disk), the **staging area / index** (`git add`, a curated set of changes), and the **repository** (`git commit`). This is the mental model behind every confusing Git question.
```bash
git init
git status                  # what is modified, staged, untracked — run this constantly
git add src/one.ts          # stage ONE file, deliberately
git commit -m "fix: handle null user in getUserById"
```
- **A commit is a snapshot plus a pointer to its parent(s),** and its identity is a hash of that content. Unchanged files are reused, which is why commits are cheap and why the repo grows by deltas.
- **`git add .` and `git add -A` stage everything including junk,** which is the single most common way secrets get committed. `git add <path>` per file is slower and keeps you in control.
- **The staging area is a feature, not a hurdle:** it is how you split "this bug fix plus its test" into two coherent commits instead of one unrelated lump.
- **`git branch` is a movable label,** not a copy. Creating one is instant and near-free; `git switch -c feat/login` creates and moves to it in one step (`git checkout -b` on older versions).
- **Commit early and often.** Because a commit is a checkpoint, a small history is recoverable in a way an unsaved working tree is not.
- **Merging brings two lines together** and always produces a merge commit recording both parents, which is why `git log --graph --oneline --all` shows the topology.
```bash
git switch main
git fetch origin
git merge feat/login          # integrate; creates a merge commit
git push origin feat/login    # publish the branch
```
- **Merging never deletes anyone else's commits,** which is the property that makes it safe on shared branches and is the exact opposite of rebase (2.3.3).
- **After every merge or rebase, run the tests.** A clean merge can still be a semantic conflict — two files that both compile and both pass their own tests, while disagreeing about the feature.
- **Uncommitted work is not lost but it is fragile:** `git stash` parks it, and it will still be there when you come back (2.3.5).

**Questions**
1. Walk me through your Git day. What commands, in what order?
2. What are the three states a change can be in?
3. What's the difference between `git add` and `git commit`?
4. What actually is a commit?
5. What's the difference between a branch and a tag?
6. When would you merge instead of rebase?
7. You have uncommitted changes and need to switch branches. What do you do?
8. Why does Git let you recover deleted commits?

**Model answer, Q1:** "I `git pull --rebase` on main in the morning to get current, `git switch -c` a branch off it, then work in small increments — `git add <file>` for the specific file I'm changing, `git commit` with a conventional message, and repeat so each commit is one logical change. When it's ready I push the branch, open a PR, and after review I merge it. If something unexpected happens mid-work I `git stash` with a message rather than trying to carry it across."
**Model answer, Q3:** "`git add` moves changes from the working tree into the staging area, and `git commit` records the staged snapshot permanently. The staging area in between is what lets me split unrelated work into separate commits — I can stage the test file and commit it separately from the fix — which I couldn't do if add and commit were one action."
**Model answer, Q4:** "A commit is a full snapshot of the project at a point in time, plus metadata and a pointer to its parent commit. Files that didn't change are referenced rather than copied, so it's cheap. Its ID is a hash of its content and parent, which is what makes the history tamper-evident and what lets Git dedupe identical trees across branches."
**Model answer, Q6:** "I merge when the branch is shared or already published, because merge preserves the real history and doesn't rewrite commits anyone else may have pulled. I rebase only my own local feature branch onto main to keep it linear and tidy up noise before the PR. The rule I hold is: never rebase commits other people already have based work on."
**Model answer, Q7:** "I'd `git stash push -m 'wip login'`, switch, do the other work, then `git stash pop` to bring it back. If the change is genuinely a separate piece of work I'd commit it on its own branch instead, because a commit is a safer parking spot than a stash. I avoid `git checkout -f` or `git reset --hard` unless I actually intend to discard work, which is usually the mistake I'm recovering from."

**What they evaluate**
- **Deliberate staging rather than `git add .`,** which is the main tell that they've debugged a leaked secret before.
- **Understanding of the working tree / index / repo model** rather than a command list.
- **Knowing branch is a label,** which is the foundation for the rebase discussion in 2.3.3.
**Probe:** "You're mid-merge and realise the feature branch has 200 commits from another team. What now?" (Expect: abort, rebase your own branch on main, and coordinate — never rebase shared history.)
**Common wrong answers:** "a commit stores a diff" (it stores a snapshot); "a branch is a copy of the files"; "you must commit before switching branches" (switching is allowed and carries changes across); `git add .` recommended as the fast path; "tag is a branch that doesn't move" with no mention that tags can move and shouldn't.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Small frequent commits vs large logical ones:** small commits are individually restorable and make review and bisect precise, but can become unreadable noise; squashing per-PR gives a clean history at the cost of intermediate steps — a bisect may then skip over a change.
- **Merge commits vs fast-forward:** fast-forward produces no merge commit and a perfectly linear log, but only when the branch has no unique commits; merge commits record that two lines converged, which costs a little noise for real information.
- **Local branches vs pushing every commit:** pushing early gives a backup and lets others see progress, but publishes commits that are then expensive to rewrite, so it trades flexibility for safety.
**Mistakes:** `git add .` with a `.env` in the tree; committing `node_modules` or build output without a `.gitignore`; committing straight to `main` on a team project; "fixing" a bad merge by resetting instead of reverting; force-pushing a shared branch; leaving merge conflicts unresolved with a `--no-verify` push.
**Mini task:** In a fresh repo, create a branch, make three separate commits where the second one includes only a test file, and inspect `git status` between each step so you can see the index working. Then merge it back and read the graph with `git log --graph --oneline --all`.
**Resources:** the official Git tutorial (`git-scm.com/book`) — the Pro Git book chapters 2 and 3 are the relevant ones; `git help` for any command rather than a blog post; GitHub's "Merging vs rebasing" write-up for the team-level view.
**Self-check:** Can you name the three states a change passes through, explain what a commit physically is, and say why you'd never run `git add .` on a real project?

## 2.3.11 Reflog and recovery
🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know** · the answer to "I lost my work" and the reason `git reset --hard` is survivable

**Definition:** The local, append-only log of every position `HEAD` and every branch reference has held, which is what makes commits that appear destroyed still recoverable.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because it's the question that separates people who *use* Git from people who *understand* it. Almost everyone knows `git reset --hard` is dangerous; very few can say why it's still recoverable, and being able to say "reflog" in that moment is the entire answer. It's also a genuinely useful production skill — recovering a lost commit at 5pm is a real event, and juniors who don't know about reflog spend hours rewriting work that was never destroyed.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.5, 2.3.10.

**Key concepts**
- **`git reflog` records every movement of `HEAD`,** with an entry like `HEAD@{0}: reset: moving to HEAD~1`. Each entry is a full snapshot, and the old commits stay in the object database until garbage collection removes unreachable ones.
- **`HEAD@{n}` is "where HEAD was n moves ago,"** and `git rev-parse HEAD@{1}` turns that into a real hash.
```bash
git reflog                          # the movement log
git reflog show feature/login       # per-branch, when you need the branch name
git reflog --date=local             # real timestamps, not reflog indices
```
- **The universal recovery recipe:** `git reflog` → find the hash before the bad move → **inspect it before touching anything** → recover.
```bash
git reflog
# a1b2c3d HEAD@{2}: commit: the feature I "lost"
git show a1b2c3d                    # look at it first, always
git switch -c rescue a1b2c3d        # put it on a branch so it can't be GC'd
# or cherry-pick: git cherry-pick a1b2c3d
```
- **Rescue onto a branch first.** A detached `HEAD` pointing at an unreachable commit is one `git gc` away from gone; a branch reference keeps it alive forever.
- **`git bisect` finds the commit that introduced a bug** by binary search over history, and `git bisect run` automates the good/bad test — a genuinely impressive trick to demonstrate.
```bash
git bisect start
git bisect bad                 # current commit is broken
git bisect good v1.2.0         # this one worked
git bisect run npm test        # let the suite drive it
git bisect reset
```
- **A pushed secret is not recoverable this way.** Reflog only protects commits that were never shared. For a leaked credential the answer is rotate first, then clean history, then tell the team — in that order.
- **Cleaning history** uses `git filter-repo` (or BFG), which rewrites every commit containing the string. Because hashes change, everyone must re-clone or hard-reset, and open PRs must be recreated.
- **Reflog is local and per-clone.** It is not pushed, it does not help on a server where the repo was deleted, and it expires under aggressive `gc` settings — so it is a safety net for hours or days, not a backup.
- **Recovery is faster the less you do afterwards.** Every new commit or checkout moves `HEAD` again, so the entry you want slides to a higher `@{n}`. Note the hashes down when you find them.

**Questions**
1. What does `git reflog` record, and what is `HEAD@{2}`?
2. You ran `git reset --hard HEAD~5` by accident. What now?
3. Does reflog help if someone else already pulled the deleted commits?
4. How do you find the commit that introduced a bug?
5. You committed an API key. What's the order of operations?
6. Can reflog save you after `git gc`? Why or why not?
7. What's the difference between `reset --soft`, `--mixed` and `--hard`?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Reflog is a local log of every commit `HEAD` has pointed at, plus every branch movement. `HEAD@{2}` is the position `HEAD` held two moves ago, which is a full snapshot rather than a diff, so I can check it out or branch from it."
**Model answer, Q2:** "Nothing's lost yet — the commits are still in the object database, they're just unreferenced. I'd run `git reflog`, find the hash from before the reset, `git show` it to confirm it's the right content, then `git switch -c rescue <hash>` to give it a permanent reference, and cherry-pick or merge from there. I would not run any more commands before inspecting, because each one moves `HEAD` again."
**Model answer, Q3:** "No. Reflog is local to a clone and only records local movements, so if the commits were pushed and someone else pulled them, reflog tells me nothing about their clones — and if I deleted a pushed branch, the objects are still there but only because *they* reference them. My recovery there would be to push again from my reflog hash."
**Model answer, Q4:** "I'd use `git bisect`: mark the current commit bad, mark a known-good earlier commit good, and Git checks out the midpoint. I run the test, report good or bad, and it halves the range each time, so O(log n) steps instead of O(n). With a working test command I can automate the whole thing with `git bisect run npm test`."
**Model answer, Q5:** "Rotate the credential first — that's the urgent step and nothing else changes the fact that it's compromised. Then I clean the history with `git filter-repo` to remove the string from every commit, force-push, and tell the team that they all need to re-clone or hard-reset and recreate open branches. Reflog won't help here because the commit was already shared."
**Model answer, Q7:** "They differ in how much they discard. `--soft` moves the branch and leaves the changes staged, `--mixed` is the default and leaves them unstaged in the working tree, `--hard` also throws away the working tree, which is the only one that can lose uncommitted work. Reflog recovers commits from all three — the irreversible part of `reset` is only the uncommitted changes."

**What they evaluate**
- **Saying "the commits still exist, they're just unreferenced"** — that's the whole insight.
- **Recommending a branch or cherry-pick,** not just "check the hash out", because a bare detached commit can be garbage-collected away.
- **Correctly scoping reflog as local and short-lived,** and knowing it doesn't help for pushed secrets.
**Probe:** "A teammate says they force-pushed over main and wiped two days of everyone's work. Walk me through what you do." (Expect: pause pushes, check GitHub's PR refs and events — force-pushed commits often remain reachable via pull request refs — then communicate, then recover.)
**Common wrong answers:** "reflog is shared with the team"; "reset --hard deletes commits permanently"; recovering a leaked secret with reflog instead of rotating it; running further commands before inspecting; `git reflog` described as a log of file changes.
**Trade-offs:**
- **`filter-repo` vs adding a `.gitignore` now:** rewriting history cleans the past but forces every collaborator to re-clone and invalidates open PRs; the ignore file only prevents future leaks, so for an already-pushed secret you must rotate regardless.
- **Rescuing with a branch vs cherry-picking:** branching preserves the original history exactly, but if you only want part of it, cherry-picking gives you a clean commit on top of your current line.
- **`gc` default retention vs `gc --prune=now`:** leaving reflog entries alone keeps unreachable objects around for weeks, costing disk; pruning immediately reclaims space but removes the safety net.
**Mistakes:** panicking and running `git gc --prune=now` before writing the hash down; re-cloning instead of checking reflog first; assuming reflog recovers files that were never committed; trying to `reset --hard` forward to "get back" without inspecting the target; rotating a secret only after cleaning history, when rotation should be first.
**Mini task:** Deliberately break it three ways — `reset --hard HEAD~3`, delete a branch, and `checkout` an old commit — then recover each using only reflog, and time yourself. Finish by running `git bisect run` against a repo where you introduce a failing commit at a known point.
**Resources:** the Pro Git book chapter on "Git Internals - Maintenance and Data Recovery"; `git help reflog`; GitHub's article on removing sensitive data; a local practice repo rather than experimenting on work.
**Self-check:** Can you explain why a hard reset is recoverable, name the two commands you'd run first, and say why reflog is useless for a secret that was already pushed?

## 2.3.12 Pull requests and reviews
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · the mechanism most of your actual work happens through

**Definition:** The GitHub workflow for proposing a set of changes on a branch, having them reviewed, and merging them into a target branch with a recorded discussion.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because it's how the interviewer finds out what it's like to work with you. They're not testing Git commands — they want to know whether you keep branches small, whether you write descriptions that get a review without a meeting, whether you can take feedback without getting defensive, and whether you notice when your own change is hard to review. A candidate who describes PRs as "I push and someone approves" has told them they don't understand code review as a collaboration.
**Prerequisites:** 2.3.10, 2.3.6.

**Key concepts**
- **A PR is not a branch — it's a request,** and the distinction matters: you can open a PR from a fork, and you can update a PR by pushing more commits to the same branch without opening a new one.
- **Push your branch before opening the PR,** otherwise the compare view is empty and you spend ten minutes on a white screen.
```bash
git switch -c fix/login-redirect
git push -u origin fix/login-redirect   # -u sets upstream, so later pushes need no args
# then open the PR on GitHub
git push                              # subsequent updates, no flags needed
```
- **The description is the review.** A good one states what changed, why, how to verify it, and what you deliberately left out.
- **Keep it small.** Under ~400 changed lines is the point where reviewers stop skimming and start reading. A 2,000-line PR usually gets rubber-stamped, which is worse than no review at all.
- **Draft PRs** let you open early for feedback on approach before the code is finished — the single best way to avoid building the wrong thing for three days.
- **Review is a conversation, and most comments are questions or suggestions, not blockers.** Say which is which, so the author knows what must change.
- **Structure review comments as: blocking / suggestion / nit,** and never as "this is wrong". The *why* is what makes feedback land.
- **Push follow-up commits rather than force-pushing during review,** unless someone asked you to; rewriting history mid-review makes the diff unreadable for anyone who already looked.
- **Draft replies to every comment,** even "done" — otherwise reviewers can't tell which items were handled.
- **CI is part of the review.** A failing build blocks the merge by design; fixing it locally first rather than three attempts later is the habit that matters.
- **Approving your own PR is a smell,** and required review or CODEOWNERS exists to make that impossible.

**Questions**
1. Walk me through opening a PR.
2. What goes in a good PR description?
3. How large should a PR be?
4. How do you handle review feedback you disagree with?
5. Why not force-push during review?
6. What's a draft PR for?
7. How do you respond to nitpicks in someone's review?
8. What do you do when a reviewer hasn't responded for three days?

**Model answer, Q1:** "I branch off an up-to-date main, push with `git push -u origin <branch>` so the branch has an upstream, then open the PR on GitHub and pick the right base. The description says what changed and why, how to verify it manually, and anything I deliberately left out. If the change is design-heavy I open it as a draft first so people can weigh in on the approach before I finish."
**Model answer, Q2:** "Four things: what changed in one line, why it needs to change, how to actually verify it works — including the manual step, since tests don't cover the UI — and what I chose not to do, with the reason. Reviewers are time-poor, so anything I'd have to explain in person should be in the description."
**Model answer, Q3:** "Around 400 changed lines, including tests and generated files. The limit isn't arbitrary — past it reviewers stop reading carefully and start approving. If a change genuinely can't be split, I split it anyway: schema first, then the code that reads it, then the UI, each as its own PR, and I say in the description that it's a stack."
**Model answer, Q4:** "First I assume they're right about something I haven't considered — usually a real case I didn't think of. I'll reply with the reasoning rather than just my preference: here's the constraint that led me here, and here's what would make me change it. If it turns out I was wrong I say so directly and fix it. What I won't do is silently ignore it or argue tone-wise; I'll have the technical discussion in the thread and take it to a call only if it's genuinely stuck."
**Model answer, Q8:** "Ping the reviewer once with a clear, single question rather than a general nudge, and give them an out — 'want me to split this, or are you happy to review as-is?'. If it's been longer, I ask the team lead or post in the team channel, because an unblocked PR that's stuck for three days is costing the whole team more than a polite follow-up would have."

**What they evaluate**
- **Whether they think in reviewable units,** which is really a question about how they plan work.
- **Empathy in the disagreement answer** — the wrong answer is "I'd just make the change" or "I'd argue until I win".
- **Awareness of draft PRs and stacked branches,** which shows they've worked in a team with a real review culture.
**Probe:** "Your PR has 1,800 changed lines and one approval arrived in four minutes. Is that good news?" (Expect: no — that's a rubber stamp, and something got through unreviewed.)
**Common wrong answers:** PR descriptions that only say "fixes bug"; force-pushing to clean up history mid-review; treating all comments as blocking or none; approving your own PR; waiting for CI to be green before pushing (which wastes a cycle).
**Trade-offs:**
- **Small PRs vs one big PR:** small is reviewable and bisectable but requires more branch management and more merges to rebase onto main; large is simpler to track but effectively unreviewed.
- **Squash vs merge commits:** squash keeps main linear and readable at the cost of losing the intermediate commits and bisect granularity; a merge commit preserves the branch's history and costs a little log noise.
- **Force-push vs merge for updates:** force-push gives reviewers a clean, current diff but rewrites history they may have already read; merging the base back in is safer and leaves a noisier diff.
**Mistakes:** pushing thousands of committed dependency files in a lockfile bump and burying the real change; committing secrets in `.env` and hoping; leaving the PR in draft by accident; resolving conversations without replying; approving to be polite rather than having read it; merging while CI is red.
**Mini task:** Take a recent branch and open a real PR against it with a four-part description and a linked issue. Then deliberately ask a colleague for review and respond to every comment, marking each as blocking, suggestion or nit.
**Resources:** GitHub's "About pull requests" and "About pull request reviews"; the Google engineering practices document on code review, which is the best short writing on the topic; Conventional Commits (2.3.9), because the convention only pays off if squashes are visible.
**Self-check:** Can you write a PR description a stranger could act on without a meeting, name your threshold for splitting, and describe how you'd push back on a reviewer constructively?

## Interview Questions (2.3)
**Questions**
1. `git fetch` vs `git pull`?
2. Merge vs rebase. When would you use each? *(model below)*
3. You committed to `main` by mistake locally. How do you move that commit to a new branch? *(`git branch feature` → `git reset --hard origin/main` (while on main), then `git switch feature`.)*
4. You pushed a bad commit to `main`. How do you undo it? *(`git revert`, because history is shared.)*
5. `reset --soft` vs `--mixed` vs `--hard`?
6. What is a detached HEAD? *(HEAD points at a commit, not a branch. New commits will be orphaned unless you create a branch.)*
7. What's `git reflog` and when saved you? 
8. How do you resolve a merge conflict? What if you pick the wrong resolution?
9. Squash vs merge vs rebase merge on PRs?
10. What does `git stash` do? What does it not include by default? *(untracked files)*
11. Trunk-based vs Gitflow?
12. How does Git store data internally?
13. What is `HEAD`, `origin`, `main`, `upstream`?
14. How do you find the commit that introduced a bug?
15. How do you remove a file from Git but keep it locally? *(`git rm --cached file`, then add it to `.gitignore`.)*
16. You accidentally committed a secret. What do you do? *(Rotate it first. Then remove it from history with `git filter-repo`/BFG if needed, force push, and notify the team. Assume it's compromised.)*
17. How do you undo `git add`? / amend a commit message? / combine the last 3 commits?
18. What is `--force-with-lease` and why is it safer than `--force`?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Both integrate changes, but they handle history differently. Merge combines two branches with a merge commit and preserves the real history, and it never rewrites anything, so it's safe on shared branches. Rebase replays my commits on top of another branch, producing new commits and a linear history, which is cleaner but rewrites history, so I only do it on my own local branches. In practice I rebase my feature branch onto main to stay current and squash noisy commits, then merge the PR via the team's chosen strategy. I never rebase something others have based work on."
**Model answer, Q7:** "The reflog is a local, per-repository log of everywhere `HEAD` has pointed, including commits that are no longer on any branch. So after a `reset --hard` or a bad rebase, the commit is usually still there — I find its SHA with `git reflog`, make a branch at it with `git branch rescue <sha>` so garbage collection can't take it, and then recover. What it can't do is help if the reflog has expired, if the work only ever existed on another machine, or if someone else force pushed and you never had the commits locally."
**Model answer, Q10:** "It parks your uncommitted changes and resets the working tree, so you can switch branches with a clean slate, and `pop` brings them back. By default it does *not* include untracked files — a new file you've never added stays in the working directory and follows you across branches, which is the surprise people hit. `git stash push -u` includes untracked files. And `pop` drops the entry if it applies cleanly, whereas `apply` keeps it, which is safer if you're not sure."
**Model answer, Q16:** "Rotate the secret first — that's the only step that fixes it. Then tell the team, since their clones are affected too. Then remove it from history with `git filter-repo` or BFG, force push with `--force-with-lease`, and have everyone re-clone. Then add it to `.gitignore` and turn on secret scanning. Cleaning history without rotating is the wrong order and leaves you with a repo that looks clean and a credential that still works."

**Mistakes:** `git add .` committing junk or secrets; `git push --force` to shared branches; giant PRs; vague messages like "fix stuff"; panicking instead of using reflog; committing `node_modules`.

**Mini tasks**
1. Create a repo with two branches that edit the same line. Merge, resolve the conflict, then redo it with rebase and compare the history graphs.
2. Make 5 messy commits, then use `rebase -i` to squash them into 2 clean ones.
3. Do `git reset --hard HEAD~3`, then recover the commits with reflog.
4. Introduce a bug across 20 commits (scripted), find it with `git bisect run`.
5. Add the CI workflow above to a repo and make a PR that fails, then fix it.

**Resources:** *Pro Git* (free online), learngitbranching.js.org (interactive), "Oh Shit, Git!?!", GitHub Docs.

**Self-check:** Can you undo any common mistake (bad commit, wrong branch, lost work, pushed secret) without searching?

---

# 2.4 PROGRAMMING FUNDAMENTALS

**Prerequisites:** none. 🟢 · **Must-know.** Examples use JavaScript, since it's the common full stack language.

**Why interviewers ask:** These are warm-up and screening questions. Weak answers on fundamentals end interviews early. Strong answers on value vs reference and recursion lay the groundwork for DSA.

## 2.4.1 Variables and Types
🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition:** How values are stored, copied and compared in JavaScript — and why the answer to "pass by value or reference?" is neither of the options people expect.
**Why interviewers ask:** Value versus reference is the single most misunderstood thing in the language, and it shows up in every bug where "the array changed and I don't know why". If you can explain the copy semantics precisely, most of the mutation bugs in a codebase become obvious to you.
**Prerequisites:** none.

**Key concepts**
- **JS primitives (7):** `string`, `number`, `bigint`, `boolean`, `undefined`, `null`, `symbol`. Everything else is an **object** (arrays, functions, dates, maps).
- **Primitives are immutable and copied by value. Objects are copied by reference** (the variable holds a pointer).
```js
let a = 1; let b = a; b = 2;           // a is still 1
const o1 = { n: 1 }; const o2 = o1; o2.n = 2;   // o1.n is 2 (same object)
const copy = { ...o1 };                // shallow copy; nested objects still shared
const deep = structuredClone(o1);      // deep copy
```
- **`const` ≠ immutable:** it prevents *reassignment*, not mutation of the object.
- **`let`/`const`/`var`:** block vs function scope, hoisting, and TDZ (deep dive in Phase 3).
- **Static vs dynamic typing; strong vs weak:** JS is dynamically and weakly typed (implicit coercion). TypeScript adds static types at compile time.
- **Coercion gotchas:** `"5" + 1 === "51"`, `"5" - 1 === 4`, `[] + {} === "[object Object]"`, `null == undefined` is true, `null === undefined` is false, `typeof null === "object"` (historic bug), `NaN !== NaN` (use `Number.isNaN`).
- **Falsy values:** `false, 0, -0, 0n, "", null, undefined, NaN`. Everything else is truthy, including `"0"`, `[]`, `{}`.
- **Equality:** always prefer `===`.
- **JavaScript is strictly pass-by-value, always.** There is no pass-by-reference in the language. What confuses people is that for objects, the *value* being passed is a reference. Reassigning the parameter rebinds a local copy of the reference; mutating through it is visible to the caller. One sentence, and it explains every case.
- **`typeof null === "object"` is a bug from 1995, preserved forever.** Fixing it would break the web, so it will never change. Worth knowing as a fact about compatibility over correctness.
- **`NaN !== NaN` because IEEE 754 says NaN compares unequal to everything,** including itself. It is the only value not equal to itself, which is why `x !== x` is the correct NaN test in modern code — and why `Number.isNaN` is clearer.
- **Shallow versus deep copy is about depth.** `{...o}` copies the top level, so `o.nested` is still shared. `structuredClone` walks the whole graph, and fails on functions and DOM nodes, which is why `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(x))` is tempting and wrong.

**Questions**
1. Pass by value vs pass by reference. What does JS actually do?
2. `==` vs `===`?
3. What are the falsy values, and what surprises people about them?
4. `null` vs `undefined`?
5. Shallow vs deep copy, and when do you need which?
6. Why is `0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3`? *(see 2.6)*
7. `typeof` of `[]`, `null`, `function(){}`, `NaN`? *(`"object"`, `"object"`, `"function"`, `"number"`)*
8. What does `const` actually guarantee?

**Model answer, Q1:** "JavaScript is strictly pass-by-value. When you pass an object, what gets copied is the reference — so the parameter is a new local variable holding the same reference the caller has. That means mutating the object through the parameter is visible to the caller, but reassigning the parameter just repoints the local copy and the caller sees nothing. So `change(o, n)` in the drill below sets `o.x` to 99 and leaves the caller's `n` at 1."
**Model answer, Q3:** "The falsy values are `false`, `0`, `-0`, `0n`, the empty string, `null`, `undefined` and `NaN`. The traps are the truthy ones people assume are falsy: the string `"0"` is truthy because it's a non-empty string, and so are `[]` and `{}` — which is why `if (arr)` is always true and never tests emptiness. That's why you check `arr.length`, and why you use `??` rather than `||` when `0` and `""` are legitimate values."
**Model answer, Q5:** "A shallow copy duplicates only the top level, so nested objects are still shared — `{...o}` then `copy.nested.x = 1` changes the original's nested object. That's fine when the data is flat, and it's why spreading an object with nested state is a common accidental-sharing bug. A deep copy walks the graph with `structuredClone`, which is what you want when the nested structure really is independent. The cost is that it's more work and it fails on functions and DOM nodes, so deep-copying state you don't own is usually the wrong fix."
**What they evaluate**
- **Precision on copy semantics,** stated without hedging into "it depends".
- **Knowing the falsy list *and* the surprising truthy cases.**
- **Noticing that `const` is about the binding, not the value.**
**Probe:** "You pass an array to a function that does `arr.length = 0`. What does the caller see, and why?" Or: "Why is `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(obj))` a bad deep clone?"
**Common wrong answers:** "JavaScript passes objects by reference"; `[]` and `{}` as falsy; `const` described as making an object immutable; `typeof null` as `"null"`; `NaN === NaN`; shallow copy described as a full copy.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Immutability vs mutation:** immutable values are safe to share and compare by reference, at the cost of allocating on every change. Mutation is cheap and easy to get wrong. Most production code picks one per layer and is explicit about it.
- **Shallow vs deep copy:** shallow is O(width) and preserves shared substructure — which is sometimes exactly what you want; deep is expensive and breaks object identity.
- **`==` vs `===`:** `==` is convenient for coercing strings from form inputs; `===` is predictable. The predictable one wins, and `Number(x)` at the boundary beats `==` at every call site.
**Mistakes:** mutating a function argument and wondering why the caller changed; using `||` for a default where `0` is valid; shallow-copying nested state; `if (array)` to test emptiness; relying on `const` to make an object's contents fixed.
**Mini task:** Predict the output of the drill below, then run it. Then write a version that mutates without the caller seeing it, and a version that returns a genuinely independent copy.
**Resources:** MDN "Data types", "Equality comparisons and sameness", and "Indexed collections"; javascript.info's chapters on types and objects.
**Self-check:** Can you answer "pass by value or reference?" in one precise sentence, list every falsy value, and explain why `const` isn't immutability?

**Code-output drill**
```js
function change(obj, n) { obj.x = 99; n = 99; obj = { x: 0 }; }
const o = { x: 1 }; let n = 1;
change(o, n);
console.log(o.x, n);   // 99 1 : mutation visible; reassignment and primitive copy are not
```

## 2.4.2 Conditionals, Loops, and Control Flow
🟢 Beginner · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The constructs that choose between paths and repeat work — conditionals, the loop family, and the operators that make control flow compact.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because the interesting failures here aren't syntax, they're semantics: a loop over object keys, a `||` that ate a legitimate zero, an early exit that doesn't exist in `forEach`. These are the bugs that appear in real code reviews constantly.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.1.

**Key concepts**
- `if/else`, `switch` (remember `break`), ternary, short-circuit (`&&`, `||`), nullish coalescing (`??` only treats `null`/`undefined` as empty, whereas `||` treats all falsy values as empty), optional chaining (`?.`).
- **Loops:** `for`, `while`, `do...while`, `for...of` (values of iterables), `for...in` (enumerable keys, avoid on arrays), `forEach`/`map`/`filter`/`reduce`. `break`/`continue` don't work inside `forEach` callbacks.
- **Early return / guard clauses** instead of deep nesting.
- **Off-by-one errors** and loop invariants.
- **Await in loops:** `for...of` with `await` runs sequentially, `Promise.all(items.map(...))` runs in parallel (Phase 3).

**The loop-choice decision, in order:** do you need the index? `for` or `for...of` with a counter. Do you need to stop early or return a value? `for...of`, `some` or `every`. Do you need a new collection? `map`/`filter`. Are you folding into one value? `reduce`. Reach for `forEach` only when you genuinely want the side effect and nothing else.
- **`for...in` iterates keys, and that includes inherited ones.** On an array it gives you string indices plus anything enumerable on `Array.prototype`, which is a classic source of "why is my loop running too many times". `for...of` gives you values, in order, and works on anything iterable including `Set` and `Map`.
- **`??` exists because `||` is too aggressive.** `0 || 10` is `10`, but so is `'' || 'fallback'`. When zero and the empty string are legitimate values — a price, a count, a search term — `??` only falls back on `null` and `undefined`, which is what you meant.
- **`break` cannot cross a function boundary,** so it does nothing inside a `forEach` callback. That is a language rule, not a bug, and it's why early exit requires `for...of`, `some` or `every`.
- **Guard clauses beat nesting.** An `if (!user) return error` at the top keeps the rest of the function at one indentation level, which makes the happy path readable without scrolling.
- **Loop invariants are how you convince yourself a loop is correct.** Stating "at the top of each iteration, `i` is the index of the first unprocessed element" is what turns reading a loop into verifying it.

**Questions**
1. `||` vs `??`? *(`0 || 10` → 10, `0 ?? 10` → 0.)*
2. `for...in` vs `for...of`?
3. How do you exit a `forEach` early? *(you can't, so use `for...of`, `some`, or `every`.)*
4. Write FizzBuzz.
5. When would you pick `for`, `for...of`, `for...in` and `forEach`?
6. Why is `break` ineffective inside a `forEach`?
7. When do you use `reduce` instead of `map`?
8. Sequential or parallel awaits — how do you choose?

**Model answer, Q1:** "`||` falls back on any falsy value, so it treats `0`, `''` and `NaN` as missing. `??` only falls back on `null` and `undefined`. So if you're defaulting a numeric config and zero is a legitimate value, `||` silently replaces it and `??` doesn't. I'll use `??` by default and reach for `||` only when I actually mean 'any falsy value'."
**Model answer, Q5:** "If I need the index, `for` or `for...of` with a counter. If I need the values, `for...of` — it works on arrays, `Set`, `Map` and strings. If I need keys, `for...in`, which I keep off arrays because it includes inherited enumerable keys. And `forEach` only when I want a side effect and no return value; if I want a new array it's `map`, and if I want to stop early it has to be `for...of`, `some` or `every`."
**What they evaluate**
- **Choosing the right loop,** which is a design decision, not syntax recall.
- **Knowing why `??` exists** — the zero-and-empty-string cases.
- **Understanding that `break` can't cross a function boundary.**
**Probe:** "You need the index of the first element failing a predicate — which loop?" Or: "Why is `forEach` a poor fit for async work?"
**Common wrong answers:** `||` and `??` as synonyms; `for...in` over arrays without noticing the hazard; expecting `break` to work in `forEach`; `forEach` returning a value; off-by-one loops on `length` instead of `length - 1`.
**Trade-offs:**
- **`for` vs `for...of`:** `for` is faster and gives the index; `for...of` is clearer, works on any iterable, and allocates an iterator. At application level, clarity wins; in a hot inner loop, it may not.
- **Loops vs array methods:** `map`/`filter`/`reduce` are declarative and read closer to the intent; explicit loops are faster and let you `break`. Pick per hot path, not per style guide.
- **Sequential vs parallel `await`:** sequential is correct when operations depend on each other or you're rate-limiting; parallel is faster but multiplies concurrent load and can overwhelm a downstream service.
**Mistakes:** `for...in` over arrays; `if (arr)` instead of `arr.length`; `||` for numeric defaults; `break` inside `forEach`; mutating the array you're iterating; off-by-one on `length`.
**Mini task:** Write the same loop four ways — `for`, `for...of`, `map` and `forEach` — and then deliberately break each one (off-by-one, `break` in `forEach`, mutating while iterating) so you recognise the failure modes.
**Resources:** MDN "Control flow and error handling" and the `Array.prototype` reference; javascript.info's loops chapter.
**Self-check:** Can you name the one case where `??` is right and `||` is wrong, explain why `break` fails in `forEach`, and pick the right loop for a problem that needs early exit?

```js
for (let i = 1; i <= 100; i++) {
  let out = '';
  if (i % 3 === 0) out += 'Fizz';
  if (i % 5 === 0) out += 'Buzz';
  console.log(out || i);
}
```

## 2.4.3 Functions
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The unit of behaviour in JavaScript — declarations, expressions and arrows, plus the higher-order patterns, closures and call stack that everything else in the language is built from.
**Why interviewers ask:** Closures and pure functions are the two ideas that unlock the rest of the language. A candidate who understands why `makeCounter` works can reason about React hooks, event handlers and memory leaks without having memorised any of them.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.1.

**Key concepts**
- **Declarations (hoisted) vs expressions vs arrow functions** (lexical `this`, no `arguments`, can't be constructors).
- **First-class functions:** can be assigned, passed, and returned. **Higher-order function:** takes or returns a function. **Callback:** a function passed to be called later.
- **Pure function:** same input → same output, no side effects (no mutation, I/O, randomness, or time dependence). Easy to test, cache (memoize), and reason about.
- **Side effects:** isolate them at the edges of your program.
- **Parameters:** defaults, rest (`...args`), destructuring.
- **Scope and closures:** a function remembers variables from where it was *defined* (full coverage in Phase 3).
- **Call stack:** each call pushes a frame, and `return` pops it. Too many frames cause a stack overflow.
- **Single responsibility, small functions, descriptive names, few parameters (use an options object).**

```js
// pure
const add = (a, b) => a + b;
// impure: depends on and mutates external state
let total = 0;
const addToTotal = n => { total += n; };

// higher-order + closure
const makeCounter = () => { let c = 0; return () => ++c; };
const next = makeCounter(); next(); next(); // 1, 2
```

- **A closure is a function plus the variables it captured where it was defined.** `makeCounter` works because the returned function keeps `c` alive after `makeCounter` has returned. Two independent calls get two independent `c`s, which is the property people actually test.
- **Arrows differ from normal functions in three ways that matter:** no own `this` (lexical, inherited from where the arrow is *written*), no `arguments`, and no `new` constructor. That's why arrow functions are wrong for object methods and event handlers that need `this`.
- **Hoisting differs by form.** A function declaration is hoisted and callable before its line; a `const`-bound arrow is in the temporal dead zone until that line executes, so calling it earlier throws. Same for `let` and `const`.
- **Purity is about the *whole* environment, not just the arguments.** Reading `Date.now()`, calling `Math.random()`, or mutating a closed-over variable makes a function impure even though its parameters never change. Time and randomness are inputs whether or not they're arguments.
- **The call stack is a stack.** Each call pushes a frame holding the parameters and locals; `return` pops it. That's why unbounded recursion overflows and why a long synchronous chain blocks everything — there's only one thread for JS.
- **Fewer parameters is a real design constraint, not a style preference.** Past about three, callers start mixing up the order, and the compiler stops helping you. An options object with named fields fixes the ordering problem and gives you a place to add the seventh thing.

**Questions**
1. What is a pure function, and why does it matter?
2. Function declaration vs expression vs arrow function?
3. What is a higher-order function? Give an example.
4. What is a callback, and what problem arises with many nested callbacks? *(callback hell → promises/async-await)*
5. What is the call stack?
6. What's the difference between parameters and arguments?
7. What is a closure, and what is it actually keeping alive?
8. Why are arrow functions wrong for object methods?

**Model answer, Q1:** "A pure function returns the same output for the same input and does nothing else — no mutation of external state, no I/O, no randomness, no reading the clock. It matters because you can test it without mocks, cache it, reason about it in isolation, and run it concurrently without coordination. The subtlety people miss is that purity covers the whole environment, not just the parameters: calling `Date.now()` inside makes a function impure, because time is an input whether or not you passed it."
**Model answer, Q7:** "A closure is a function bundled with the variables from the scope where it was defined. In `makeCounter`, the returned arrow keeps a reference to `c`, so `c` stays alive after `makeCounter` returns. That's what makes the counter work — and it's also the memory-leak mechanism, because that reference can't be collected while the function exists. Each call to `makeCounter` creates its own `c`, which is why two counters are independent."
**What they evaluate**
- **Whether they can explain closures without hand-waving** about "accessing outer variables".
- **Knowing the three differences of arrows,** especially lexical `this`.
- **Treating purity as a design tool** for testability, not a definition to recite.
**Probe:** "Why does an arrow function break an object method?" Or: "What's the difference between a parameter and an argument?"
**Common wrong answers:** a closure described as "when a function has access to a parent scope" without saying the parent stays alive; purity defined only in terms of arguments; arrow functions described as just shorter syntax; parameters and arguments used interchangeably; the call stack described as where local variables live forever.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Arrow vs `function`:** arrows are concise and lexically scoped; `function` gives you own `this`, `arguments` and `new`. Pick per use, not per file.
- **Pure vs impure:** pure functions are trivially testable and cacheable; impure ones are where all the interesting behaviour lives. Isolate the impure parts at the edges — handlers, services, the module top level.
- **Few parameters vs an options object:** positional args are terse and fine up to about three; an object is self-documenting, extensible, and safe against reordering.
**Mistakes:** arrow functions as class methods or DOM handlers; mutating a parameter and assuming the caller doesn't see it; a closure holding a large object alive forever; a "pure" function that reads the clock; relying on hoisting of a `const` arrow.
**Mini task:** Implement `makeCounter`, `makeCounter` returning `{ inc, dec, get }`, and a version that stops counting at 100 using a closure instead of a class. Then write the three test cases that distinguish "shared state" from "independent state".
**Resources:** MDN "Functions" and "Closures"; javascript.info's functions and closures chapters; *Eloquent JavaScript* chapter 9 (free).
**Self-check:** Can you explain what a closure keeps alive, name the three differences between an arrow and a function declaration, and give an example of a function that looks pure but isn't?

## 2.4.4 Recursion
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** A function that solves a problem by calling itself on a strictly smaller version of the same problem, until it reaches a case it can answer directly.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because it's the clearest test of whether you can reason about a base case, prove progress, and reason about complexity without a loop counter in sight. It's also a proxy for tree thinking — DOM traversal, JSON transforms, and file walking all look exactly like recursion.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.3 (you need the call stack to reason about it).

**Key concepts**
- **Every recursion needs:** a **base case** (stop) and a **recursive case** that progresses toward it.
- **Call stack depth:** each call uses a stack frame, so depth n costs O(n) space. Deep recursion (roughly 10k+ frames in JS) throws `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`.
- **Tail-call optimization** is in the spec, but most JS engines (V8) don't implement it, so don't rely on it.
- **Recursion ↔ iteration:** anything recursive can be iterative with an explicit stack.
- **Overlapping subproblems → memoization** (the bridge to dynamic programming).
- **Use cases:** trees, graphs (DFS), divide and conquer, backtracking, nested structures (JSON, DOM, file systems).
- **Three questions validate any recursion:** is there a base case, does every recursive call move toward it, and does the result combine correctly from what the recursive call returned. If you can't answer all three, the code isn't ready.
- **Naive Fibonacci is exponential because the subproblems overlap.** `fib(n-1)` and `fib(n-2)` both compute `fib(n-2)`, so the work doubles at every level — roughly 2ⁿ calls. Memoizing collapses that to O(n), which is the moment recursion stops being a toy and becomes dynamic programming.
- **Space cost is the depth, not the input.** Recursive factorial is O(n) time and O(n) space; the O(n) space is entirely the stack of pending frames. That's why "convert it to a loop" is sometimes a memory fix rather than a style fix.
- **Some structures aren't trees.** A graph can contain a cycle, and a naive recursive walk will loop forever. Recursion over a graph needs a visited set — which is exactly the bug a cycle-detection interview question is fishing for.

```js
const factorial = n => (n <= 1 ? 1 : n * factorial(n - 1));

// Naive Fibonacci: O(2^n) time
const fib = n => (n < 2 ? n : fib(n - 1) + fib(n - 2));

// Memoized: O(n) time, O(n) space
const fibMemo = (n, memo = new Map()) => {
  if (n < 2) return n;
  if (memo.has(n)) return memo.get(n);
  const v = fibMemo(n - 1, memo) + fibMemo(n - 2, memo);
  memo.set(n, v);
  return v;
};

// Practical: flatten nested arrays
const flatten = arr => arr.reduce(
  (acc, x) => acc.concat(Array.isArray(x) ? flatten(x) : x), []);

// Practical: walk a nested object
const countKeys = obj => Object.values(obj).reduce(
  (n, v) => n + 1 + (v && typeof v === 'object' ? countKeys(v) : 0), 0);
```

**Questions**
1. What are the two required parts of a recursive function?
2. Why is naive Fibonacci slow? How do you fix it? *(exponential from recomputing subproblems → memoization or iteration.)*
3. What causes a stack overflow, and how do you avoid it?
4. When would you choose iteration over recursion?
5. Write a function to deep-clone, flatten, or traverse a nested structure recursively.
6. What's the time and space complexity of recursive factorial? *(O(n) time, O(n) space for the stack)*
7. How do you prevent infinite recursion on a structure that contains a cycle?
8. When does recursion genuinely beat iteration?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Because the subproblems overlap. `fib(n)` calls `fib(n-1)` and `fib(n-2)`, and both of those recompute `fib(n-2)` from scratch, so the number of calls roughly doubles at each level — that's 2ⁿ, which is why `fib(40)` takes seconds. Two fixes: memoize, which computes each value once and brings it to O(n) time and O(n) space; or convert to iteration, an explicit loop or array of values, which is O(n) time and O(1) extra space if you only need the last two. I'd memoize if I need the whole table and iterate if I just need the final value."
**Model answer, Q7:** "The recursion needs a visited set. I keep a `WeakSet` of objects I've already walked, and at the top of each call I check whether the current node is in it — if so, return early. That handles both cycles and the same object appearing twice in a tree-like structure, which is common once you're walking a data graph rather than a strict tree. I use `WeakSet` rather than `Set` so the tracking doesn't itself prevent garbage collection."
**What they evaluate**
- **Base case and progress,** named explicitly before any code.
- **Space complexity including the stack** — a very common omission.
- **Whether they distinguish trees from graphs,** which is where cycle detection comes from.
**Probe:** "Your recursive function works on 1000 items and throws on 100,000. Why, and what do you change?" Or: "How do you detect a cycle in a linked list recursively?"
**Common wrong answers:** a recursive case that doesn't shrink; claiming recursive factorial is O(1) space; "it overflows because of infinite recursion" with no mention of depth; believing tail-call optimization saves you in Node; exponential Fibonacci described as "slow because it's recursive".
**Trade-offs:**
- **Recursion vs iteration:** recursion states the shape of the problem directly and is shorter for trees; iteration avoids stack limits, is often faster, and needs an explicit stack for the same traversal.
- **Memoization vs a precomputed table vs iteration:** memoization is the least code and O(n) space; iteration can be O(1) space; a table is fastest when you need many queries.
- **Naive vs optimized:** the naive version is clearer and is often the right thing to write first, since it makes the overlapping-subproblem structure visible.
