**Mistakes:** forgetting the base case for one input shape; mutating a shared accumulator so results depend on call order; returning an array from every recursive call and concatenating, which is O(n²); recursion over data that isn't a tree.
**Mini task:** Write `flatten`, `deepClone` (handling Date and arrays) and `countLeaves`, each twice — once recursive, once iterative with an explicit stack. Then generate a 200,000-element nested array and compare time and memory. That is the day you learn which one to reach for.
**Resources:** MDN "Recursion" and "RangeError"; javascript.info's recursion chapter; *Eloquent JavaScript* chapter 11.
**Self-check:** Can you state the three questions that validate any recursion, give the space complexity of recursive factorial, and explain in one sentence why naive Fibonacci is exponential?

## 2.4.5 Object-Oriented Programming Basics
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** Modelling a system as objects that bundle state with the operations allowed on that state, organised around encapsulation, abstraction, inheritance and polymorphism.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because the four pillars are easy to recite and hard to apply, and the follow-up — "when would you *not* use inheritance?" — is where the real understanding shows. It also connects to the design questions in Phase 11, so it's worth more than its difficulty rating suggests.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.3.

**Key concepts**
- **Four pillars:** **encapsulation** (hide internal state behind an interface), **abstraction** (expose only what's necessary), **inheritance** (reuse via an "is-a" relationship), **polymorphism** (same interface, different behavior).
- **Class** = blueprint, **object/instance** = concrete thing. **Constructor**, **methods**, **properties**, **static** members, **private fields** (`#field`).
- **JS classes are syntactic sugar over prototypes** (Phase 3).
- **Composition over inheritance:** prefer "has-a" and small composable pieces. Deep inheritance trees get brittle.
- **SOLID** preview (Phase 11).

```js
class Account {
  #balance = 0;                      // private field: encapsulation
  deposit(n) {
    if (n <= 0) throw new RangeError('Deposit must be positive');
    this.#balance += n;
  }
  get balance() { return this.#balance; }
}
class SavingsAccount extends Account {   // inheritance
  addInterest(rate) { this.deposit(this.balance * rate); }
}
```
**Polymorphism example:** `shapes.map(s => s.area())`, where each shape class implements `area()` differently.

- **Encapsulation exists to protect invariants, not to make things private for its own sake.** `#balance` matters because it guarantees the balance can never go negative — the only way to change it is through `deposit` and `withdraw`, which enforce the rule. A public field with a comment saying "don't set this" enforces nothing.
- **Abstraction is what the caller doesn't have to know.** They call `account.deposit(100)` without caring whether the balance is a number, stored in a ledger table, or requires a database transaction.
- **Polymorphism is what lets you write the loop once.** `shapes.map(s => s.area())` works because every shape answers to `area()`. Adding a `Triangle` requires no change to the calling code — that's the actual payoff, and it's the reason to reach for an interface rather than a chain of `instanceof` checks.
- **Inheritance couples you to the base class's future.** A subclass depends on its parent's internals, so a change deep in the hierarchy can break a subclass that was written years ago. Composition replaces that coupling with an explicit "has-a" relationship you can see in one line.
- **The `instanceof` chain is the smell.** `if (x instanceof A) ... else if (x instanceof B)` means the caller knows every implementation, which is exactly what polymorphism is supposed to prevent.
- **`#field` is enforced, `_field` is a convention.** Only the `#` syntax is actually private; an underscore is a naming promise that any caller can break.

**Questions**
1. Explain the four pillars of OOP with examples.
2. Inheritance vs composition? When do you prefer composition?
3. What's the difference between a class and an object?
4. What is encapsulation useful for in practice? *(protecting invariants, so the balance can't go negative from outside code)*
5. Abstract class vs interface? *(In TS: interfaces describe shape and have no implementation. Abstract classes can share partial implementation.)*
6. What problem does polymorphism actually solve?
7. Why is inheritance riskier than composition?
8. When is OOP the wrong tool?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Encapsulation is hiding state behind an interface so the object can guarantee its own invariants — the `Account` example only lets you change the balance through `deposit`, which is why it can never go negative. Abstraction is exposing the minimum someone needs to use it without knowing how it works. Inheritance reuses an implementation across an is-a relationship. Polymorphism is many types answering to the same interface, so `shapes.map(s => s.area())` works without knowing which shape it is — that's the one that lets you add a new type without touching the calling code."
**Model answer, Q2:** "Inheritance is is-a and couples the child to the parent's internals — change the parent and you can break a subclass written two years ago. Composition is has-a and makes the relationship visible in one line at the point of use. So I reach for composition by default and reserve inheritance for genuine subtype relationships where the child really can stand in for the parent everywhere. If I find a hierarchy more than two or three levels deep, that's usually a signal I wanted composition."
**What they evaluate**
- **Whether the pillars are demonstrated, not defined,** with concrete code consequences.
- **Naming the risk of inheritance** rather than reciting "prefer composition".
- **Understanding polymorphism as decoupling,** which is its real purpose.
**Probe:** "Where would you put the invariant that an order can't be cancelled after shipping?" Or: "Your class hierarchy is four levels deep. What's wrong?"
**Common wrong answers:** the pillars defined without examples; "inheritance is for code reuse" as the primary reason; composition described as "more modern"; polymorphism explained as "overriding" only; encapsulation as "using private keywords".
**Trade-offs:**
- **Inheritance vs composition:** inheritance gives terse syntax and a clear is-a, at the cost of tight coupling; composition requires wiring but keeps the parts replaceable.
- **Mutable vs immutable domain objects:** mutable objects are efficient and easy to change internally; immutable ones are safe to share and to pass across threads of reasoning, at the cost of re-allocating.
- **Deep hierarchies vs flat composition:** hierarchies scale the definition of a type; flat composition scales the number of collaborators you have to pass around.
**Mistakes:** inheritance for code reuse alone; public fields with "don't touch" comments; a long `instanceof` chain; a base class that imports everything; treating a constructor as the place for I/O instead of for initialising state.
**Mini task:** Refactor a three-level hierarchy into composition without changing behaviour, then add a new type that the existing code handles without modification. That second part is the proof polymorphism was working.
**Resources:** MDN "Classes" and "Private properties"; *Eloquent JavaScript* chapter 15 (free); the SOLID articles in the Phase 11 material.
**Self-check:** Can you define each pillar with a code example, say what breaks in a deep hierarchy, and explain why polymorphism reduces the amount of code that changes when you add a type?

## 2.4.6 Functional Programming Basics
🟢 Beginner → 🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** Building behaviour from small pure functions composed together, with data transformed rather than mutated in place.
**Why interviewers ask:** Not to check whether you can write `.reduce`, but because immutability is the mechanism behind React's re-rendering and behind state you can reason about. A candidate who understands *why* reference equality is load-bearing in React has understood something structural.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.3.

**Key concepts**
- **Pure functions, immutability, first-class and higher-order functions, composition, declarative style.**
- **`map` / `filter` / `reduce`:**
```js
const orders = [{ total: 20, paid: true }, { total: 50, paid: false }, { total: 30, paid: true }];
const revenue = orders.filter(o => o.paid).map(o => o.total).reduce((a, b) => a + b, 0); // 50
```
- **Immutability:** create new values instead of mutating (`{...obj, a: 1}`, `[...arr, x]`, `arr.toSorted()`). It matters in React and Redux, which detect changes by reference.
- **Composition:** `const pipe = (...fns) => x => fns.reduce((v, f) => f(v), x);`
- **Currying / partial application:** `const add = a => b => a + b;`
- **Imperative vs declarative:** *how* vs *what*.
- **Trade-offs:** FP gives predictability and testability. Immutability has a copying cost (mitigated by structural sharing in libraries like Immer).
- **`reduce` is the general one and the other two are sugar.** `map` is `reduce` building an array, `filter` is `reduce` building a subset. Once you can read a `reduce`, the other methods stop being things to memorise.
- **The real cost of immutability is copying, and the real risk is forgetting.** `arr.sort()` and `arr.push()` mutate the array you were given — including React props and Redux state — so the non-mutating `toSorted`, `toReversed`, `with` and spread exist. The cost is a new allocation per change, which is why libraries use structural sharing: unchanged parts keep their identity and only the path to the change is copied.
- **Currying is partial application with a fixed shape.** It buys you composability — `pipe(add(1), double)` reads left to right — at the cost of a call stack of tiny functions and a stack trace full of anonymous arrows. It's a tool for building pipelines, not a house style.
- **Declarative doesn't mean slower, and imperative doesn't mean clearer.** `filter(...).map(...).reduce(...)` states the transformation; a three-line loop states the steps. The first scales better in review because it separates *what* from *how*.

**OOP vs FP, a balanced answer:** "They're tools, not religions. I use OOP-style encapsulation for entities with state and invariants, such as services and domain objects, and functional style for data transformation, with pure functions and immutability. In JS and React the two mix naturally."

**Questions**
1. What's a pure function and why prefer them?
2. `map` vs `forEach`? *(`map` returns a new array, `forEach` returns undefined and is for side effects.)*
3. What does `reduce` do? Implement `map` with `reduce`.
4. What is immutability and why does React care?
5. What's function composition? Currying?
6. Mutating vs non-mutating methods: `sort`, `reverse`, `splice` vs `toSorted`, `toReversed`, `slice`? *(`sort`, `reverse`, `splice`, `push`, `pop` mutate.)*
7. OOP or functional — which do you use, and why?
8. What does structural sharing have to do with immutability?

**Model answer, Q3:** "`reduce` folds a collection into a single value — an accumulator and the current item, returning whatever the final accumulator is. The accumulator's type is whatever you choose, which is why the same function can sum, group or build. `map` with `reduce` is: `reduce((acc, x) => { acc.push(x * 2); return acc; }, [])`. Note the empty array as the initial value — without it you have nothing to push into, which is the classic bug."
**Model answer, Q4:** "Immutability means you create a new value instead of changing one in place. React cares because it detects changes by reference — `Object.is(prevState, nextState)`. If you mutate an object in place, the reference is the same, so React concludes nothing changed and skips the re-render. That's why the rule is update immutably: set state to a new object or array. Redux has the same constraint, which is why it uses Immer to let you write mutations and get immutable results."
**What they evaluate**
- **`reduce` as the general case,** not three unrelated methods.
- **Understanding reference equality in React,** which is the real application.
- **A balanced OOP-vs-FP answer** rather than a religion.
**Probe:** "You call `props.items.sort()` inside a component. What breaks and why?" Or: "When is immutability the wrong choice?"
**Common wrong answers:** `reduce` described as "summing an array"; mutation as inherently un-FP with no mention of cost; `map` and `forEach` treated as interchangeable; `sort()` believed to be non-mutating; "functional means no classes".
**Trade-offs:**
- **Immutability vs mutation:** safe sharing, cheap comparison and time-travel debugging, against per-change allocation and more verbose updates.
- **`reduce` vs `map`/`filter`:** `reduce` is general and composable; the named methods are clearer and marginally faster. Readability usually wins until it's in a hot loop.
- **Currying vs plain arguments:** excellent for point-free pipelines, worse for debugging and for anyone reading the stack trace.
- **Declarative vs imperative:** declarative separates intent from mechanism and reviews well; imperative gives you exact control over allocation and short-circuits in performance-sensitive code.
**Mistakes:** mutating props or Redux state in place; `sort`/`reverse` on data you don't own; `reduce` with no initial value on an empty array; treating immutability as free; a `reduce` that does four unrelated things.
**Mini task:** Implement `map`, `filter` and `groupBy` using only `reduce`. Then take a component that mutates state and rewrite it immutably, and confirm with React DevTools that the re-render now happens.
**Resources:** javascript.info's "Array methods" and "Functional programming" chapters; MDN's `Array.prototype.reduce` page; Immer's documentation on structural sharing.
**Self-check:** Can you implement `map` with `reduce` from memory, explain in one sentence why React needs immutable state updates, and say when mutation is the better choice?

## 2.4.7 Error Handling
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Deciding what can fail, classifying the failure, propagating it with enough context to be diagnosable, and showing the user something that isn't an internal secret.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because a `try/catch` that swallows everything looks identical to one that handles everything, and only one of them is shippable. The operational-versus-programmer-error distinction is the one interviewers probe, because it decides whether you log-and-continue or log-and-crash.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.3, 2.4.5.

**Key concepts**
- **Error types:** syntax errors (can't parse), runtime errors (thrown while running), logic errors (wrong result, no exception, the hardest).
- **Operational vs programmer errors:** operational ones are expected (network down, invalid input, file missing) and handled gracefully. Programmer ones are bugs (`undefined is not a function`) and are fixed, not caught and ignored.
- **`try/catch/finally`**, `throw`, custom error classes, and error `cause`:
```js
class ValidationError extends Error {
  constructor(message, field) { super(message); this.name = 'ValidationError'; this.field = field; }
}
try {
  await save(user);
} catch (err) {
  if (err instanceof ValidationError) return res.status(422).json({ field: err.field, error: err.message });
  throw new Error('Failed to save user', { cause: err }); // preserve context
} finally {
  await conn.release();          // always runs
}
```
- **Async errors:** `try/catch` works with `await`. Un-awaited promises produce **unhandled rejections**, which crash modern Node by default.
- **Principles:** fail fast; don't swallow errors (`catch {}`); don't use exceptions for normal control flow; log with context; return meaningful errors to users without leaking internals (no stack traces to clients); validate at boundaries.
- **Alternatives:** result types (`{ok, value, error}`), error codes.
- **`finally` runs on every exit path** — normal return, thrown error, or `return` from inside the `try`. That makes it the only safe place for cleanup, which is why connection release and temp-file deletion belong there rather than at the end of the function.
- **Wrapping with `cause` preserves the chain.** Rethrowing a new `Error('Failed to save user', { cause: err })` keeps the original as the cause, so the stack and the message both survive. `throw new Error(err.message)` destroys it, and so does logging the new error and dropping the old one.
- **An empty `catch {}` is worse than no `catch` at all,** because it converts a loud failure into a silent wrong answer. If you must swallow something, say why in a comment and record a metric — an unexplained swallow is indistinguishable from a bug.
- **The boundary is where error handling belongs.** Validate untrusted input once, at the edge, and then trust internal code. Validating the same field in twelve places is how you end up with twelve different error shapes.
- **Never leak internals to the client.** A stack trace or a SQL error in a response tells an attacker your framework, your table names and your file paths. Send a safe message and a correlation id; keep the detail in the logs.

**Questions**
1. `try/catch/finally`: when does `finally` run? *(Always, even after a `return` or throw.)*
2. How do you handle errors in async code?
3. What are operational vs programmer errors, and how should each be handled?
4. Why shouldn't you swallow errors?
5. What do you show a user, and what do you log?
6. Why create custom error classes?
7. What happens with an unhandled promise rejection in Node?
8. When is a result type better than an exception?

**Model answer, Q3:** "An operational error is an expected failure of the environment — the database is down, the input is invalid, the file isn't there. It's part of normal operation, so you handle it: log it with context, return a sensible response, maybe retry if it's transient. A programmer error is a bug — `undefined is not a function`, a null dereference. You don't handle it gracefully, you let it crash loudly so it gets fixed, because swallowing it turns a clear bug into a silent wrong result that's much harder to find later. The line is roughly: would this still be a bug if everything around it were correct?"
**Model answer, Q5:** "The user gets something safe and actionable — a field-level validation message, or a generic failure with a support or correlation id. The server log gets everything: the full message, the stack, the `cause` chain, the request or job id, and the input that caused it. The rule is that nothing in the response should tell an attacker about your internals — no stack traces, no SQL, no file paths — and nothing in the logs should contain a credential."
**What they evaluate**
- **The operational/programmer distinction,** and what follows from it for each.
- **Whether they know `finally` runs on every exit,** which is the cleanup question.
- **Restraint:** not swallowing, not leaking.
**Probe:** "Your `catch` block logs and returns null. What's the actual cost of that?" Or: "How do you decide whether to retry an error?"
**Common wrong answers:** empty `catch {}` as "handling"; stack traces in API responses; distinguishing the error types by whether they're in a try block; assuming `try/catch` catches async errors without `await`; wrapping with `new Error(err.message)` and losing the original.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Exceptions vs result types:** exceptions are compact and force you to think about failure, but they're invisible in the type signature and easy to forget. Result types are explicit and compose well, at the cost of nesting and verbosity.
- **Retry vs fail fast:** retrying handles transient failures like a blip in the network, at the risk of amplifying load and duplicating side effects. Only retry what is idempotent.
- **Detailed error objects vs plain strings:** structured errors are inspectable and safe to log, but need discipline so nothing sensitive leaks into them.
**Mistakes:** swallowing errors; returning stack traces to clients; logging secrets in the error path; `throw` inside a `catch` without `cause`; using exceptions for expected validation on hot paths; not handling `finally` for resource release.
**Mini task:** Build a small module with a typed error hierarchy, a `wrap()` helper that preserves `cause`, and a top-level boundary handler that returns safe responses and logs full detail with a request id. Then throw a secret inside the deepest call and prove the response doesn't contain it while the log does.
**Resources:** MDN "Control flow and error handling" and `Error.cause`; the Node docs on unhandled rejections; *Eloquent JavaScript* chapter 11 (errors and testing).
**Self-check:** Can you state the operational/programmer test in one sentence, say when `finally` runs, and explain why wrapping with `cause` beats wrapping with a message?

## 2.4.8 Reading Documentation and the Debugging Mindset
🟡 Intermediate → 🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · the highest-value topic in this group

**Definition:** Two professional habits — finding an answer in documentation you haven't read before, and narrowing an unknown fault by hypothesis rather than by guess.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because there is no way to test what you know by asking about something you could look up. So interviewers use this topic to test process: they'll give you a bug they know you can't Google and watch how you reduce it. Almost every strong backend answer you'll give in Phase 7 is an application of this section.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.7, 2.5.4.

**Symptom**
"I have no idea why this is happening." The first job is not to fix it — it's to convert an unknown into a question you can answer.

**Key concepts**
**Reading docs**
- Start with the *overview/concepts*, then *API reference*, then *examples*. Check the **version** of the docs against your installed version.
- Read changelogs and migration guides, and read error messages fully, because they usually name the cause.
- Prefer official docs, then source code and tests, then reputable articles, then Q&A sites (verify the date).

**The scientific debugging method**
1. **Reproduce** reliably, with a minimal case.
2. **Observe:** read the full error and stack trace (the *first* line and the first frame in your code matter most).
3. **Hypothesize** one cause at a time.
4. **Test** the hypothesis (log, breakpoint, assertion), changing one thing at a time.
5. **Fix the root cause**, not the symptom.
6. **Verify and prevent:** add a regression test.
- **Techniques:** rubber-duck debugging, binary search (comment out half), `git bisect`, diffing against last known good, reading the actual data rather than assuming it, checking the network tab and logs.
- **Bug types to recognize:** off-by-one, null/undefined access, async ordering and race conditions, stale state or closures, mutation of shared state, type coercion, timezone and encoding issues, environment differences ("works on my machine").

- **The version check is the whole first step.** Most "the docs are wrong" is actually "the docs are for 4.x and I'm on 5.x", and the fastest fix in the world is useless if you're reading the wrong page.
- **An error message is data, not decoration.** It usually contains the operation, the resource and sometimes the cause. People scroll past the only sentence that was trying to help them.
- **One hypothesis at a time is what makes debugging converge.** Change five things, the bug goes away, and you have learned nothing about any of them — and you can't undo the four changes that were innocent.
- **Reproducing is half the work.** A bug you can trigger on demand can be bisected, unit-tested and proven fixed. A bug you can only observe once in production cannot be verified at all.
- **Look at the data, don't imagine it.** The overwhelming majority of "impossible" bugs are a value that isn't what the code assumed: `null` where you expected an array, a string `"false"`, a timestamp in the wrong zone, an id that came back as a number on one call and a string on another.
- **Fix the cause and add a test.** A fix without a regression test is a coincidence you expect to happen again, and "we fixed that last month" is a phrase every team recognises painfully.

**Questions**
1. Walk me through how you debug a bug you can't reproduce locally.
2. A function sometimes returns the wrong result. First steps?
3. How do you approach an unfamiliar codebase?
4. How do you use documentation efficiently, and how do you know it's the right version?
5. Why one hypothesis at a time?
6. A bug appears only in production. What do you do first?
7. You've been stuck for two hours. When do you ask for help, and what do you send?
8. What's the difference between fixing a symptom and fixing a cause?

**Model answer, Q1:** "First I'd resist the urge to change code and instead gather information: pull the logs for the failing requests, note the versions and configuration that were running, and find out what's different about the failing users or inputs. Then I'd compare configs between environments, since a missing or stale variable is the usual culprit. I'd add targeted logging at the boundary — the actual request payload and response — to see the real data rather than what I assume it is. Then I'd try to reproduce with production-like data, and if the blast radius is small, use a feature flag or a canary to isolate whether it's code or data. If I can't reproduce it, I'd add instrumentation that will catch it next time rather than shipping a hopeful fix."
**Model answer, Q7:** "After about two hours of genuine progress — hypotheses tested, evidence gathered — I'd ask, because being stuck alone for hours is itself a smell. What I send matters as much as asking: what I expected, what actually happened, the exact error, what I've already ruled out, and the smallest reproduction I have. That turns a vague 'anyone seen this?' into something a colleague can answer in a minute, and it usually makes me see the answer while writing it."
**What they evaluate**
- **Process, not answers.** They are grading how you reduce uncertainty.
- **Information before change** — logs, versions, config, actual data.
- **Knowing when to escalate,** and what to escalate with.
**Probe:** "The bug happens once a week for one user. Walk me through it." Or: "You fixed it and it came back. What did you miss?"
**Common wrong answers:** changing code before reproducing; reading the docs for the wrong major version; restarting the service as a first step; asking for help with no evidence attached; fixing the symptom by adding a null check at the call site; no regression test.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Instrumentation vs shipping quickly:** logging and tracing cost time, storage and some noise, and are the only way to catch a bug you can't reproduce.
- **Reproduce locally vs reason from logs:** a local repro enables bisect and a real regression test; reasoning from logs is sometimes all you have, and is slower and less certain.
- **Asking early vs asking late:** asking early spreads the cost and can feel premature if the answer is one search away; asking late wastes hours and hides that you were stuck.
**Root cause:** the first link in the causal chain you can actually change — not the crash, and not the line that dereferenced null, but the reason the value was null.
**Investigation:** read the whole error; find the first frame in your own code on the stack; log the actual inputs and outputs at the boundary, not the derived values; reproduce with a minimal case; bisect the input space or the commit range; diff against the last known-good state.
**Fix:** change the thing that was wrong, then add a regression test that fails without the fix. If you genuinely can't reproduce it, the honest fix is instrumentation plus a monitoring alert, and saying so out loud.
**Prevention:** a regression test, an assertion at the boundary that would have caught it, a stricter type or schema validation, an alert on the symptom, and a note in the runbook if it's a known environmental trap.
**Tools:** the browser and server debuggers, structured logs with request ids, `git bisect run`, diffing against last known good, and a feature flag or canary when you need to isolate without redeploying.
**Mistakes:** guessing fixes; five changes at once; reading the wrong docs version; skipping the actual data; asking for help with no reproduction; fixing without a test; treating a restart as a fix.
**Mini task:** Take a real bug you've had and write the six-step method as you actually did it, then note which steps you skipped. Do the same for a bug you fixed under time pressure — the skipped steps are the ones that will cost you later.
**Resources:** MDN's debugging guide and the DevTools docs; *The Pragmatic Programmer* on orthogonal debugging; the "rubber duck" and `git bisect` documentation; your framework's troubleshooting guides.
**Self-check:** Can you describe your debugging process end to end without naming a single tool, and say what you'd do first on a bug that only one user sees once a week?

## 2.4.9 OOP basics
🟢 Beginner · **Must-know** · classes, objects, `this`, and the prototype chain, in the language you'll be asked in

**Definition:** Object-oriented programming in practical terms: bundling data and the functions that operate on it into objects, using constructors to create them, and relying on a prototype chain for shared behaviour and inheritance.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because it's the first place a candidate's programming fundamentals show as *applied* knowledge rather than vocabulary. 2.4.5 covers the pillars as design principles; this covers the mechanics — what `new` actually does, where methods live, why `this` changes, and how `class` relates to prototypes. Interviewers ask it precisely because most candidates can recite encapsulation but can't explain why `this` is `undefined` in a callback, and that gap is where they learn what you actually know.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.1, 2.4.3.

**Key concepts**
- **An object is a key-value bag.** Its keys can be properties (data) or methods (functions), and there's no meaningful difference at runtime — `obj.method` is just a lookup that happens to return a function.
- **`new` does four things:** create an empty object, set its prototype to the constructor's `.prototype`, call the constructor with `this` bound to the new object, and return it (unless the constructor returns an object, in which case that wins).
- **The prototype chain is how inheritance works in JS.** Every object has a hidden link to another object, and property lookup walks the chain until it hits `null`.
```js
function Animal(name) { this.name = name; }        // constructor function
Animal.prototype.speak = function () {             // shared method, one copy
  return `${this.name} makes a noise`;
};
const dog = new Animal('Rex');
dog.speak();                 // "Rex makes a noise"
dog instanceof Animal;       // true — checks the prototype chain
Object.getPrototypeOf(dog) === Animal.prototype;   // true
```
- **`class` is syntax over that same machinery.** A method defined in a class body goes on the prototype, so instances share it rather than each getting a copy.
- **`this` is not the object — it's determined by *how the function is called*.** `obj.method()` binds `this` to `obj`; a bare `method()` gives `undefined` in strict mode (which modules are, by default).
```js
const counter = {
  n: 0,
  bump() { return ++this.n; },
};
setTimeout(counter.bump, 1000);   // `this` is undefined -> TypeError
setTimeout(() => counter.bump(), 1000);  // arrow inherits `this` -> works
const { bump } = counter;
bump();                          // detached, so `this` is undefined
```
- **Arrow functions have no own `this`** — they close over the enclosing one. That makes them right for callbacks and wrong for methods that need dynamic `this`.
- **`extends` and `super()`:** `super()` must be called before using `this` in a derived constructor, and `super.method()` walks up the chain rather than recursing.
- **Static members live on the class, not instances,** which is where factory functions belong — `static fromJson()` next to the class instead of a `fromJson` special case every caller has to remember.
- **Private fields (`#name`) are enforced at the language level,** unlike the `_name` convention, which is only a promise any caller can break (2.4.5).
- **Plain objects vs class instances:** object literals with methods cover most application code. Reach for a class when you need shared behaviour, invariants enforced by the type, or a real identity concept.

**Questions**
1. What's the difference between a class and an object?
2. What does `new` do?
3. What is the prototype chain?
4. What is `this` bound to, and how is it determined?
5. Why does `this` come out `undefined` in a callback, and how do you fix it?
6. When would you not use a class?
7. What's the difference between a static method and an instance method?
8. How does `class` syntax relate to prototypes?

**Model answer, Q2:** "`new` creates a fresh object whose hidden prototype link points to the constructor's `.prototype`, calls the constructor with `this` set to that new object, and returns it. So `new` is what makes `this` inside the constructor refer to the instance rather than being `undefined`."
**Model answer, Q3:** "Every object has a hidden reference to another object, its prototype. Looking up a property walks that chain until it finds a match or hits `null`. So an instance can use a method defined on its class's prototype, and that prototype can itself have one — that's JavaScript's inheritance. `instanceof` just checks whether the constructor's prototype appears anywhere in that chain."
**Model answer, Q4:** "`this` isn't the object; it's decided by the call site. `obj.method()` sets `this` to `obj`. Calling it bare, or passing it as a callback, loses that reference, and in strict mode — which modules are — `this` becomes `undefined` rather than the global object. Arrow functions are the exception, since they capture the enclosing `this` instead of receiving one."
**Model answer, Q5:** "Because passing a function as a callback detaches it from the object it was defined on, so there's no receiver to bind. I fix it by wrapping it — `setTimeout(() => obj.method(), 1000)` — since the arrow captures `this` from the enclosing scope. Alternatively I bind explicitly with `obj.method.bind(obj)`, though the wrapper is usually clearer."
**Model answer, Q6:** "When the type is just data. For DTOs, API responses, config objects and most React props, an object literal is simpler and has no constructor cost or `this` to reason about. I also avoid classes when the objects are immutable, when I need a literal shape to compare or use as a TypeScript type, or when the behaviour is a single function — those are better as plain functions. Classes pay off when there's real shared behaviour or an invariant worth enforcing."
**Model answer, Q8:** "`class` is syntax layered on the same mechanism. A constructor is still a function, and methods declared in the class body are still assigned to the constructor's prototype, so instances share one copy rather than each getting their own. What `class` adds is stricter mode semantics, real private fields, and a readable syntax — the underlying lookup is identical."

**What they evaluate**
- **Can they explain `this` from the call site rather than saying "it's the object",** which is the single highest-signal answer here.
- **Awareness that method definitions live on the prototype,** which connects directly to 2.6.2's memory discussion.
- **Restraint** — a good answer names several cases where a plain object is the right call.
**Probe:** "This works until I pass it to `array.map`. Why?" (Expect: `map` invokes the callback with no receiver, so `this` is `undefined`; the fix is an arrow wrapper.)
**Common wrong answers:** "a class is a blueprint JavaScript enforces at runtime"; "`this` is always the object the method was defined on"; "arrow functions and regular functions are interchangeable"; "inheritance in JS uses `extends` only" (ignoring the prototype chain underneath); `static` described as "frozen" or "shared across all files".
**Trade-offs:**
- **Classes vs object literals:** classes give shared behaviour, real privacy and a place for invariants, at the cost of a constructor step and `this` complexity; literals are the fastest thing to write and the easiest to compare and spread.
- **Prototype methods vs own properties:** prototype methods are shared and cheap, but they're mutable for *every* instance — patching `Animal.prototype.speak` changes all of them; defining a method per instance costs memory and hides shared behaviour.
- **Private fields (`#x`) vs closure state:** `#x` is enforced and inspectable in devtools, but needs the class syntax; a closure over a variable hides state completely and works with plain functions.
**Mistakes:** Relying on `this` inside a callback; mutating a shared object because two instances received the same reference; forgetting `super()` before `this` in a derived constructor; defining methods as arrow-function class fields, which binds `this` once at construction and breaks when the method is passed around; comparing objects with `===`, since two structurally identical objects are different references.
**Mini task:** Write a `Counter` class with `increment`, `decrement` and `value`, then break it three ways — pass `increment` to `setTimeout`, destructure it, and subclass it without calling `super()` — and explain each error before fixing it. Finish by adding a `static from()` factory.
**Resources:** MDN "Using classes" and "Object-oriented programming"; *Eloquent JavaScript* chapter 9 (free); the V8 blog post on how hidden classes and prototypes affect performance; your language's docs on `this` binding.
**Self-check:** Can you say what `new` does in one sentence, explain why `this` breaks in a callback, and name two cases where a plain object beats a class?

## 2.4.10 Debugging mindset
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know** · the one topic where you are judged on how you think, not what you memorised

**Definition:** The disciplined, repeatable approach to finding the cause of a defect: reproduce, isolate, hypothesise, test one variable at a time, fix the cause, and verify it can't come back.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because every other topic in this phase can be faked for an hour. Debugging mindset cannot — they hand you a broken snippet and watch. What they're measuring is whether you form a theory and test it, or start changing things and hope. Candidates who jump straight to code edits, blame a library, or reinstall dependencies look experienced and aren't; the one who says "let me reproduce it first, then narrow where the data stops being correct" is the one they want. This is also the most predictive question for the actual job.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.7, 2.4.8.

**Key concepts**
- **Reproduce first, always.** A bug you can't trigger reliably can't be confirmed fixed, and "works on my machine" is a diagnosis failure, not an environment failure. Get a minimal reproduction before changing code.
- **Read the error, all of it.** The stack trace's top frame is where it threw, not necessarily where the bug is; the actual cause is often upstream. TypeError messages are the most informative logs most developers ever get.
```text
TypeError: Cannot read properties of undefined (reading 'map')
  at renderList (src/components/List.jsx:14:22)   <- threw here
  at processQueue (src/app.js:88:3)                <- called from here
  the real cause: fetch returned undefined, not []  <- originated here
```
- **Bisect the problem, not the code.** Narrow by halving: is it the input, the module, the environment, or the request? Change one variable at a time, because two changes at once produce two hypotheses and no evidence.
- **Read the error before adding logging,** and prefer real tools: a debugger breakpoint, `git bisect` on the commit, `--inspect`, or a focused test. Console logging in a loop teaches you less and leaves noise behind.
- **Check the boring explanations first:** wrong environment, stale build or cache, wrong branch, missing dependency, wrong config value, a typo in the string you're comparing. "It works locally" almost always means one of these.
- **Form a falsifiable hypothesis and write it down.** "The cache isn't being invalidated" is testable; "something's wrong with the cache" is not. Writing it down is what turns flailing into an experiment.
- **Isolation techniques:** reduce the input until the bug disappears, replace the suspect dependency with a stub, reproduce in a unit test, or bisect the commit range. Whichever removes the signal has narrowed the cause.
- **Fix the cause, not the symptom.** A guard clause that stops the crash while leaving bad data flowing downstream moves the bug, it doesn't remove it.
- **Ask for the logs before asking for the repro** when you can't reproduce locally — a request id, user id, timestamp window, and version is usually enough for someone else to find it in production.
- **Verify the fix, then guard against regression.** Confirm the bug is gone, confirm you didn't break the neighbouring case, and add a test that fails without the fix. A fix without a test is a coincidence until proven otherwise.
- **Know when to escalate.** If you've isolated it to the boundary you don't own — a vendor API, a runtime bug, a corrupt dependency — a minimal reproduction plus a clear hypothesis is worth more than another hour.

**Questions**
1. Walk me through your debugging process.
2. You have a bug you can't reproduce. What do you do?
3. How do you decide where to look first?
4. Why is "just restart it" not a fix?
5. Tell me about a time you were wrong about the cause of a bug.
6. A user's report contradicts your local behaviour. What now?
7. How do you prevent the same bug returning?
8. When do you stop and ask for help?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Reproduce it, always — if I can't, I gather logs and versions first because I can't fix what I can't see. Then I bisect the system rather than guessing: is it the input, the module, the environment, or this one request? I set a breakpoint and walk the data to find the last point where it's correct, because a stack trace tells me where it threw, not where it broke. Once I have a falsifiable hypothesis I test exactly one change, confirm the hypothesis was right, then fix the cause rather than adding a guard. Finally I add a test that fails without the fix and check the neighbouring case."
**Model answer, Q2:** "I ask for the version, environment, exact steps, and any request or user id, plus the console or server logs. Most reports carry enough of those to reproduce. If I still can't, I try to match their environment exactly — same browser, same data volume, same network conditions — because the gap between the environments is usually the bug. What I don't do is start changing code on a theory."
**Model answer, Q5:** "Once on a payment bug I assumed a third-party webhook was rejecting our payload and spent a day on our request signing. The actual cause was a timezone conversion in our own code, which only surfaced under the merchant's local timezone — my tests all ran in UTC. What changed my approach: I now verify the *data* at each boundary rather than trusting that a third-party's error message describes our bug, and I test with realistic, varied inputs."
**Model answer, Q6:** "I'd treat the report as the source of truth and mine as a hypothesis that just failed. First I check whether they could be on a different version or a stale cached asset. Then I try to reproduce with their exact input and environment. If I still can't, I instrument: log at the boundaries and see where the data diverges from what they describe. Assuming they're wrong is almost always the wrong call — either the environment differs or there's a code path I haven't imagined."
**Model answer, Q8:** "When I've isolated the failure to a boundary I don't own and my hypothesis is verified but the fix isn't mine to make — a vendor bug, a runtime defect, a corrupted dependency. At that point a minimal reproduction plus the exact hypothesis and the evidence saves the next person days, and I loop in whoever owns it. Escalating early with good evidence isn't giving up; escalating with a vague hunch just moves the guessing to someone else."

**What they evaluate**
- **Whether they reproduce before editing,** which is the single clearest signal.
- **Hypothesis-driven rather than random change** — the phrase "one variable at a time" or "falsifiable" does a lot of work here.
- **Honesty in Q5.** A story where they were never wrong reads as a story they rehearsed; "I was wrong about X, here's what I changed" is what they want to hear.
**Probe:** "The bug only happens in production, once a week, for one user." (Expect: don't reproduce locally, gather version and logs, find the request id, look at what's unique — their data, their locale, their client version — and add observability so the next occurrence is easier.)
**Common wrong answers:** Listing tools instead of a process; "add console.log until it works"; restarting the server or clearing the cache and calling that fixed; changing several things at once and declaring victory; "I'd read the code again" as the whole method; blaming a dependency without evidence.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Debuggers vs logging:** a debugger gives exact state and control flow with no residue, but it's slower to set up on a fast-moving bug and unusable in production; logging is always available and survives deployment, but it's easy to litter and slow to reason about without stack context.
- **Fast fix vs root cause:** a one-line guard stops the incident now, but leaves the bad state in the system and usually returns in a worse form; the root-cause fix costs longer and sometimes needs a schema or API change, which is why teams need both and should say which one they're doing.
- **Reproducing locally vs instrumenting production:** local reproduction gives a fast edit-test loop, but intermittent environment bugs may take days to surface; production instrumentation finds it immediately and gives you data, but you can't iterate as freely and it costs observability work up front.
**Mistakes:** Editing code before reproducing; changing multiple things at once; debugging the wrong layer because you trusted the stack trace's top frame; not checking for a stale build, wrong branch, or missing env var; leaving a debugging console.log in; fixing the symptom with a guard; skipping the regression test; escalating with "it's broken" instead of a minimal reproduction.
**Mini task:** Take a real bug and write the full sequence you actually used, then mark the steps you skipped. Do it again for a bug you fixed under time pressure — the skipped steps are the ones that will cost you in production. Then reproduce a deliberately planted bug three ways: bad input, wrong environment, and stale build, and time each diagnosis.
**Resources:** MDN's debugging guide and the DevTools documentation; the "rubber duck" method and `git bisect` documentation; *The Pragmatic Programmer* on orthogonal debugging and hypothesis-driven work; your framework's own troubleshooting guides, which usually list the known failure modes.
**Self-check:** Can you describe your process end to end without naming a single tool, state what you'd ask for when you can't reproduce, and name one thing that makes a hypothesis falsifiable?

## DRILL: Phase 2.4 Interview Warm-up Coding Set (write these in under 5 minutes each)
**Questions**
1. Reverse a string / check palindrome
2. Count vowels or character frequency
3. FizzBuzz
4. Find max/min of an array without `Math.max`
5. Remove duplicates from an array
6. Flatten a nested array
7. Fibonacci (recursive and memoized)
8. Check for anagrams
9. Two Sum (hash map)
10. Factorial / power / sum of digits recursively

```js
const isPalindrome = s => {
  const t = s.toLowerCase().replace(/[^a-z0-9]/g, '');
  return t === [...t].reverse().join('');
};
const unique = arr => [...new Set(arr)];
const isAnagram = (a, b) => {
  if (a.length !== b.length) return false;
  const count = {};
  for (const c of a) count[c] = (count[c] || 0) + 1;
  for (const c of b) { if (!count[c]) return false; count[c]--; }
  return true;
};
```

**Mistakes (whole 2.4):** confusing mutation with reassignment; using `==`; forgetting the recursion base case; swallowing errors; mutating function arguments; writing 100-line functions.

**Mini tasks:** solve the warm-up set in JS, then re-solve each in TypeScript with types. Write a small CLI that reads a JSON file, validates it, and prints useful errors for each failure class.

**Resources:** javascript.info (Fundamentals chapters), *Eloquent JavaScript* (free), MDN JavaScript Guide, *Structure and Interpretation of Computer Programs* (bonus, for depth).

**Self-check:** Can you explain value vs reference, recursion's base case, and pure functions with code examples, in under 3 minutes each?

---

# 2.5 DEVELOPER ENVIRONMENT

**Prerequisites:** terminal basics. 🟢 → 🟡 · **Must-know**

**Why interviewers ask:** Tooling fluency is a proxy for real-world experience. Pair programming and debugging rounds expose it immediately.

## 2.5.1 VS Code and Editor Fluency
🟢 Beginner · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** Operating your editor at a speed where navigation, renaming, formatting and launching a debug session take seconds instead of interrupting your train of thought.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because they are watching you work in a pair session, and how you move through code tells them how much code you have actually read. Someone who renames a symbol with one keystroke and jumps to the definition is someone with years of codebase experience, whether or not they can recite the answer.
**Prerequisites:** none — this is the tooling you need for everything in this group.

**Key concepts**
- **Navigation beats typing.** You should almost never type a name you could jump to.
- **Shortcuts worth drilling (Cmd on Mac, Ctrl elsewhere)**
  - `Cmd+P` go to file · `Cmd+Shift+P` command palette · `Cmd+Shift+F` search project
  - `F12` go to definition · `Shift+F12` find references · `F2` rename symbol
  - `Cmd+D` select next occurrence · `Alt+Click` multi-cursor · `Alt+↑/↓` move line
  - `Cmd+/` comment · `Cmd+.` quick fix · `Ctrl+\`` toggle terminal
  - `Cmd+B` toggle sidebar · `Cmd+Shift+O` go to symbol in file
- **Multi-cursor and selection:** `Cmd+D` for repeated occurrences, `Alt+Click` for scattered ones, `Cmd+Shift+L` for every line matching the current selection. These are what make a refactor across 40 call sites a two-minute job.
- **Setup:** extensions (ESLint, Prettier, GitLens, Error Lens, language support), settings sync, format on save, workspace settings committed in `.vscode/` for team consistency, and snippets.
- **Search properly:** `Cmd+Shift+F` supports regex, which is how you find "every place that calls fetch without a timeout" instead of every occurrence of the word fetch.
- **The Problems panel and Error Lens** surface diagnostics inline, so a type error is visible while you're editing rather than in a build log ten minutes later.
- **Debugging config (`.vscode/launch.json`)** — committed so everyone on the team runs the app the same way, which removes a whole category of "works on my machine":
```json
{
  "version": "0.2.0",
  "configurations": [
    {
      "type": "node",
      "request": "launch",
      "name": "Debug API",
      "program": "${workspaceFolder}/src/server.js",
      "envFile": "${workspaceFolder}/.env"
    }
  ]
}
```
- **Integrated terminal and the JS debug terminal** mean you don't leave the editor to run the server or the tests.
- **Multi-root workspaces** let one window hold the API and the frontend — useful in the Phase 5 monorepo.
- **Profiles** let you switch between a minimal setup and a fully-loaded one.
- **Watch out for:** extension bloat slowing startup, an auto-format rule that fights the formatter, and a workspace file so personal it breaks the build for everyone else. The `.vscode/settings.json` you commit should contain team conventions, not your font size.
- **Interview tip:** in a pair session, a smooth rename-symbol, jump-to-definition, and debugger use shows more experience than any quiz answer. It also frees your attention for the problem instead of the tooling.

**Questions**
1. What are your top five editor shortcuts, and what does each replace?
2. How do you rename a symbol safely across a codebase?
3. Why commit `.vscode/` settings, and what should you never commit there?
4. What is the command palette and when is it faster than searching?
5. How do you search for something structural, like every call to a function?
6. Why is editor speed a real engineering skill and not just a convenience?
7. When would you not use your editor's tooling?
8. How do you keep a shared project config from breaking someone else's setup?

**Model answer, Q2:** "`F2`, rename symbol — the editor finds every reference through the language server, shows me the count, and edits them together. The point of the editor's version over search-and-replace is that it understands scope: it won't touch a same-named variable in a different function, and it renames references, not just the declaration. Before a large rename I make sure the code is committed so I can use `git diff` to review what changed, and I run the typecheck and tests afterwards. If the rename crosses a package boundary, the project references need to be configured or it will silently miss some call sites."
**Model answer, Q6:** "Because navigation speed determines how much of a codebase you can hold in your head. If jumping to a definition takes two seconds, you stop looking things up, and you start guessing instead — and guessing is what produces bugs. Over a day that's hundreds of small interruptions that each cost more than the keystroke. It's also the clearest signal in a pair interview: how much code has someone actually navigated suggests how much code they've actually read."
**What they evaluate**
- **Whether navigation is muscle memory** rather than known-in-theory.
- **Awareness that rename symbol is scope-aware,** not a find-and-replace.
- **Judgement about what belongs in shared config.**
**Probe:** "You want to change an API across 12 files. Walk me through it." Or: "Why does renaming sometimes fail in a monorepo?"
**Common wrong answers:** formatting and file saving mistaken for core skills; the rename-symbol shortcut unknown; committing personal settings; relying on global search and replace; assuming extensions are always the answer.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Editor automation vs manual edits:** rename symbol is safe and instant but depends on a working language server; manual edits always work but risk missing a call site.
- **Heavy extensions vs startup speed:** more tooling means more help, and every extension is code running while you type.
- **Committed workspace config vs individual freedom:** a shared config keeps the team consistent, and must be limited to conventions rather than personal preferences.
**Mistakes:** typing paths instead of jumping; a 40-call-site change done with find-and-replace; committing a workspace file that breaks others; ignoring the Problems panel; an editor so heavy that it's the bottleneck.
**Mini task:** Do a refactor entirely with keyboard only — rename a symbol, jump to every reference, extract a function, and add a logpoint — without touching the mouse. Then add a launch config and a snippet to a project and commit the `.vscode/` files.
**Resources:** VS Code's "Tips and Tricks" and "Your First Extension" docs; the integrated terminal and debugging docs; the "Editing" section of the VS Code API docs.
**Self-check:** Can you rename a cross-file symbol and verify the result, and can you explain why you'd commit `.vscode/settings.json` but not your own theme?

## 2.5.2 Package Managers (npm, pnpm, yarn)
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The tools that resolve, install, and record third-party dependencies so that everyone on a project — and every CI run — gets byte-identical code.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because every project you touch runs through one of these, and "it works on my machine" is almost always a version-resolution or lockfile problem. Interviewers also use this to see whether you think about supply-chain risk, which is a Phase 7 topic and a real job concern.
**Prerequisites:** 2.5.1.

**Key concepts**
- **`package.json`:** metadata, `scripts`, `dependencies` (needed at runtime), `devDependencies` (build and test only), `peerDependencies` (the host app must provide, common for plugins and libraries), `engines`.
- **Lockfile** (`package-lock.json`, `pnpm-lock.yaml`, `yarn.lock`): pins exact resolved versions for reproducible installs. **Commit it.**
- **`npm install` vs `npm ci`:** `install` may update the lockfile. `ci` installs exactly from it, fails if it's out of sync, and is faster and deterministic, so use it in CI.
- **Semantic versioning:** `MAJOR.MINOR.PATCH`. Major means breaking changes, minor means backwards-compatible features, patch means backwards-compatible fixes.
  - `^1.2.3` allows `>=1.2.3 <2.0.0` (minor and patch updates)
  - `~1.2.3` allows `>=1.2.3 <1.3.0` (patch only)
  - `1.2.3` exact
- **`node_modules` resolution:** Node looks in `./node_modules`, then each parent directory's `node_modules`, upward.
- **pnpm:** content-addressable global store with hard links (saves disk, faster installs) and strict, non-flat `node_modules` that prevents "phantom dependencies" (using packages you didn't declare).
- **`npx` / `pnpm dlx`:** run a package binary without installing globally.
- **Scripts:** `npm run build`, with lifecycle hooks (`prepare`, `postinstall`).
- **Workspaces:** monorepo support (npm, pnpm, yarn).
- **Security hygiene:** `npm audit`, Dependabot or Renovate, review new dependencies (maintenance, size, downloads), avoid install scripts from unknown packages, and pin versions for critical tools. Typosquatting and compromised packages are real supply-chain risks (Phase 7).
- **Transitive dependencies:** your dependencies' dependencies. A single `npm install` can pull in hundreds.

- **The lockfile is what makes a build reproducible, and it is the only reason `npm ci` is safe.** `package.json` records what ranges you *asked* for; the lockfile records what you *got*, including the full transitive tree with integrity hashes. That's why you commit it even though it's machine-generated noise in review after the first few hundred lines — it's diffable once you only look at meaningful changes.
- **`npm install` vs `npm ci` is a determinism choice, not a speed choice.** `npm install` reconciles the lockfile with `package.json`, so it may silently upgrade things. `npm ci` installs exactly the lockfile, errors if the two disagree, and is faster because it skips resolution. In CI you want the failure, because a build that resolves fresh dependencies isn't testing the code you shipped.
- **The lockfile is not a security tool.** It's a reproducibility tool. It happily pins the exact version of the compromised package you installed last Tuesday — which is why audit and automated update PRs are needed alongside it.
- **pnpm's real contribution is strictness.** A flat `node_modules` means your code can `require` a package you never declared, because it happens to be hoisted from someone else's dependency. pnpm's non-flat layout makes that fail, which is why it finds bugs that npm hides. The content-addressable store with hard links is a disk and speed win, and it's why pnpm feels much faster on a big monorepo.
- **`dependencies` vs `devDependencies` is about the production install, not importance.** A package in `devDependencies` is simply not installed by `npm ci --omit=dev` in a production image. Put a build tool, a test runner, or ESLint in `devDependencies` and you keep a 200 MB image instead of a 40 MB one. Peer dependencies are a contract: the library declares what the host must provide, which is how a React component avoids shipping two copies of React.
- **`engines` is advice, not enforcement,** unless something enforces it. `nvm`/`fnm` with a committed `.nvmrc` is what actually guarantees the right Node version for everyone.
- **Install scripts are a real attack surface.** `postinstall` runs arbitrary code from every package in the tree. That's why `-ignore-scripts` is a defensible CI posture for untrusted dependencies, and why a single unmaintained transitive package is a supply-chain incident waiting to happen.

**Questions**
1. `dependencies` vs `devDependencies` vs `peerDependencies`?
2. What does the lockfile do? Should you commit it?
3. `npm install` vs `npm ci`?
4. What does `^1.2.3` mean? How about `~1.2.3`?
5. How do you upgrade dependencies safely?
6. Why pnpm over npm?
7. How would you evaluate whether to add a new library?
8. How do you resolve a "works on my machine" dependency problem?

**Model answer, Q3:** "`npm install` reconciles `package.json` with the lockfile, so it may resolve and upgrade within your ranges — that makes it right for a developer working on a feature. `npm ci` installs exactly what the lockfile says, fails fast if `package.json` and the lockfile disagree, and skips the resolution step so it's faster. In CI I always use `npm ci`, because a build that re-resolves dependencies is no longer testing the code that was shipped — it might pull a new minor version and fail for reasons that have nothing to do with my change."
**Model answer, Q7:** "Four things. First, does the standard library or an existing dependency already do it? Second, how maintained is it — last release, open issue count, how many dependents. Third, what's the transitive and install cost, because a 'tiny' utility can pull a hundred packages and run install scripts. Fourth, security: I'll check the advisories and whether the name looks like a typosquat. Then I'll check bundle size if it's client-side. If it's a one-line function I could write in ten minutes, I usually do, because every dependency is code I didn't write and have to keep working for years."
**What they evaluate**
- **`npm ci` in CI for determinism,** with the reason.
- **Understanding that the lockfile pins the whole transitive tree,** not just your direct deps.
- **Judgement about new dependencies,** not enthusiasm for libraries.
**Probe:** "Your CI passed yesterday and fails today with no code change. Why?" Or: "Why does `npm ci` fail if you added a dependency but didn't reinstall?"
**Common wrong answers:** lockfile treated as optional or gitignored; `npm install` recommended in CI; `^` and `~` swapped or described as "latest version"; `devDependencies` described as less important; pnpm's benefit described only as speed; a dependency added with no evaluation criteria.
**Trade-offs:**
- **npm vs pnpm vs yarn:** npm ships with Node so there's nothing to install, and has the flattest `node_modules` and therefore phantom dependencies; pnpm is faster and stricter with a harder-to-reason-about layout; yarn is similar to pnpm with its own resolutions and Plug'n'Play option.
- **Lockfile determinism vs easy updates:** committing the lockfile makes builds reproducible and dependency upgrades become explicit, reviewable pull requests.
- **Many small dependencies vs writing it yourself:** a maintained library beats your weekend implementation, and every one is code you'll be responsible for indirectly.
- **`engines` documentation vs enforcement:** the field is a courtesy unless a tool or `.nvmrc` actually enforces it.
**Mistakes:** gitignoring the lockfile; `npm install` in CI; global installs for project tools; carets on critical tools; `--force` or `--legacy-peer-deps` as a habit; ignoring audit warnings indefinitely; committing `node_modules`; assuming `package.json` alone pins anything.
**Mini task:** Take an existing project and make it reproducible end to end: commit the lockfile, add `.nvmrc` plus `engines`, switch the CI workflow to `npm ci`, and add Renovate for update PRs. Then delete `node_modules`, `npm ci`, and confirm the tests pass.
**Resources:** npm's docs on `package.json` and `npm ci`; pnpm's motivation page; semver.org; the npm docs on install scripts and supply-chain security.
**Self-check:** Can you explain why `npm ci` is the CI choice in one sentence, what the caret and tilde allow, and the one question you'd ask before adding a dependency?

## 2.5.3 Browser DevTools
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The browser's built-in instrumentation for inspecting what actually happened — the DOM, the network, the performance profile, and the heap — as opposed to what you assumed happened.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because frontend debugging is mostly empirical. When they say "the page is slow," they're testing whether you can open the right panel and read it, rather than guess whether it's the network, the render, or a loop. It also transfers directly to the Phase 5 and Phase 9 work.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.8.

**Key concepts**

| Panel | Use it for |
|---|---|
| **Elements** | Inspect and live-edit DOM/CSS, see box model, computed styles, force `:hover` state |
| **Console** | Logs, errors, run JS, `console.table`, `console.time`, `$0` (selected element) |
| **Sources** | Breakpoints, conditional breakpoints, logpoints, call stack, scope, watch, blackboxing, source maps |
| **Network** | Waterfall, status codes, headers, payloads, timing breakdown, throttling, "Disable cache", copy as cURL |
| **Performance** | Record and flame-chart CPU, long tasks, layout/paint, frames |
| **Memory** | Heap snapshots, allocation timeline, find leaks (detached DOM nodes) |
| **Application** | Cookies, localStorage, sessionStorage, IndexedDB, service workers, cache storage |
| **Lighthouse** | Audits: performance, accessibility, SEO, best practices |

**Practical skills**
- Debug a failed API call: Network tab → filter Fetch/XHR → check status, request payload, response body, headers (CORS errors show up in the Console, and the preflight is an `OPTIONS` request).
- Debug layout: Elements → inspect the box model → toggle properties → check which rule wins.
- Find slow interactions: Performance tab → record → look for long tasks (over 50ms).
- Device emulation and network throttling to test mobile conditions.
- Read the **waterfall**: the critical path is the longest chain of blocking requests, not the sum of all durations. Four requests that each take 200 ms in parallel take 200 ms, not 800.
- **`Disable cache` plus throttling** is how you reproduce a bug that only happens for real users on a slow connection.
- **Console `$0`, `copy(obj)`, `table()`, `time()`** turn the console into a scratchpad instead of a log dump.
- **Live-editing in Elements** is the fastest way to test a hypothesis about CSS, and then you copy the winning rule into your stylesheet — otherwise you lose it on refresh.
- **Coverage tab** shows which bytes were actually downloaded versus executed, and it's the fastest way to find a dependency you're paying for and not using.
- **The Network panel answers "what did the server actually send?"** — after a lot of debugging, the answer is frequently "a 200 with the wrong body" or "the request never happened."

**Questions**
1. How would you debug a request that fails with a CORS error?
2. How do you find why a page is slow?
3. How would you find a memory leak?
4. How do you inspect what cookies a site set, and their flags?
5. How can you reproduce an API request outside the browser?
6. What do you look at in the Network waterfall first, and why?
7. Why did a CORS error appear in the Console rather than the Network panel?
8. How do you check whether a dependency is actually worth loading?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Two errors look the same, so I'd tell them apart first. A **hard failure** means the preflight or the request was blocked by the browser: I check the Console for the exact reason — the common ones are a missing or wrong `Access-Control-Allow-Origin`, credentials mode `include` without `Access-Control-Allow-Credentials: true`, or a wildcard origin combined with credentials. Then I look at the Network panel for the `OPTIONS` preflight and its response headers. A **soft failure** means the browser sent it and the server answered, but the JS couldn't read it — the real request is a 200 and the server is missing the header, which only breaks the front-end read. So the answer to 'whose bug is it' is: the browser never lied, so it's a server header problem."
**Model answer, Q6:** "The critical path, not the total. The waterfall's blocking chain is what determines when the page is usable, and requests running in parallel all cost you the same time as the slowest one. So I look for the longest contiguous chain ending in first paint, and the usual culprits are a render-blocking resource, a non-preloaded font, and a JS bundle that has to download before it can discover more work. I also check for a serialized chain — three requests where the third could have started during the first — because removing a dependency from the chain is usually cheaper than making any single request faster."
**What they evaluate**
- **Panel choice matched to the symptom,** not a general "I'd check the console."
- **Reading the waterfall as a critical path,** which is the real skill.
- **Knowing the browser enforced policy,** not that CORS is mysterious.
**Probe:** "The API returns 200 in the Network tab but the frontend still errors. Where do you look?" Or: "A page takes 3 seconds to become interactive. What's your first move?"
**Common wrong answers:** "I'd check the Network tab" with no follow-through; CORS described as a frontend fix via a proxy with no server-side explanation; the waterfall's total duration treated as the metric; a memory leak diagnosed by watching live heap size rather than comparing snapshots; throttling used without disabling cache.
**Trade-offs:**
- **DevTools vs log statements:** DevTools shows what the browser and runtime actually did, but only while you're watching; logs persist and work in production, at the cost of code churn and noise.
- **Throttling vs testing on real devices:** throttling is instant and repeatable but doesn't reproduce real radio or thermal behaviour; a real device is the ground truth and much slower to iterate on.
- **Live CSS edits vs editing the source:** live editing is instant and disposable, and loses everything on refresh unless you copy the winning rule back.
- **Reading panels manually vs automated Lighthouse budgets:** Lighthouse is repeatable and fits CI, and it can't tell you why.
**Mistakes:** ignoring the `OPTIONS` request; treating CORS as a client-side problem; chasing a total-time number instead of the critical path; taking one heap snapshot and calling it a leak; measuring in the Network panel with a warm cache; fixing the browser CSS without moving the rule into the stylesheet.
**Mini task:** Load a real page, record a performance profile, and identify the longest task and the critical path to first paint. Then use "Copy as cURL" to run the failing request in a terminal, and use throttling plus "Disable cache" to reproduce a bug that only shows up on a slow connection.
**Resources:** developer.chrome.com/docs/devtools for the panel-by-panel guides and the Network and Performance references; the Chrome DevTools "Rendering Performance" course; Firefox's equivalent panels where they differ.
**Self-check:** Can you name the panel for a network failure, a slow render, and a leak, and explain what you'd compare in two heap snapshots to call it a leak?

## 2.5.4 Debuggers and Breakpoints
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Pausing execution at a chosen point so you can inspect the entire live state of the program, rather than inferring it from whatever you managed to print.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because a candidate who can drop a conditional breakpoint on a request id and walk the call stack is demonstrating a debugging process that scales, which is exactly what they're evaluating. It's also the fastest way to make the async and state bugs in Phase 5 tractable.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.8, 2.5.3.

**Key concepts**
- **Breakpoint types:** line, conditional (`user.id === 42`), logpoint (log without editing code), exception (pause on throw), DOM mutation, XHR/fetch.
- **Controls:** step over, step into, step out, continue.
- **Inspect:** call stack, local and closure scope, watch expressions, hover values.
- **`debugger;` statement** pauses when DevTools are open.
- **Node:** `node --inspect-brk app.js` → attach Chrome DevTools (`chrome://inspect`) or VS Code.
- **Debugger vs `console.log`:** logs are fine for quick checks and async flows. A debugger is better for complex state, because you can inspect everything at the pause and don't pollute code. Use both.
- **Source maps** map bundled/transpiled code back to the original, and must be configured (and not shipped publicly in production without thought).
- **Debugging tests:** run a single test with `--inspect`, or use the VS Code JS debug terminal.
- **Logpoints are the underrated one:** you get the value at that exact line with no code change, so nothing is left behind to be committed by accident.
- **Exception breakpoints are how you find out where something came from** when the stack trace is unhelpful — pause on caught exceptions too, since a swallowed error means the throw site is the only evidence.
- **Conditional breakpoints can be slow.** A breakpoint in a hot loop evaluates the expression on every hit; for a million iterations that may cost more than the bug. Blackboxing library code, or using a logpoint, avoids it.
- **`--inspect-brk` vs `--inspect`:** `brk` breaks before your first line, which is what you want when the interesting code runs at import time. Plain `--inspect` starts immediately.
- **Watch expressions and the call stack are the two things you can't get from logs.** A watch expression can evaluate `this.items.length` in the paused scope; a log would have captured a stale value.
- **Async/await and breakpoints:** `debugger` works inside an async function, and the stack will show the promise chain rather than a single deep stack — expect to look at the closure scope for the captured variables.

**Questions**
1. Breakpoint types you'd reach for, and when?
2. `console.log` vs debugger — when do you switch?
3. Step over vs step into vs step out?
4. How do you debug an async flow that intermittently fails?
5. What's a logpoint, and why is it better than a console.log?
6. How do you attach a debugger to a Node process?
7. Why do source maps matter when debugging a bundled app, and what's the risk?
8. How do you find where an exception was thrown when the stack trace is unhelpful?
9. What's blackboxing, and when does it help?

**Model answer, Q4:** "Intermittent failures usually mean a race or a stale value, so I'd narrow the conditions rather than the code. First I'd add logging or a logpoint with a filter — the request id, the resolved value, the timing — and run it repeatedly to see the pattern. Then I'd put a conditional breakpoint on the one request that's failing, so I catch that case rather than pausing on every call. If the ordering is the suspect, I pause on both sides of the boundary and read the call stack to see what's in flight. And I'd reproduce it in a loop or under a test with artificial delays, because a bug that happens one in fifty times is much easier to fix once you can make it happen fifty times on demand."
**Model answer, Q5:** "A logpoint prints to the console when execution reaches that line, without adding a `console.log` to the source. So nothing has to be cleaned up afterwards — you can't accidentally commit the debug output, and the file on disk stays clean the whole time. You can also make it conditional and put an expression rather than a string, so it logs the whole object at the moment it was that value, not the object you see ten lines later. That's the real advantage: a log shows you what the variable is *now*, a logpoint shows you what it was *then*."
**What they evaluate**
- **Range of breakpoint types,** especially conditional and exception breakpoints.
- **Async debugging judgment** — filters, repeatability, timing.
- **Cleanliness:** logpoints and not leaving debug code behind.
**Probe:** "A breakpoint is making the app 10× slower. What do you do?" Or: "Your code is minified and the stack trace is unreadable. Now what?"
**Common wrong answers:** only line breakpoints; `console.log` as the only technique; stepping line by line through an async flow without filtering; not knowing how to attach to Node; assuming source maps fix stack traces automatically; leaving `debugger;` in committed code.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Debugger vs logs:** a debugger gives full state and control flow inspection but requires being present and doesn't survive into production; logs work everywhere including production but pollute code and freeze a single moment.
- **Line breakpoints vs conditional:** conditional is fast to find a rare case but can be slow in hot loops and is awkward when you don't yet know the discriminating value.
- **Exception breakpoints vs reading the stack trace:** pausing on throw catches silent catches and gives you the throw site, at the cost of stopping constantly in code that throws deliberately.
- **Attaching a debugger vs a remote shell:** attaching gives full inspection; a shell is available when the process isn't yours to instrument at all.
**Mistakes:** committed `debugger;` statements; pausing on every iteration of a loop; stepping into library internals instead of blackboxing them; logging derived values instead of the raw ones; forgetting source maps for a bundled app; treating a debugger as a substitute for a reproduction.
**Mini task:** Debug a deliberately broken Express route using breakpoints and a conditional breakpoint to catch a single bad request out of many. Then convert the breakpoints to logpoints, run it again, and confirm the source file is unmodified. Finally attach a Node debugger from VS Code to the same code.
**Resources:** VS Code's "Debugging" docs including the JavaScript Debug Terminal; Chrome's "How To" for the debugger; the Node `--inspect` CLI docs; MDN's `debugger` statement reference.
**Self-check:** Can you attach a debugger to a Node process from scratch, and can you name a situation where a logpoint is strictly better than a `console.log`?

## 2.5.5 Linters and Formatters (ESLint, Prettier)
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Automated checks that keep code consistent and catch classes of defect before review, with a formatter handling the decisions humans shouldn't be spending review time on.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because "how do you enforce quality on a team" is a real engineering question, and the strong answer separates the two tools by what they own, adds CI as the authority, and has a migration story for legacy code. It also connects to the Phase 7 code review material.
**Prerequisites:** 2.5.2.

**Key concepts**

| | Linter (ESLint) | Formatter (Prettier) |
|---|---|---|
| Purpose | Find bugs and enforce code-quality rules (unused vars, `no-undef`, hooks rules) | Consistent style (whitespace, quotes, line length) |
| Opinion | Configurable rules | Intentionally opinionated |
| Fixes | Some auto-fixable | Always auto |
