- **Why both:** formatting debates disappear from code review, and real issues get caught early.
- **Enforcement stack:** editor on-save → **pre-commit hooks** (`husky` + `lint-staged`, run only on staged files) → **CI** (the final authority, since local hooks can be bypassed).
- **Typecheck in CI:** `tsc --noEmit`.
- **Config:** ESLint flat config (`eslint.config.js`), `eslint-config-prettier` to avoid rule conflicts. Biome is a fast all-in-one alternative.
- **EditorConfig** for cross-editor basics.
- **`eslint-config-prettier` exists because the two tools disagree.** Prettier turns off formatting rules and ESLint disables them, so a rule like `quotes` doesn't fight Prettier's output and produce an unfixable loop.
- **Rules have severities.** `error` fails the build, `warn` is visible but non-blocking, `off` is disabled. Migrating a legacy codebase means promoting rules one at a time from `off` to `warn` to `error`.
- **`--fix` is the bulk of the value.** Many rules are mechanically fixable — unused imports, missing semicolons, `==` to `===`. Letting the tool do it turns "a thousand-line style PR" into a commit no human had to read line by line.
- **`lint-staged` is what makes pre-commit hooks usable.** Husky running your full lint suite on every commit is slow and annoying, so people disable it. Restricting to staged files means you only pay for what you're actually committing.
- **CI is the authority, always.** Hooks can be bypassed with `--no-verify`, can be missing because someone cloned badly, and don't run for changes made through the GitHub UI or a rebase. A rule that only lives in a hook is a suggestion.
- **`typecheck` is separate from `lint`.** ESLint with type-aware rules can catch some type problems, but `tsc --noEmit` is the authoritative answer and takes a fraction of the time of the type-aware ESLint rules. Run both.
- **Format-on-save, not format-on-commit,** so the working tree is already consistent and the diff contains only logic.
- **Biome** does both jobs in Rust, so it's dramatically faster, and the trade is a smaller rule set and less ecosystem maturity for framework-specific rules.

```json
// package.json (excerpt)
"scripts": { "lint": "eslint .", "format": "prettier --write .", "typecheck": "tsc --noEmit" },
"lint-staged": { "*.{ts,tsx,js}": ["eslint --fix", "prettier --write"] }
```

**Questions**
1. What's the difference between a linter and a formatter, and why run both?
2. Why run checks in CI as well as locally?
3. How do you introduce a linter to a legacy codebase without a thousand-line PR?
4. What is `eslint-config-prettier` for?
5. `error` vs `warn` severity — when do you use each?
6. Why run lint-staged instead of linting everything on every commit?
7. Is a formatter negotiable in review?
8. ESLint's `no-undef` didn't fire but the app crashed on an undefined variable. Why?

**Model answer, Q3:** "In stages. I'd start with the auto-fixable formatting and correctness rules only — run `--fix` and commit that as one commit, so review shows 'no behavior change' rather than 1,200 real lines. Then I'd enable the remaining rules as `warn`, with an `ignoreBlameRevs` list for the files that already have thousands of violations, so new code is clean without touching the old. From there I'd enforce on changed files only, which is what services like Danger or a CI lint-diff tool give you. The point is that every PR gets stricter, and nobody ever has to review 1,200 lines of unrelated changes. I'd also add `--max-warnings 0` once the warnings are actually at zero, which is the signal that it's safe."
**Model answer, Q2:** "Because local enforcement is unreliable in ways people forget. Hooks can be skipped with `--no-verify`, a fresh clone might not have them installed, a commit made through the GitHub UI or during a rebase never runs them, and CI settings can drift per developer. CI is the shared, unskippable contract — it's the one place where the rule applies to everybody, including me. The local hook is for fast feedback while I'm typing; CI is for enforcement. I'd put the same commands in both, so if CI fails, it failed locally first."
**What they evaluate**
- **A clean split of responsibilities** between the two tools.
- **A staged migration plan** rather than "just enable it."
- **Knowing CI is the authority** and why hooks aren't enough.
**Probe:** "A teammate's editor reformats files you didn't touch. What did you misconfigure?" Or: "Your lint script takes 90 seconds. How do you make pre-commit usable?"
**Common wrong answers:** the linter and formatter treated as the same tool; a linter described as enforcing style; "we don't need CI, hooks are enough"; the migration plan being "turn it all on and fix the errors"; `eslint-config-prettier` described as a preset of rules.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Linting in hooks vs only CI:** hooks give instant feedback and fix mistakes before they exist, at the cost of slowing every commit and being skippable.
- **Enforcing the full codebase vs changed files only:** full enforcement is consistent and impossible to forget, and legacy violations make it impossible to start.
- **ESLint + Prettier vs Biome:** two tools mean best-in-class for each job and a config to keep coherent; Biome is one fast binary with a smaller rule set.
- **Auto-fixing vs manual review:** auto-fixing is fast and consistent, and a formatter's output is not worth a human's attention — but a rule that changes behaviour should never be auto-applied without a test.
**Mistakes:** ESLint configured to fight Prettier; `--max-warnings 0` before the warnings are zero; running the whole suite on every commit; committing auto-fix output mixed with a feature change; relying on hooks alone; linting generated files; ignoring ESLint's own errors.
**Mini task:** Add ESLint, Prettier, husky, lint-staged and a `typecheck` script to a project, with `eslint-config-prettier` so the two don't conflict. Then take a messy file and land it as a pure auto-fix commit, reviewable as "no behaviour change."
**Resources:** ESLint's "Getting Started" and flat config docs; Prettier's rationale docs; husky and lint-staged READMEs; the Biome docs if you're evaluating the alternative.
**Self-check:** Can you name one job for each of ESLint, Prettier and `tsc`, and describe a three-stage plan for enabling rules on a legacy codebase?

## 2.5.6 Semantic Versioning, Node Versions, and Reproducibility
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The set of conventions — semver ranges, pinned runtimes, committed lockfiles, declared environment variables — that turn "works on my machine" into "works on every machine."
**Why interviewers ask:** Because reproducibility is a property of a system, not of a developer, and it's the thing that makes CI and a team possible at all. It's also the answer to every "why did this break on deploy?" question you'll be asked in Phase 7.
**Prerequisites:** 2.5.2.

**Key concepts**
- **Semver in practice:** libraries should bump major on breaking changes. `0.x` versions can break at any minor.
- **Node versions:** use even-numbered LTS releases for production. Manage with `nvm`, `fnm`, or `volta`, and pin with `.nvmrc` and `engines` in `package.json`.
- **Reproducibility checklist:** lockfile committed, pinned Node version, `.env.example`, one-command setup (`npm run dev` or `docker compose up`), documented prerequisites, and seed data.
- **Changelogs and releases:** conventional commits + `semantic-release` or Changesets automate versions.
- **`engines` is documentation unless enforced.** Add `.nvmrc` and a CI matrix step that uses it — an `engines` field alone produces a warning and nothing else.
- **Odd-numbered Node releases are never LTS,** which makes them the wrong choice for production and fine for local experiments.
- **`0.x` means "no stability promise."** Under semver, `0.1.0 → 0.2.0` may break anything. That's why many libraries treat 0.x minors as majors in practice.
- **A caret is still a range, so unpinned dependencies drift.** `^4.17.0` will happily take you to 4.20.0. If you truly need bit-identical behaviour forever, pin the exact version and accept that you'll need to upgrade deliberately.
- **`.env.example` is documentation that can't go stale silently** — it's committed, it's in review, and it lists every variable the app expects without containing a secret. A missing variable in CI should be a loud failure at startup, not a mystery three weeks later.
- **A one-command setup is a design goal, not a convenience.** If cloning and running takes more than two commands, someone will skip it and you'll get a bug report about their environment instead of about your code.
- **Seed data makes "impossible" bugs reproducible.** A database with ten hand-picked rows hides ordering, timezone and collation issues; a seeded fixture set with a thousand rows surfaces them in CI.
- **Docker pins the OS layer too,** which is how you remove the last variable — the base image, the libc, and the native modules that only compiled on someone's machine.

**Questions**
1. What does `engines` actually do? How do you make it enforced?
2. Which Node versions do you run in production, and why?
3. What does `0.x` mean for stability?
4. Walk me through your reproducibility checklist for a new project.
5. Why commit `.env.example` but never `.env`?
6. What are conventional commits and what problem do they solve?
7. How far do you go pinning dependency versions?
8. Why does `^` still mean a build can change without a code change?
9. What goes wrong with only a lockfile and no pinned Node?

**Model answer, Q4:** "Six things. One: commit the lockfile so the transitive tree is exact. Two: pin the runtime with a committed `.nvmrc` plus `engines`, and have CI read the same file. Three: commit `.env.example` listing every variable the app expects, and make startup fail loudly if one is missing rather than defaulting to undefined. Four: one command to run — `npm run dev` or `docker compose up` — so nobody debugs their setup. Five: document prerequisites and seed data, so a fresh database has rows and a new hire isn't guessing. Six: fail fast at startup and in CI for anything missing. The test is whether someone who has never seen the repo can get from clone to running tests without asking me anything."
**Model answer, Q8:** "Because `^4.17.0` means `>=4.17.0 <5.0.0`. So semver says that minor and patch releases are backwards-compatible, and it trusts that promise, which means a fresh install six months later can resolve a newer 4.x than the one that passed CI — with the same `package.json`. The lockfile prevents exactly that, and that's why the lockfile is the thing that has to be committed and why CI uses `npm ci`. Semver is the compatibility contract; the lockfile is the actual guarantee. Where I'm responsible for a tool that must behave identically, I pin the exact version and upgrade deliberately."
**What they evaluate**
- **Understanding the difference between a declared and an enforced runtime version.**
- **A checklist that covers more than the lockfile.**
- **Failing loudly on missing configuration.**
**Probe:** "Your deploy worked yesterday and the build is broken today. Walk me through the cause." Or: "Why does `engines` not stop anyone?"
**Common wrong answers:** `engines` described as enforcing versions; `.env` committed for convenience; an odd-numbered Node release in production; "pin everything exactly" as the universal answer; semver's compatibility promise treated as a guarantee.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Exact pins vs ranges:** pins give identical behaviour and mean you find and fix the breaking upgrade yourself; ranges get free patches and can change under you.
- **Docker vs `nvm`:** Docker pins the OS, libc and native modules too, and is heavier to iterate on; `nvm` is instant and still leaves the OS as a variable.
- **Seeding a large realistic dataset vs a minimal one:** realistic data catches collation, timezone and ordering bugs, and slows every test run.
- **Failing fast vs defaulting:** failing fast surfaces configuration errors immediately, and can break a deployment that used to work on a default.
**Mistakes:** `engines` without enforcement; an uncommitted lockfile; `.env` committed "just this once"; no `.nvmrc`; defaulting a missing env var to a plausible value; a README with twelve manual steps and no one-command path; assuming CI and local run the same Node version.
**Mini task:** Make a project reproducible from scratch: committed lockfile, `.nvmrc`, `engines`, an `.env.example`, a startup check that throws on a missing variable, one-command setup, seed data, and a CI job that reads `.nvmrc`. Then time a cold clone to passing tests — the target is under two minutes.
**Resources:** semver.org; the Node release schedule and LTS policy; `nvm`/`fnm` docs; Changesets or `semantic-release` documentation; twelve-factor app config.
**Self-check:** Can you explain what `engines` does versus what `.nvmrc` plus CI does, and name three things in your checklist that aren't the lockfile?

## 2.5.7 AI Coding Assistants: Use and Limits
🟢 Beginner → 🔴 Advanced · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** Using a language model to accelerate parts of the work — scaffolding, tests, explanation, refactoring — while remaining accountable for every line it produces.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because this is now a normal part of professional practice, and the interesting question isn't "do you use them" but "how do you verify what they produce." A candidate who reviews AI output more skeptically than a colleague's PR is demonstrating exactly the judgement the role requires.
**Prerequisites:** 2.5.2, 2.5.5.

**Key concepts**
**Where they help:** boilerplate, tests, regex, explaining unfamiliar code, refactoring, translating between languages, rubber-ducking, and docs drafts.

**Where they fail:** hallucinated APIs and packages, outdated library usage (a version mismatch), subtle logic and concurrency bugs, security holes (SQL injection, missing authorization), overly complex solutions, and confidently wrong explanations.

**Professional practice**
- **You own every line you commit.** Read and understand it. Review AI code with *more* skepticism than a colleague's.
- Verify with tests, types, linters, and the docs. Don't paste secrets or proprietary code into unapproved tools (follow company policy).
- Give context (stack, versions, constraints, examples) and iterate in small steps.
- Check licensing and provenance concerns for large generated chunks.
- Keep your fundamentals sharp, because you can't review what you can't understand.

**The failure modes worth naming precisely**
- **Hallucinated APIs and packages** — a method that doesn't exist, or an entire package that isn't. Both fail immediately at install or runtime, which is the good case.
- **Outdated library usage** — this is the dangerous one. The API is real, the code compiles, and it silently does the wrong thing because the signature changed in v3.
- **Subtle logic and concurrency** — especially async ordering, which is why every generated `await` deserves a look for whether the error is actually handled.
- **Security holes** — string-concatenated SQL, missing authorization checks, invented input validation. The code looks professional and is wide open.
- **Confidently wrong explanations** — the prose is fluent and the reasoning is inverted. This is the mode that most often gets accepted, because it reads well.
- **Absence of a dependency** — asked to use a library, the model sometimes writes the logic inline instead, so you lose the audit trail and the bug fixes.

**In interviews:** follow the stated rules. If AI tools aren't permitted, don't use them. If they are, show your judgment: prompt precisely, verify output, and explain what you changed and why.

**Questions**
1. How do you use AI tools in your workflow, and how do you verify their output?
2. Tell me about a time an AI suggestion was wrong. How did you catch it?
3. What risks do AI tools introduce to a codebase?
4. Which parts of your work would you never delegate, and why?
5. How do you write a prompt that actually gets you working code?
6. How do you review a large AI-generated diff?
7. What's your policy on sending code to an external tool at work?
8. Why does "it runs without errors" not mean AI-generated code is correct?

**Model answer, Q1:** "I use it in three places: scaffolding — a first draft of a test file, a boilerplate Express route, a regex — because the shape is predictable; explanation, when I'm staring at unfamiliar code and want a hypothesis to verify; and rubber-ducking, describing a bug out loud to see what falls out. I don't use it for anything where I can't easily verify the answer. Verification is mechanical, in this order: does it install and do the types check, do the tests pass including the ones it wrote, do the linters pass, and then does the docs for the exact version I'm on confirm the API exists. If I can't confirm the API from the source or docs, it doesn't go in. And I read every line before it goes into a commit, because the accountability doesn't transfer."
**Model answer, Q3:** "Security first — generated code has confidently written SQL injection, missing authorization checks, and invented validation, none of which a linter catches. Then correctness: subtle async ordering bugs, off-by-one, and library usage from an older major version that still compiles and still runs wrong. Then maintenance: inconsistent style that fights the codebase's, and more abstraction than the problem needed. Then organizational risk — a dependency that doesn't exist, licensing on large generated chunks, and proprietary code leaving the company through an unapproved tool. The long-term one is skill atrophy: if I stop being able to write and debug this myself, I've lost the ability to review it, and reviewing is the job."
**What they evaluate**
- **A verification procedure,** not enthusiasm or refusal.
- **Specific failure modes,** especially the dangerous one — outdated API usage that still compiles.
- **Unwillingness to delegate review** to the tool.
**Probe:** "It generated 200 lines that pass all your tests and the types are clean. Now what?" Or: "How do you review a diff you didn't write and don't fully understand?"
**Common wrong answers:** "I always read it" with no verification mechanism; assuming anything that compiles is right; rejecting AI tools entirely as a position; sending proprietary code to a public tool and calling it a style preference; trusting a generated package name.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Speed vs verification overhead:** generating then verifying is much faster than writing by hand, and the verification step is where the real time goes if you do it properly.
- **Bigger prompts vs iteration:** a long, context-rich prompt gets closer in one shot, and iteration is easier to review and to course-correct.
- **Delegating boilerplate vs design:** delegating mechanical work frees time for design, and the design is exactly the part where a subtle wrong answer is most expensive and hardest to notice.
- **Local models vs external tools:** local keeps code inside the boundary, at the cost of quality and speed on hard problems.
**Mistakes:** accepting unread output; trusting a package name; skipping the exact-version docs check; letting generated code define the style; not reading a diff before approving it; pasting secrets; using generated tests as the only evidence a feature works.
**Mini task:** Generate tests for a module you already wrote well, then check whether the generated tests actually catch the bugs the existing ones catch. Now delete the module's implementation, keep the tests, and see if they fail for the right reasons. That's the fastest way to find out whether AI-generated tests are load-bearing.
**Resources:** your organisation's AI tooling policy; OWASP's guidance on code generated by LLMs; the npm and docs pages for the libraries it mentions, which is the verification step itself.
**Self-check:** Can you describe your verification procedure in order, name a failure mode that still compiles, and say which parts of the job you'd never delegate?

## 2.5.8 VS Code and extensions
🟢 Beginner · **Nice-to-know** · the editor is tooling, and tooling is part of the answer

**Definition:** How to configure an editor so it works for you — keybindings, settings, extensions — and how to explain that as a deliberate choice rather than a preference.
**Why interviewers ask:** Rarely as a technical question in itself, but they will ask *"what's your setup?"* as a conversation opener, and what you say reveals whether you can reason about your tools or just accumulate them. The junior who has 40 extensions installed and can't name what any of them does sounds like someone who copies setup guides; the one who has four, knows why, and can say which ones they've deliberately turned off sounds like someone who pays attention. Editing performance shows up too — if your editor is janky, so are you.
**Prerequisites:** 2.5.1.

**Key concepts**
- **Settings are scoped:** user (your machine), workspace (the repo's `.vscode/settings.json`, checked in), and language-specific, and the order of precedence matters when you're wondering why a setting isn't taking effect.
- **Keybindings are worth customising, not accepting.** The default arrow-key-plus-modifier scheme is fine; a handful of real bindings are better.
```json
// .vscode/keybindings.json
[
  { "key": "ctrl+shift+k", "command": "editor.action.deleteLines" },
  { "key": "ctrl+d", "command": "editor.action.addSelectionToNextFindMatch" },
  { "key": "alt+click", "command": "editor.action.insertCursorAtEndOfEachLineSelected" }
]
```
- **`editor.formatOnSave` plus a formatter is the highest-value setting** — it removes an entire class of "why is this whitespace wrong" diff noise.
- **Extensions are code that runs in your editor with broad access,** so treat the list like dependencies: check the publisher, the last update, the open issues, the install count, and the permissions it requests. A 30,000-download, last-published-in-2017 extension with filesystem access is a supply-chain risk.
- **Keep the extension list short and justified.** Most needs are already met by the language server, ESLint, Prettier, and GitLens; everything else should have a reason.
- **`settings.json` is code — use it.** Reviewers can read it, new machines reproduce it, and a comment explaining an unusual setting is documentation.
- **Learned settings are per-machine, synced via Settings Sync;** workspace settings are shared. Knowing which bucket a setting lives in is half of debugging "why is this only happening on my laptop".
- **The integrated terminal is the same shell as outside it,** so don't waste time on editor-specific command behaviour — and remember relative paths resolve against the workspace root, which trips people up constantly.
- **Worth knowing the built-ins:** command palette, Quick Open, Go to Definition, Rename Symbol, multi-cursor, and split editor. If you're searching the web for something the editor can do natively, that itself is the finding.
- **Profiles solve extension sprawl** — separate installations for different stacks, so a React profile doesn't carry Python tooling.

**Questions**
1. What's your editor setup, and why?
2. Which extensions do you actually rely on?
3. What does `formatOnSave` do, and why enable it?
4. Where do settings live, and what wins?
5. Have you written your own extension or keybinding?
6. How do you debug a setting that "isn't working"?

**Model answer, Q1:** "VS Code, mostly because it's free, and because the language servers and debugging work are the best I've used. I keep it to ESLint and Prettier with format-on-save, a Git extension for history, and a Tailwind one when I'm in a frontend repo. Everything else is off. I customise maybe six keybindings so my hands don't leave the home row, and I check the settings into the workspace so the team shares them."
**Model answer, Q3:** "It runs the configured formatter on every save, so files never arrive with inconsistent whitespace and the diffs stay about actual logic. It only makes sense alongside a formatter like Prettier — the setting just triggers it. The caveat is that it can rewrite files you didn't mean to touch, so I scope it to the languages where the formatter is actually the team's style."
**Model answer, Q4:** "User, workspace, and language-specific scopes, and the more specific one wins — language-specific beats workspace beats user. Workspace settings live in `.vscode/settings.json` and get committed, which is why they can fix a problem for everyone rather than just me. The debugging order is: check the file is actually being picked up, check the scope, then check whether another extension is overriding it."
**Model answer, Q6:** "First I confirm which `settings.json` is in play — there are several and I've edited the wrong one — then check whether another extension with formatter support is also hooking format-on-save, which is a real conflict. Then I verify the formatter binary is actually resolvable, since a global install moving can break the path silently. If the setting is inherited from the workspace I look at the repo's file, not mine."

**What they evaluate**
- **Whether the answer is reasoned or recited** — "it's free and fast" is fine; "I like the icons" is not.
- **Awareness that extensions are a supply-chain surface,** which is the answer that surprises them.
- **Willingness to say what's turned off,** since a bloated extension list is a smell.
**Probe:** "A dependency takes 40 seconds to start. Your editor, or the app?" (Expect: profile the app first — VS Code's own startup profiler names the offending extension, and a slow language server is a much more common culprit than the editor.)
**Common wrong answers:** Recommending 15 extensions as a productivity upgrade; "settings sync means my config is on the team"; extensions described as sandboxed and harmless; not knowing workspace settings are committed; treating keybindings as personal preference that can't be shared.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Workspace settings vs user settings:** committing them gives the team one consistent experience, but forces opinions onto everyone and can annoy people who prefer their own setup; keeping them local means every new contributor re-establishes your configuration.
- **Extensions vs built-in commands:** extensions add power but cost startup time, update risk and review attention; the built-ins already cover a surprising amount, and `editor.action.*` commands need no install at all.
- **Format on save vs format on commit:** save-time formatting keeps files clean continuously but can surprise you mid-edit; a commit hook does it once at the point where others will see it, and lets you keep control locally in between.
**Mistakes:** Installing an unfamiliar extension mid-interview or mid-deadline; checking in personal settings like font size or theme to the repo; a formatters-on-save fight between Prettier and ESLint --fix with no `eslint-config-prettier`; debugging relative paths against the wrong directory; assuming a missing command means a missing extension.
**Mini task:** Set up a fresh profile with only ESLint, Prettier and Git, add format-on-save, customise six keybindings, and commit the workspace settings to a repo you own. Then measure cold-start time before and after removing each extension, and note the difference.
**Resources:** VS Code's official docs on settings scopes and keybindings; the marketplace listing permissions panel for any extension you're considering; the "Profiles" feature docs for extension management; your team's `.vscode/` directory as the best local example.
**Self-check:** Can you name your three settings and where each lives, explain what format-on-save needs to be useful, and name one risk of installing extensions blindly?

## Interview Questions (2.5) — Tooling, DevTools, and Debugging
**Definition:** The group-level practice set for testing and tooling fluency. Everything here is answerable from experience — the point is to practise saying the *process* out loud, not to memorise shortcuts.

**Questions**
1. Walk me through your debugging process for a bug you can't reproduce locally.
2. `dependencies`, `devDependencies`, `peerDependencies` — when does each apply?
3. Why does CI use `npm ci` rather than `npm install`?
4. What does `^1.2.3` allow, and what does it not allow?
5. How would you evaluate whether to add a new dependency?
6. Linter or formatter — what's the difference, and why run both?
7. How do you introduce strict linting to a legacy codebase?
8. Why run checks in CI when you already have pre-commit hooks?
9. The API returns 200 in the Network tab but the frontend throws. Where do you look?
10. How do you find why a page is slow?
11. How would you identify a memory leak using DevTools?
12. What makes a project reproducible for someone who has never seen the repo?
13. `console.log` or a debugger — when do you switch?
14. What is a logpoint, and when is it strictly better than logging?
15. How do you attach a debugger to a Node process?
16. Why is the lockfile a reproducibility tool rather than a security tool?
17. What are the three steps that turn "slow" into "measured"?
18. How do you use AI coding assistants, and how do you verify what they produce?
19. Why is a passing test suite not sufficient evidence that generated code is correct?
20. What's your reproducibility checklist for a brand-new project?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Information before change. I'd pull logs for the failing requests, note the versions and config that were running, and find what's different about the failing users or inputs. Then compare environments, add targeted logging at the boundary to see the actual payload rather than the assumed one, and try to reproduce with production-like data. If the blast radius is small I'd use a feature flag or canary to isolate code from data. If I truly can't reproduce it, the honest deliverable is instrumentation plus an alert, not a hopeful fix."
**Model answer, Q9:** "That's the case where the browser got a response and the server sent it, but the JavaScript couldn't read it. So I look at the Console for the real error and the Network panel's response headers. Usually it's a missing CORS header on a 200, or the frontend is trying to read a response body it never received. The browser doesn't lie in this case, so it's a header or a parsing bug, not a network failure."
**Model answer, Q12:** "Six things: the lockfile committed, the runtime pinned with `.nvmrc` plus `engines` and CI reading the same file, `.env.example` listing every expected variable, one command to run the app, documented prerequisites, and seed data. Plus fail-fast startup so a missing variable is loud instead of undefined. The test is whether a stranger can go from clone to passing tests without asking me anything."
**Model answer, Q18:** "Scaffolding, explanation, and rubber-ducking — the predictable shapes and the cases where I need a hypothesis to check. Verification is mechanical: install and typecheck, tests including the ones it wrote, linters, then confirm against the docs for the exact version I'm on. If the API can't be confirmed, it doesn't ship. I read every line, because the accountability doesn't transfer to the tool."
**What they evaluate**
- **Process under uncertainty** — the thread through all twenty answers.
- **Panel and tool choice matched to the symptom.**
- **Honesty about what you cannot verify.**

## Group 2.5 Wrap-Up — Testing, Debugging & Tooling

**Common mistakes across 2.5:** not committing the lockfile; global installs for project tools; `npm install` in CI; ignoring audit warnings forever; relying only on `console.log`; accepting AI output unread.

**Mini tasks:** Set up a fresh project with ESLint, Prettier, husky, lint-staged, a `typecheck` script, `.nvmrc`, `.env.example`, and a CI workflow, so the repo is clone, `npm ci`, run in under 2 minutes.

**Resources:** VS Code docs ("Tips and Tricks"), Chrome DevTools docs (developer.chrome.com/docs/devtools), npm docs, pnpm docs, ESLint and Prettier docs, semver.org.

**Self-check:** Can you debug a failing API call end to end (Network tab → payload → server breakpoint) in 5 minutes?
