# 2.6 COMPUTER SCIENCE BASICS FOR WEB

**Prerequisites:** programming basics. 🟢 → 🟡 · **Good-to-know** (but frequently probed through "why does this behave this way?" questions)

## 2.6.1 Binary and Number Systems
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** How numbers are represented as bits, why that representation is not always what you'd expect, and the practical consequences for money, IDs, and comparisons in JavaScript.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because the float-money bug and the `2^53` ID corruption bug are real production incidents, not exam trivia. Knowing *why* a 64-bit database ID silently becomes the wrong number when it goes through JSON is a mark of someone who has debugged a real bug.
**Prerequisites:** 2.4.1.

**Key concepts**
- **Bit** (0/1), **byte** = 8 bits (0-255). **Hex** (base 16) is compact for bytes: `0xFF = 255`, used in colors (`#ff8800`), hashes, and memory addresses. **Octal** appears in Unix permissions.
- **Conversions:** `(10).toString(2) → "1010"`, `parseInt("1010", 2) → 10`, `(255).toString(16) → "ff"`.
- **Two's complement:** how signed integers are stored. In 8 bits, `-1` = `11111111`. The range is -128 to 127.
- **Bitwise operators:** `&`, `|`, `^`, `~`, `<<`, `>>`, `>>>`. Useful for flags, permissions masks, and some algorithm tricks. JS bitwise ops work on **32-bit signed ints**.
```js
const READ = 1, WRITE = 2, EXEC = 4;
let perms = READ | WRITE;        // 3
const canWrite = (perms & WRITE) !== 0;   // true
const isPowerOfTwo = n => n > 0 && (n & (n - 1)) === 0;
```
- **Floating point (IEEE 754):** JS `number` is a 64-bit double. It can't represent most decimals exactly, so `0.1 + 0.2 === 0.30000000000000004`.
  - Compare with a tolerance (`Math.abs(a - b) < Number.EPSILON`).
  - **Never use floats for money.** Use integer cents or a decimal library.
  - Safe integer range: `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER = 2^53 - 1 = 9007199254740991`. Beyond that, precision is lost, so use `BigInt` (`123n`). This matters for 64-bit database IDs (e.g., Twitter snowflake IDs get corrupted in JSON → JS, so send them as strings).
- **Overflow:** fixed-width integers wrap (in languages like C/Java). JS numbers lose precision instead.

- **Why `0.1 + 0.2` isn't `0.3`:** both `0.1` and `0.2` are stored as the *nearest* binary fraction to those decimals, and the errors don't cancel when you add them. `0.1` is really `0.1000000000000000055511151231257827`. This isn't a JS bug — it's IEEE 754, and every language using binary doubles has it.
- **The fix for floats is an epsilon comparison, not rounding.** `Math.abs(a - b) < Number.EPSILON` is the right instinct, though in practice you'd pick a tolerance appropriate to your magnitude rather than the smallest possible one.
- **Money is the case where you must not tolerate error at all.** Because each operation compounds the error, the correct approach is to store **integer minor units** — cents — and do arithmetic in integers, formatting only at the display boundary. Or use a decimal library. Storing dollars as a float and trusting rounding is how totals end up off by a penny and reconciliation becomes someone's job.
- **The `2^53` boundary is a silent failure.** Beyond `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER`, `n + 1 === n` — arithmetic still "works" and returns a wrong number, with no error anywhere. That's why 64-bit IDs like Twitter snowflake IDs must cross the wire as strings, and why `parseInt` on an ID is the right call rather than `Number(id)`.
- **Bitwise operators truncate to int32.** `>>>` and friends coerce through `ToInt32`, which silently discards the fractional part and wraps anything outside int32 range. `'5.9 | 0` is `5`, and a snowflake ID bitwise-ANDed with a mask will be wrong for reasons nobody will notice.
- **Bitmasks are the one place bitwise operators earn their keep in web work** — file permissions, feature flags, and Unix-style modes. `perms & WRITE !== 0` is the clearest expression of "can it write?"
- **The power-of-two trick is a real interview answer.** A power of two has exactly one set bit, and `n - 1` flips that bit to zero and turns all lower bits on, so `n & (n - 1)` clears exactly that one bit — the result is zero only when `n` had a single set bit. The `n > 0` guard is required, because `0 & -1 === 0`.
- **Overflow vs precision loss:** fixed-width integers in C or Java **wrap**, so you can get a large negative number and no error. JS loses precision silently instead. Different failure, same warning: don't rely on the runtime to catch it.

**Questions**
1. Why is `0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3`? How do you handle money?
2. What's the largest safe integer in JS and what happens beyond it? When have you seen this in the real world?
3. Convert 13 to binary. What's `0xFF` in decimal?
4. How would you check if a number is a power of two?
5. Why are colors written in hex?
6. How are negative numbers represented?
7. What does `'5.9 | 0` evaluate to, and why?
8. Why send a 64-bit ID as a string in an API response?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Because both `0.1` and `0.2` are stored as the nearest binary fraction to those decimals, and adding them leaves a residue — `0.30000000000000004`. That's IEEE 754, not a JS bug; every binary-floating-point language does it. For general comparison I'd use an epsilon tolerance rather than `===`. For money I wouldn't use a float at all: I'd store integer minor units — cents — and do all arithmetic in integers, formatting only at the edge. Or a decimal library. Floats are wrong for money because the error compounds over many operations and reconciliation becomes a real job."
**Model answer, Q2:** "`Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER` is 2^53 minus 1, 9007199254740991. Beyond that, integers can't be represented distinctly, so `n + 1 === n` — arithmetic returns a wrong number with no error. The real-world case is 64-bit IDs like Twitter's snowflake IDs: they come out of Postgres as `bigint`, get serialized into JSON, and `JSON.parse` coerces them to doubles, so two different tweets can end up with the same ID in the client and the wrong one gets fetched. The fix is to send them as strings and `parseInt` on the client if arithmetic is needed, or use `BigInt` for values beyond the safe range."
**What they evaluate**
- **Understanding that it's binary representation,** not "JS is imprecise."
- **Money answered with integer minor units,** not "use `toFixed`."
- **The `2^53` failure described as silent.**
**Probe:** "Your totals are off by a penny after a few thousand line items. Explain." Or: "Why does `Object.is(0.1 + 0.2, 0.3)` also return false?"
**Common wrong answers:** floating point blamed on JS; money handled with `toFixed(2)` or rounding to 2 decimals; `MAX_SAFE_INTEGER` given as `2^53`; hex explained as "shorter than binary" with no mention of bytes; the power-of-two trick without the `n > 0` guard; `BigInt` recommended as a JSON fix (it can't be serialized by `JSON.stringify`).
**Trade-offs:**
- **Integers-as-cents vs a decimal library:** cents are fast, dependency-free and exact, and break on currencies with three decimal places or variable minor units; a decimal library handles all of that at the cost of a dependency and slower arithmetic.
- **`BigInt` vs string for large IDs:** `BigInt` gives exact arithmetic but cannot go through `JSON.stringify` without a custom replacer; strings travel through JSON perfectly and need `parseInt` if you must do math.
- **Bitwise masks vs named constants:** masks are compact and fast for permission checks, and unreadable without practice; named booleans are clearer and cost a comparison.
- **Exact float comparison vs epsilon:** exact is simpler and always wrong for values that should be equal; epsilon is correct but needs a tolerance chosen for the magnitude.
**Mistakes:** floats for money; `==` or `===` on computed decimals; 64-bit IDs parsed with `Number`; using bitwise operators for IDs or large values; assuming a wraparound will be caught; rounding money with `Math.round` as a strategy.
**Mini task:** Write `addMoney(a, b)` and `allocate(amount, shares)` using integer cents, with a formatter that handles rounding on the remainder. Then prove why it matters: sum a thousand random float amounts and show the total drifts, then show the integer version is exact.
**Resources:** MDN "Numbers" and `Number.MAX_SAFE_INTEGER`; the "Representing decimal in binary" explainer; Petzold's *Code*, chapter 7; Joel Spolsky's "The Overflow Files" for the historical reason `2^53` matters.
**Self-check:** Can you explain `0.1 + 0.2` in one sentence without saying "floating point is imprecise", and name the one rule you'd follow for money?

## 2.6.2 Memory: Stack vs Heap, Garbage Collection
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** Where a running program's data lives while it runs — the small, fast, automatically-managed call stack, and the large, GC-managed heap — and what follows for leaks and overflows.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because memory explains a whole category of failure you cannot debug with a stack trace: an app that gets slower and then dies with an out-of-memory error after two hours. Knowing that stack overflow and heap exhaustion are different problems with different fixes is the level of understanding they're after.
**Prerequisites:** 2.6.1, 2.4.4.

**Key concepts**
- **Stack:** fast, automatically managed, LIFO memory for function call frames, local variables, and primitives/references. Fixed, limited size, which is why infinite recursion overflows it.
- **Heap:** larger, dynamically allocated memory for objects, arrays, and closures. It needs management (GC), and allocation is slower than stack allocation.
```
STACK                          HEAP
┌─────────────────┐            ┌──────────────────────┐
│ main()          │            │ {name:'Ada', ...} ◀──┼─┐
│  user ──────────┼──pointer──▶│                      │ │
│  count = 5      │            └──────────────────────┘ │
│ greet()         │                                     │
│  u ─────────────┼─────────────────────────────────────┘
└─────────────────┘
```
- **Garbage collection (V8):** automatic reclamation of memory unreachable from roots. Uses **mark-and-sweep** (reachability), not just reference counting, so circular references are collected. It is **generational:** new objects go in a "young generation" (collected often and cheaply), survivors move to the "old generation". GC may cause short pauses, and V8 uses incremental and concurrent techniques to minimize them.
- **Memory leaks in JS** (objects remain reachable accidentally):
  - Forgotten timers/intervals and event listeners never removed
  - Closures capturing large objects
  - Growing global caches or arrays with no eviction (use `Map` with LRU or `WeakMap`)
  - Detached DOM nodes still referenced
  - In React: effects subscribing without cleanup
  - In Node: unbounded in-memory queues, per-request data stored globally
- **`WeakMap`/`WeakRef`:** hold references that don't prevent GC.
- **Node memory limits:** the V8 old-space default depends on the version and system. Raise it with `--max-old-space-size`, but fix leaks rather than raising limits.

- **The stack holds frames; the heap holds what frames point at.** A local variable like `user` is a small reference on the stack, and the object it references lives on the heap. That's why "where do variables live" is a slightly wrong question — the reference is on the stack and the object is on the heap. Say "conceptually" out loud, because engines optimise primitives onto the stack, objects onto the heap, and dedupe strings, and a candidate who insists JS exactly follows this model sounds like they've memorised a diagram.
- **Stack overflow is a depth problem; OOM is a size problem.** A stack overflow happens in milliseconds because the recursion never terminates and the fixed stack fills. Heap exhaustion happens after hours because objects accumulate and stay reachable. Different mechanism, different fix, and conflating them is the most common mistake in an interview on this topic.
- **Mark-and-sweep, not reference counting.** V8 traces reachability from roots — the global object, the stack, active handles — and collects anything it can't reach. This is why two objects referencing each other in a cycle are collected fine, which is exactly what a naive reference-counting collector leaks. It's also why a leak in JS is always a **reachability** bug: something is still pointing at the object, and the job is finding what.
- **Generational GC exploits a statistical truth:** most objects die young. New objects go into the young generation, which is collected frequently and cheaply. Objects that survive a couple of collections get promoted to the old generation, collected less often. That's why allocating heavily in a loop is expensive but short-lived garbage is cheap to reclaim.
- **The leak taxonomy is really one taxonomy: something still reachable.** Every item on the list — the timer, the listener, the closure, the global array, the React effect without cleanup — is an object that should have died but is still referenced. So the debugging method is the same each time: find the retained set.
- **Detached DOM nodes are the sneakiest case.** Removing an element from the DOM does not make it collectable if a JS reference to it still exists — the element is detached but retained. In Chrome DevTools, detached nodes show up in a heap snapshot as a distinct constructor (`Detached HTMLDivElement`), which makes them findable.
- **`WeakMap` is for *association*, not storage.** Putting an object in a `WeakMap` doesn't keep it alive, and if nothing else does, the entry disappears with it. That's exactly right for attaching metadata to a DOM node or a request object, and exactly wrong for a cache where you *want* the entry to persist. `WeakRef` is the lower-level primitive, and the `FinalizationRegistry` caveat applies: cleanup timing is not deterministic.
- **Raising `--max-old-space-size` changes when you fail, not whether.** It's a legitimate setting for a workload with a genuinely high ceiling, and it's a way of postponing a leak investigation until the outage is worse.
- **A `Map` is an unbounded cache unless you bound it.** Using a `Map` keyed by user id as a lookup cache looks harmless and is a leak with a long fuse; the fix is an LRU with eviction, or `WeakMap` where the lifetime can follow the key.

**Code: classic leak**
```js
// LEAK: listener never removed; component instances pile up
useEffect(() => {
  window.addEventListener('resize', onResize);
}, []);

// FIX
useEffect(() => {
  window.addEventListener('resize', onResize);
  return () => window.removeEventListener('resize', onResize);
}, []);
```

**Questions**
1. Stack vs heap? Where do primitives and objects live?
2. How does garbage collection work in JS?
3. What causes a memory leak in a JS app? How do you find one?
4. What is a stack overflow? How is it different from running out of heap memory?
5. How would you implement a bounded in-memory cache?
6. Why does a detached DOM node still consume memory?
7. What is `WeakMap` for, and when would it be the wrong choice?
8. When is it legitimate to raise `--max-old-space-size`?

**Model answer, Q3:** "A leak in JS is always a reachability problem — the object should have been collected but something still points at it. The usual causes are an event listener, timer or interval that was never removed, a closure capturing something large, a global cache with no eviction, or a React effect with no cleanup. To find one I take a heap snapshot, perform the suspect action, take another, and compare — and you need *both*, because one snapshot tells you what's alive but not what's growing. Then I look at the retained size and the retainer path, which names the exact reference keeping it alive; `Detached HTMLDivElement` in the snapshot is a strong signal of a removed node that's still referenced. In Node I'd watch a `process.memoryUsage()` trend across a loop of requests rather than a single reading."
**Model answer, Q4:** "A stack overflow is a *depth* problem — recursion that never terminates, or is too deep, fills the fixed call stack in milliseconds and throws `RangeError: Maximum call stack size exceeded`. Running out of heap is a *size* problem — objects accumulate over hours and stay reachable until V8 gives up with an out-of-memory error. So the fix for the first is to fix the recursion or convert to iteration with an explicit stack; the fix for the second is to find what's retaining the objects. Raising the stack size or the old-space limit doesn't fix either — it just moves when you crash."
**What they evaluate**
- **Reachability as the unifying explanation** of leaks.
- **Depth vs size** as the distinguishing pair.
- **A concrete method** — two snapshots and a comparison.
**Probe:** "Your Node server dies after about six hours with an OOM. Walk me through the first hour." Or: "Why isn't the GC bug with the circular reference?"
**Common wrong answers:** reference counting described as V8's algorithm; stack and heap described as where variables live rather than where objects live; leak debugging from a single snapshot; `WeakMap` recommended for caches; `--max-old-space-size` as a fix; a stack overflow attributed to "too much memory."
**Trade-offs:**
- **Manual free vs GC:** explicit disposal is deterministic and portable, and easy to forget; GC is automatic and safe, and its pauses and retention rules are invisible until they matter.
- **`WeakMap` vs `Map` for a cache:** `WeakMap` can't leak because entries die with their keys, and it can't be enumerated or iterated, so you can't implement LRU on it.
- **`--max-old-space-size` vs fixing the leak:** raising the limit buys time and hides evidence; fixing the leak removes the outage.
- **Bounded cache vs unbounded cache:** eviction costs a hit rate you can measure; no eviction costs you the process eventually.
**Mistakes:** an effect without a cleanup function; a global `Map` used as a cache; retaining DOM references after removal; a closure capturing a whole request object in a hot path; assuming "no references from my code" means "not reachable"; raising memory limits to stop a recurring OOM.
**Mini task:** Build the leak, prove it, fix it. A Node server that stores per-request data in a global array; log `process.memoryUsage().heapUsed` over a thousand requests and watch it climb; then cap the array and watch it plateau. Then do the same in React with a missing cleanup, and find the detached node in a heap snapshot.
**Resources:** MDN's "Memory management" and "Memory management API" guides; javascript.info's "Memory management" chapter; V8's blog posts on Orinoco and the generational design; Chrome's "Fix memory problems" DevTools guide.
**Self-check:** Can you explain in one sentence why V8's GC handles cycles, and can you list three leaks by naming the retainer rather than the symptom?

## 2.6.3 Compilation vs Interpretation
🟡 Intermediate · **Good-to-know**

**Definition:** The mechanisms that turn source code into something a machine runs — ahead-of-time compilation, line-by-line interpretation, and the just-in-time compilation that modern JavaScript engines actually use.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because the answer "JavaScript is interpreted" is now wrong, and the follow-ups — what a JIT does, why it deoptimizes, why TypeScript types vanish at runtime — lead straight into the performance work in Phase 9 and Phase 12.
**Prerequisites:** 2.6.1.

**Key concepts**
- **Compiled** (C, Go, Rust): source → machine code ahead of time (AOT). Fast execution, build step, platform-specific binaries.
- **Interpreted** (historically Python, early JS): executed line by line by an interpreter. Flexible, slower.
- **Modern reality: JIT (just-in-time) compilation.** V8 parses JS to an AST, generates bytecode (Ignition), runs it, and hot code is optimized to machine code (TurboFan). Optimizations rely on assumptions (e.g., a function always receives integers), and if violated, it **deoptimizes**. Writing type-stable code helps.
- **Transpilation:** source → source (TypeScript → JS, JSX → JS, modern JS → older JS via Babel/SWC).
- **Bundling/minification:** many modules → few optimized files for browsers.
- **Static vs dynamic typing:** type errors caught at compile time vs at runtime. TypeScript is erased at compile time, so there are *no runtime types*. Validate external data at runtime (Zod).
- **Bytecode/VM languages:** Java (JVM), C# (CLR), and WebAssembly (a portable binary format that runs near-native in browsers).

- **V8 does four things in order: parse, compile, run, then re-optimize.** It parses to an AST, Ignition compiles that to bytecode, the interpreter runs it while collecting type feedback for every call site, and TurboFan recompiles hot functions to machine code using what it observed. So it's not "interpreted until it's slow" — it's interpreted *as a probe* for a compiler.
- **Deoptimization is the interesting part.** TurboFan's generated code bakes in assumptions — this parameter was always an integer, this object's shape never changes. If an assumption is violated it throws away that code and falls back. That's why adding a property to an object after creation, or a function that sometimes receives a `string` and sometimes a `number`, can make a hot path slower than a naive implementation.
- **Type-stable code helps because of that feedback loop.** A monomorphic function — one that only ever sees the same shape and types — gets the best-optimized code. Polymorphic call sites generate worse code, which is why a code review comment about consistent object shapes in Phase 9 is a real performance concern, not a style preference.
- **TypeScript is a compile-time-only contract.** The types are erased; there's no runtime check. So `fetch` returning `unknown` or an untyped API response is genuinely unchecked, and that's the whole reason a runtime validator like Zod exists. "But it's TypeScript" is not an answer to "where does this data get validated?"
- **Transpilation and compilation are different jobs.** Babel and SWC take modern source and emit older-but-equivalent source plus JSX; that output still needs a JIT. A bundler is a third thing again: it resolves imports into a dependency graph and concatenates modules for fewer round trips — an HTTP optimization, not a compilation step.
- **WebAssembly is a compilation *target*, not a language.** You compile Rust or C to a `.wasm` binary, and the browser runs it at close-to-native speed in its own instance with linear memory and explicit imports. It's worth it when the bottleneck is genuinely CPU — video, image processing, crypto, a game engine — and it's pure overhead when the bottleneck is network or the DOM.
- **The AOT/JIT/interpreter trade-off is about when you pay.** AOT pays once at build time for consistent runtime cost, and can't use runtime observations. The interpreter pays nothing up front and is slow per operation. JIT defers the cost and only pays for the code that's actually hot, which is a great deal when most of your code runs once.

**Questions**
1. Is JavaScript compiled or interpreted?
2. What does TypeScript compile to? Do types exist at runtime?
3. What is a JIT compiler?
4. What's the difference between a compiler, interpreter, and transpiler?
5. What does a bundler do and why do we need one?
6. What is WebAssembly and when would you use it?
7. What is deoptimization and why does it happen?
8. If types are erased, where do you validate data coming from an API?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Both, depending on the era and the engine. V8 parses the source to an AST, compiles it to bytecode with Ignition, runs that bytecode in the interpreter, and watches what actually happens — the types and shapes flowing through each call site. When a function gets hot it recompiles it to machine code with TurboFan. So it starts as an interpreter but progressively compiles the code that matters, which is why you sometimes hear that V8 'de-optimizes' hot functions when your assumptions turn out to be wrong. The honest answer is that 'interpreted' and 'compiled' is a false dichotomy for modern JS — it's JIT-compiled."
**Model answer, Q8:** "At the boundary, at runtime, with a schema validator — Zod, Valibot, or a JSON Schema check. TypeScript's types are erased at compile time, so a `Response.json()` cast to my interface is a lie the compiler can't check: the server is a different codebase, possibly a different language, and the shape can change without a compile error. So I parse as `unknown` and validate, then use the inferred type downstream. That's what gives you both compile-time ergonomics and runtime safety, which is exactly the value Zod adds over `interface`."
**What they evaluate**
- **The four-stage V8 pipeline,** at least in outline.
- **Deoptimization understood as feedback-driven assumptions.**
- **Erasure**, and the resulting need for runtime validation.
**Probe:** "Why did a function get slower after you added one field to an object?" Or: "If types are erased, why is TypeScript still worth using?"
**Common wrong answers:** JS is "interpreted, therefore slow"; "JIT is just a cache"; TypeScript described as adding runtime safety; a bundler described as a compiler; WebAssembly recommended for small utility functions; deoptimization described as an engine bug.
**Trade-offs:**
- **AOT vs JIT:** AOT gives predictable, uniformly fast code and can't use runtime facts; JIT pays only for hot code and specializes on real data, and can deoptimize when reality changes.
- **Types vs runtime validation:** TypeScript gives excellent developer ergonomics with no runtime cost and cannot check external data; a validator like Zod gives real safety at the cost of running the check and maintaining a schema.
- **One bundle vs many modules:** a single bundle is fewer round trips and easier to cache as one asset, and rebuilds and cold-start parsing get slower as it grows; code splitting is the opposite trade.
- **WASM vs plain JS:** WASM is near-native and shares memory predictably with the host, and needs a toolchain, manual memory management and a boundary copy for every data transfer.
**Mistakes:** "JavaScript is interpreted" as a final answer; TypeScript types treated as a runtime guarantee; assuming a bundled app's stack trace maps back without source maps; reaching for WebAssembly to fix a network-bound problem; ignoring `deoptimize_bailout` in a profile.
**Mini task:** Write a function that receives `number` then, after ten thousand calls, receives a `string`. Take a CPU profile before and after with the Performance panel and find the deopt entry. Then make the call site monomorphic and confirm the profile improves.
**Resources:** V8's blog on Ignition, TurboFan and deoptimization; MDN's "JavaScript technologies" overview; the TypeScript handbook on type erasure and narrowing; MDN's WebAssembly pages.
**Self-check:** Can you describe the parse → bytecode → JIT → deopt sequence in under a minute, and explain in one sentence why `as` is not validation?

## 2.6.4 Processes vs Threads, and Concurrency Basics
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The difference between independent address spaces and shared-memory execution, and why JavaScript's single-threaded event loop makes CPU-bound work a correctness problem rather than a style one.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because event-loop blocking is the single most common cause of "the API got slow and we don't know why" in Node, and because the read-modify-write race on a balance is the canonical example of why you need a transaction. This topic is also the foundation of the Phase 10 system-design material.
**Prerequisites:** 2.6.2, 2.4.7.

**Key concepts**
- **Process:** an independent running program with its own memory space. Isolated, so a crash doesn't take down others, and communication needs IPC. Heavier to create.
- **Thread:** a unit of execution *within* a process, sharing its memory. Lightweight, and shared memory makes communication easy and also creates **race conditions** and **deadlocks**.
- **Concurrency vs parallelism:** concurrency is *dealing with* many things at once (interleaving), and parallelism is *doing* many things at once (multiple cores).
- **JS/Node model:** JavaScript runs on a **single thread** with an **event loop** for concurrency. I/O is handed off (to the OS or libuv's thread pool) and callbacks are queued. This is great for I/O-bound work and bad for CPU-bound work, which blocks everything. Solutions: `worker_threads`, `cluster` / multiple processes, offloading to a queue, or another service.
- **Browser:** the main thread runs JS, layout, and paint, so long tasks cause jank. Use Web Workers for heavy computation.
- **Context switching:** the OS saving and restoring execution state to switch tasks. It has a cost.
- **Blocking vs non-blocking, sync vs async I/O.**
- **Race condition:** the outcome depends on timing. **Deadlock:** two tasks each wait for the other's resource. (OS and concurrency depth in Phase 10.)

```js
// BLOCKS the entire Node server for the duration (CPU-bound on the main thread)
app.get('/hash', (req, res) => { res.send(crypto.pbkdf2Sync(pw, salt, 1e7, 64, 'sha512')); });

// FIX: async version uses the libuv thread pool
app.get('/hash', async (req, res) => { /* crypto.pbkdf2 (async), or worker thread */ });
```

- **The distinction is the memory space.** A process gets its own address space and needs IPC to talk to another; a thread shares one heap with its siblings and gets shared-memory access for free. That single fact explains both halves: processes are isolated and expensive to create, threads are cheap and unsafe without synchronisation.
- **Concurrency is a design property, parallelism is a hardware outcome.** You can be perfectly concurrent on one core by interleaving — that's what the event loop does. You need real cores for parallelism. Conflating them is what leads to "but Node is single-threaded, so it can't handle concurrency," which is wrong.
- **Node's concurrency is delegation, not parallelism.** When your handler awaits a database call, Node hands the work to the OS or libuv's thread pool, returns to the event loop, and picks up the next request. So one thread serves thousands of *concurrent* connections — but none of them run at the same instant.
- **Blocking the thread blocks every request.** A `pbkdf2Sync` with a high iteration count doesn't slow down that request; it stalls the whole process, including health checks, so a load balancer declares every instance dead at once. That's the failure mode to recognise: latency spikes that hit unrelated endpoints simultaneously.
- **Async ≠ parallel, and libuv's thread pool is small.** By default `UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE` is 4. `crypto.pbkdf2` async moves work off the main thread, which fixes the event loop, but 20 concurrent hashes contend for 4 threads. For genuinely CPU-heavy work, `worker_threads` gives you as many threads as cores with real parallelism.
- **The canonical race needs a transaction.** Two concurrent withdrawals doing `balance = balance - amount` can interleave between the read and the write and both succeed, or the last write wins and money appears. The fix is a database transaction with the right isolation, or better, a single atomic statement — `UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 100 WHERE balance >= 100` — which is race-free by construction and doesn't require a round trip for a read.
- **The browser's problem is the same problem with a different victim.** On the main thread, JS, style and layout share one thread, so a long computation doesn't just block requests — it stops the page from rendering. That's jank, and Web Workers are the browser equivalent of `worker_threads`.
- **Context switching is real but usually not your bottleneck.** It matters when you have thousands of threads fighting over a lock. With one thread and an event loop, you're not paying for switches — you're paying for whatever you've accidentally put on the critical path.
- **Deadlock needs a demonstrated example.** Two transactions each locking rows in a different order — A locks row 1 then 2, B locks 2 then 1 — and neither can proceed. The fix is a consistent lock ordering, or shorter transactions, or retrying on deadlock.

**Questions**
1. Process vs thread?
2. Concurrency vs parallelism?
3. If JS is single-threaded, how does Node handle thousands of connections?
4. What happens if you run a heavy loop in a Node request handler? How do you fix it?
5. What's a race condition? Give a web example.
6. What's a deadlock?
7. Is Node truly single-threaded?
8. What is `UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE`, and when would you change it?
9. How do you prevent two concurrent requests from overdrawing the same account?

**Model answer, Q4:** "It blocks the event loop, and that's worse than it sounds: while it's running, no other JavaScript executes in that process. So a 200 ms loop makes every in-flight request take 200 ms longer, and a 2-second loop makes the health check time out and a load balancer kill the instance — while it's perfectly healthy. The fix depends on why the loop is long. If it's I/O, don't block: use the async API and `await`. If it's genuinely CPU-bound, like hashing a password with high iterations or processing an image, move it off the thread — `worker_threads` for real parallelism, or the async libuv-backed API when the operation exists. If it's a burst of real CPU work, the answer is a queue and separate workers, so the web tier only ever does I/O."
**Model answer, Q5:** "A race condition is when the outcome depends on timing rather than on the values. Classic web example: two requests withdraw from the same account. Both read the balance as 100, both compute 100 minus 90, both write back, and the second write wins — you've paid out twice from a balance that only covered it once. Read-modify-write is the pattern that breaks it. Fix it at the data layer, not in application code: a single atomic statement, `UPDATE accounts SET balance = balance - 90 WHERE id = 1 AND balance >= 90`, checks and decrements in one operation. If you need a multi-statement transaction, use the appropriate isolation level and a consistent lock order. Doing it in JavaScript with a check-then-write doesn't help at all, because your event loop is single-threaded but your database isn't."
**What they evaluate**
- **Isolation and IPC for processes; shared memory and synchronisation for threads.**
- **Concurrency vs parallelism kept distinct.**
- **Event-loop blocking explained with real consequences,** not "it's slow."
**Probe:** "Your health check starts timing out and every instance goes unhealthy at the same moment. What happened?" Or: "Two requests overdraw one account. Where do you fix it?"
**Common wrong answers:** Node described as unable to be concurrent; the async API described as making work parallel; fixing a race with an in-process `Mutex` around a database write; a deadlock explained as a slow function; `worker_threads` recommended for a 5 ms loop; the browser main thread described as separate from JS.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Threads vs processes:** threads are cheap and share memory, and one bad write or data race corrupts everything; processes are isolated and safe, and need IPC plus more memory per instance.
- **Event loop vs worker threads:** the event loop is perfect for I/O and has zero context switching, and cannot use more than one core; workers use all cores at the cost of serialising data across a message boundary.
- **In-process mutex vs database atomicity:** a mutex is fast and simple, and doesn't help because the race is between database sessions, not threads in your process.
- **Retries vs serialisation:** retrying a deadlock or a transient failure is simple and needs a random backoff; serialising through a queue gives determinism at the cost of throughput and a new component.
**Mistakes:** a sync crypto or filesystem call in a request path; a busy-wait; assuming `await` means parallel; a check-then-write balance update; starting transactions and not committing or rolling back; `UV_THREADPOOL_SIZE` increased to "fix" a CPU bottleneck; lock ordering inconsistent across transactions.
**Mini task:** Block the event loop with a CPU loop and measure the latency of a second endpoint before, during and after. Then fix it with `worker_threads` and measure again. Then write the overdraw race with two concurrent requests and kill it with an atomic conditional update.
**Resources:** Node's "The Node.js Event Loop, Concurrency, and CPU-Bound Work" guide; MDN's Web Workers and `Worker` docs; libuv's documentation on the thread pool; the Postgres docs on isolation levels and deadlocks.
**Self-check:** Can you explain in 90 seconds why one Node thread handles thousands of connections, and can you name the exact SQL statement that makes a balance update race-free?

## 2.6.5 Character Encoding
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The layers between a character you can see and the bytes on disk or the wire — Unicode code points, encodings like UTF-8, and the decoding mistakes that produce mojibake.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because "why is `'😀'.length === 2`" is the most commonly asked question in this topic, and because emoji breaking in production is a real bug that has taken down real features. The reason it comes up is that JavaScript strings are UTF-16, which almost nobody expects.
**Prerequisites:** 2.6.1, 2.4.1.

**Key concepts**
- **ASCII:** 7-bit, 128 characters (English only).
- **Unicode:** a universal catalog assigning each character a **code point** (`U+1F600` 😀). It is *not* an encoding.
- **UTF-8:** variable-width encoding (1-4 bytes per code point), ASCII-compatible, the dominant web encoding. **UTF-16** (2 or 4 bytes) is what JS strings use internally. **UTF-32** is fixed 4 bytes.
- **Always:** `<meta charset="utf-8">`, `Content-Type: text/html; charset=utf-8`, UTF-8 in the DB (`utf8mb4` in MySQL, because MySQL's `utf8` is a 3-byte subset that can't store emoji!).
- **JS string gotchas**
```js
'😀'.length                 // 2 (UTF-16 code units, not characters)
[...'😀'].length            // 1 (iterates by code point)
'é'.length                  // 1 or 2 depending on normalization (é vs e + combining accent)
'é'.normalize('NFC') === 'é'.normalize('NFC')   // true
new TextEncoder().encode('é').length   // 2 bytes in UTF-8
```
- **Mojibake:** garbled text (`Ã©` instead of `é`) from decoding bytes with the wrong encoding.
- **Base64:** encodes binary as ASCII text (about 33% bigger). It's for transport, **not encryption**. **URL encoding** (`encodeURIComponent`) escapes unsafe characters in URLs. **HTML escaping** prevents XSS. All three are *encoding*, not security (Phase 7: hashing vs encryption vs encoding).

- **Unicode is a catalog; an encoding is how you write those numbers down.** The clearest analogy: Unicode is the list of character codes, UTF-8 is a way of writing those numbers as bytes, and UTF-16 is another. Decoding with the wrong one gives you valid-looking garbage, which is mojibake — `é` is two UTF-8 bytes, and reading those bytes as Latin-1 gives you `Ã©`.
- **`'😀'.length === 2` because JS strings are a sequence of UTF-16 code units.** `U+1F600` is above the BMP, so it doesn't fit in one 16-bit unit and is stored as a surrogate *pair*. `.length` counts units, not characters. `[...'😀']` gives one because the spread uses the iterator protocol, which steps by code point. This is the single most useful fact in the topic, and it has a practical consequence: anything that indexes a JS string by `.length` can split a surrogate pair and produce a lone surrogate.
- **Normalization is a different axis entirely.** `é` can be one code point (NFC, precomposed) or two (NFD, a base `e` plus a combining accent U+0301). They look identical and compare as different strings, which breaks unique indexes and equality checks on names. Normalize to NFC before storing or comparing — that's the fix, and it's why search for a user's name can miss them.
- **UTF-8 is a variable-width encoding: ASCII is 1 byte, Latin-1 accented is 2, CJK is 3, emoji are 4.** That's why `'é'` is 2 bytes but `'😀'` is 4, and why "100 characters" is not "100 bytes" — which is the whole problem with truncating at a byte limit.
- **Truncating by bytes can split a multi-byte sequence** and produce invalid UTF-8, which then fails to decode on the other side. The fix is `TextEncoder`/`TextDecoder` with a streaming decode, or backtracking to a character boundary, or doing the truncation with a library that counts characters rather than bytes.
- **MySQL's `utf8` is not UTF-8.** It's a 3-byte subset, so emoji fail to insert with an unhelpful error while Latin text works fine. Use `utf8mb4`. This is the single most common encoding bug you'll meet in a PHP or legacy codebase.
- **Base64, URL-encoding and HTML-escaping are three different encodings for three different problems.** Base64 makes binary safe for text channels, URL-encoding makes a string safe inside a URL, HTML-escaping makes text safe inside markup. **None of them is encryption** — anyone can decode any of them without a key. Confusing base64 with encryption is still a routine finding in security reviews.
- **The encoding must be declared at every hop**, and a mismatch anywhere shows up as garbage: file on disk, the HTTP `Content-Type` header, the `<meta charset>`, the database column, the connection's character set, and the JDBC or driver setting. When mojibake appears, the fix is to find the hop where it changed, which is why "declare UTF-8 everywhere and never rely on a default" is the only practical policy.

**Questions**
1. ASCII vs Unicode vs UTF-8?
2. Why does `'😀'.length` equal 2?
3. What is Base64 and is it secure?
4. Why would a database or page show garbled characters?
5. Bytes vs characters: how would you truncate a string to 100 *bytes* safely for UTF-8?
6. What's the difference between NFC and NFD, and why does it matter in a database?
7. Why does MySQL's `utf8` reject emoji?
8. How do you safely count "characters" in a string that might contain emoji?
9. Why is `encodeURIComponent` not a security control?

**Model answer, Q2:** "Because JavaScript strings are a sequence of UTF-16 code units, and `.length` counts code units rather than characters. The emoji `U+1F600` is above the Basic Multilingual Plane, so it needs a value above `0xFFFF` and is stored as a surrogate pair — two code units — even though it's one character to a human. Iterating with `for...of` or spreading the string uses the string iterator, which steps by code point, so `[...'😀'].length` is 1. The practical consequence is that slicing by `.length` can cut a surrogate pair in half and leave a lone surrogate, which is invalid, and that's why anything user-facing that truncates should go through `Array.from(str)` or `Intl.Segmenter` rather than raw slicing."
**Model answer, Q4:** "It means two hops disagreed about the encoding. The bytes were written with one encoding and read with another. I'd trace it: check the `<meta charset>` in the HTML, the `Content-Type: ...; charset=utf-8` response header, the column's character set in the database, and the connection's character set in the driver. If the right characters arrive at the server and leave as garbage, the mismatch is in the response encoding; if the server never received them, it's the request body or the file encoding. The MySQL trap is worth checking specifically, because `utf8` there is only a 3-byte subset and silently can't store 4-byte emoji — that's `utf8mb4`."
**What they evaluate**
- **Unicode vs encoding kept distinct.**
- **Surrogate pairs** as the mechanism behind `.length === 2`.
- **Normalization treated as a real bug source,** not a footnote.
**Probe:** "A user's name matches in one table and not in another. Where do you look?" Or: "Why does `encodeURIComponent` not make a URL safe?"
**Common wrong answers:** UTF-8 described as a character set rather than an encoding; "emoji are 4 bytes so `.length` is 4"; mojibake attributed only to the HTML file; base64 treated as "obfuscation" or light encryption; normalization ignored; MySQL `utf8mb4` recommended with no explanation of what broke.
**Trade-offs:**
- **Byte-oriented vs character-oriented truncation:** bytes match what a database column or buffer actually enforces, and can split a character; characters are always safe to display, and don't match the storage limit.
- **NFC vs NFD storage:** NFC is one code point per character and slightly larger; NFD composes better for linguistic work and matches what some OS input methods produce.
- **`Intl.Segmenter` vs manual iteration:** `Segmenter` handles grapheme clusters — flags, skin tones, combining accents — correctly, and isn't available everywhere; `Array.from` handles code points and still splits a cluster.
- **Base64 vs a signed URL:** base64 avoids characters that break URLs and is trivially reversible; a signed URL with an expiry and an HMAC can't be altered or replayed.
**Mistakes:** MySQL `utf8` instead of `utf8mb4`; no `charset` on the response; slicing strings with `.length` and breaking emoji; comparing names without normalizing; `Buffer.from(s, 'binary')` and other silent latin1 coercions; a byte limit applied as a character limit; assuming base64 hides a token.
**Mini task:** Take the string `"héllo 😀"`, and compute its length in code units, code points, and UTF-8 bytes — then the Base64 and URL-encoded forms and their sizes. Then build a `truncate(str, 100)` that can't split a character, and one that can't split a grapheme cluster with `Intl.Segmenter`.
**Resources:** Spolsky's "The Absolute Minimum Every Software Developer Must Know About Unicode"; MDN's `TextEncoder`, `String.prototype.normalize`, and `Intl.Segmenter` pages; Unicode's Normalization Forms annex; `WHATWG Encoding` for the spec-level detail.
**Self-check:** Can you explain the surrogate pair in one sentence, name one thing normalization fixes that encoding does not, and say why MySQL `utf8` isn't UTF-8?

## 2.6.6 Data Formats: JSON, XML, YAML, CSV
🟡 Intermediate · **Must-know**

**Definition:** The interchange and configuration formats a web system uses to move structured data, and the specific way each one loses or mangles information you didn't expect it to.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because JSON is on every API boundary you will ever design, and its failure modes — `undefined` silently vanishing, `Date` becoming a string and never coming back, `NaN` becoming `null` — are the ones that actually cause bugs. YAML's type coercion and CSV's quoting rules are classic follow-ups.
**Prerequisites:** 2.6.5, 2.4.1.

**Key concepts**

| Format | Strengths | Weaknesses | Typical use |
|---|---|---|---|
| **JSON** | Ubiquitous, lightweight, native in JS | No comments, no dates, no `undefined`/`NaN`, no trailing commas | APIs, config |
| **XML** | Schemas, namespaces, attributes, mature tooling | Verbose | SOAP, RSS/Atom, SVG, legacy and enterprise |
| **YAML** | Human-friendly, comments, anchors | Whitespace-sensitive, surprising type coercion (`no` → boolean in YAML 1.1, "Norway problem") | Config (Docker Compose, CI, Kubernetes) |
| **CSV** | Simple tabular, spreadsheet-friendly | No types, quoting/escaping edge cases, no nesting | Exports, data import |
| **Binary (Protobuf, MessagePack)** | Compact, fast, schema-enforced | Not human-readable | gRPC, high-performance systems |

**JSON gotchas**
```js
JSON.stringify({ a: undefined, b: () => {}, c: NaN, d: new Date(0) });
// → {"c":null,"d":"1970-01-01T00:00:00.000Z"}
// undefined and function props are dropped; NaN → null; Date → ISO string (not revived on parse)

JSON.parse('{"a":1,}')    // SyntaxError: no trailing commas
JSON.parse(userInput)     // always wrap in try/catch
// JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(x)) as a deep clone is lossy: use structuredClone
```
- **Circular references** throw in `JSON.stringify`.
- **JSON Schema/Zod** validate shape at runtime.
- **Dates:** transmit as ISO 8601 strings in UTC (`2025-01-31T10:00:00Z`) or epoch milliseconds. Convert for display on the client.
- **Large IDs:** send as strings if they exceed 2^53.

- **`JSON.stringify` silently drops data, and that's the danger.** `undefined` properties, functions, and `Symbol` keys just disappear — no error, no warning. So a typo in a key name, or an object you meant to serialize but constructed with an `undefined` field, produces a smaller JSON object that looks plausible and is missing something. This is why "the API returned no value for `userId`" is often a serialization bug upstream.
- **JSON has no date type, so `Date` becomes a string and stays a string.** `JSON.stringify(new Date(0))` gives `"1970-01-01T00:00:00.000Z"`, and parsing it back gives a *string*, not a Date — so `parsed.createdAt.getFullYear()` is `undefined is not a function`. Fix it at the boundary with a schema validator that coerces the field, or a custom reviver, and transmit ISO 8601 in UTC.
- **`NaN`, `Infinity` and `-Infinity` all become `null`.** That's a great way to lose a value without noticing: a division by zero in a report goes out as `null` and nobody knows it was ever a number. Same for `NaN` inside arrays, where `undefined` becomes `null` instead of being dropped.
- **BigInt throws, rather than degrading.** `JSON.stringify({ n: 1n })` raises a `TypeError` — the one case where JSON is loudly broken instead of quietly lossy, which is arguably a kindness.
- **`JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(x))` loses Dates, Maps, Sets, RegExps, `undefined`, class prototypes, and cycles.** It's a clone that discards type information, so it's a debugging tool and nothing more. `structuredClone` handles Dates, Maps, Sets and cycles correctly and is available in Node 17+ and all modern browsers.
- **`JSON.parse` on untrusted input is two separate risks.** One is a `SyntaxError` — always `try/catch`, because malformed input is normal, not exceptional. The other is that valid JSON can still be the wrong shape, which no parse step catches; that's what Zod or a JSON Schema is for. And never `eval` the string — that's arbitrary code execution, and it's how injection gets in.
- **YAML's real danger is type coercion and indentation.** `no`, `yes`, `on`, `off`, `y`, `n` become booleans in YAML 1.1, so `country: NO` parses as `false` — the Norway problem. An indentation slip silently changes the structure rather than erroring. And `password: 12345` becomes an integer, so you need quotes to keep a string. YAML is excellent for config a human maintains and a poor choice for anything machine-generated.
- **CSV's quoting rules are where importers break.** A field may contain the delimiter, newlines and quotes if the whole field is wrapped in quotes, and embedded quotes are escaped by doubling — so `"a ""quote"" inside"` is one field containing `a "quote" inside`. Then there's the BOM, the line ending, the delimiter, and the fact that a leading zero turns `007` into `7`. Excel will also happily execute a formula in a CSV cell, which is a genuine injection vector when you export user data.
- **Protobuf is compact for a structural reason, not a syntactic one.** Fields are transmitted as *numbers* rather than names, and integers use variable-length encoding — a small number takes one byte. Both ends already know the schema from the `.proto` file, so the names are never sent. The trade is that you need the schema to read the payload and it isn't human-inspectable.
- **Choosing:** JSON for APIs and anything machine-to-machine; YAML for human-authored config; CSV only for exports and spreadsheet round-trips; XML when you need schemas, namespaces, or you're in an enterprise system that already speaks it; Protobuf when bandwidth and typed contracts between services matter more than debuggability.

**Questions**
1. JSON vs XML vs YAML. When use each?
2. What can't JSON represent? What happens to `undefined`, `Date`, `NaN`?
3. Why is `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(obj))` a flawed deep clone?
4. How do you safely parse JSON from an untrusted source?
5. What are the pitfalls of CSV parsing?
6. Why is Protobuf more compact than JSON?
7. What is the Norway problem?
8. A client says the API "dropped" a field. What do you check first?
9. When would you choose CSV over JSON, and what would you watch out for?

**Model answer, Q2:** "JSON has no date, no undefined, no NaN, no infinity, no comments, no trailing commas, and no way to express a type. `JSON.stringify` *drops* `undefined` properties and functions entirely — so you get a smaller object with no warning. `NaN` and `Infinity` become `null`. A `Date` becomes an ISO string and stays a string after parsing, so you have to validate and revive it. And `BigInt` throws a `TypeError` rather than degrading, which is the loudest failure in the set. The pattern I use is: parse as `unknown`, validate with Zod, and explicitly decide what each date and each numeric edge case should be — because every one of these is a silent data-loss path."
**Model answer, Q4:** "Three separate things. Syntax: `try/catch` around `parse`, because malformed JSON is ordinary input from the outside world, not an exceptional event. Size: reject oversized bodies before parsing, so you don't become the thing that exhausts memory. And shape: parsing successfully proves nothing about the content, so validate against a schema with Zod or a JSON Schema and use the inferred type afterwards. And never `eval` the string — that's code execution. TypeScript's `as SomeType` is a cast the compiler can't check, so it doesn't count as validation."
**What they evaluate**
- **Silent vs loud failure modes named precisely** — dropping `undefined`, `Date` to string, `NaN` to `null`.
- **Parsing separated from validating.**
- **A reasoned format choice,** not a preference list.
**Probe:** "The API returns `createdAt` and the client calls `.getFullYear()` on it. What happened?" Or: "Your import of a customer list lost a leading zero and turned a country into false. Which format is this?"
**Common wrong answers:** JSON described as lossy only in the abstract; `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(x))` recommended as a clone; `JSON.parse` treated as validation; YAML described as "JSON with comments" without the coercion risk; CSV described as fine because it's simple; Protobuf's compactness explained as "no whitespace".
**Trade-offs:**
- **JSON vs XML:** JSON is smaller, native to JS and trivially parsed; XML has schemas, namespaces and mature enterprise tooling, and is verbose with manual escaping.
- **JSON vs YAML:** YAML has comments and reads better for humans; JSON is unambiguous, safe to generate, and harder to get wrong.
- **JSON vs Protobuf:** JSON is self-describing and debuggable with no shared schema; Protobuf is smaller and faster with an enforced contract, and unreadable without the `.proto`.
- **CSV vs anything else:** CSV opens in a spreadsheet and needs no library; it has no types, no nesting, and quoting rules that break naive parsers.
- **Text vs binary over HTTP:** JSON compresses extremely well with gzip, which closes much of Protobuf's size advantage for typical payloads.
**Mistakes:**
- **JSON** — `JSON.parse` with no `try/catch` and no size limit; `as` used instead of validation; `Date` round-tripped as a string and used as a Date; assuming an `undefined` field reaches the client; `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(x))` as a clone.
- **YAML** — unquoted `no`/`yes`/`on`/date-like scalars; tabs for indentation; a hand-edited indent slip silently changing structure.
- **CSV** — naive `split(',')` on quoted fields; ignoring BOM and CRLF; assuming a leading zero survives; trusting an export opened in Excel, where a leading `=` becomes a formula.
- **XML** — assuming it's obsolete rather than schema-driven; string-concatenated markup instead of escaping.
**Mini task:** Take a rich object — Date, Map, Set, `undefined`, `NaN`, a BigInt, a cycle — and round-trip it through `JSON.stringify`/`parse`, then through `structuredClone`, and tabulate exactly what each one did to each field. Then write a `parseCsv` that handles quoted delimiters, embedded newlines and doubled quotes.
**Resources:** MDN's `JSON.parse`, `JSON.stringify` and `structuredClone` pages; the Protobuf documentation on encoding and field numbers; the YAML spec on type resolution and the `no`/`yes` problem; RFC 4180 for CSV; OWASP's guidance on JSON and CSV injection.
**Self-check:** Can you list three things `JSON.stringify` silently loses, and can you explain why valid JSON still needs validation?

## 2.6.7 Processes vs threads
🔴 Advanced · **Must-know** · the concurrency model underneath Node, browsers, workers, and every "it's slow" conversation

**Definition:** A process is an isolated address space running its own program; a thread is an independently scheduled stream of execution inside one process, sharing that process's memory.
**Why interviewers ask:** Because it explains behaviour you'll otherwise memorise as magic — why one `JSON.parse` of a huge payload freezes a Node server while everything else stays responsive, why `Promise.all` doesn't parallelise CPU work, why a shared array can tear, and why a crash in one part of a program can take down all of it. Candidates who can't state the isolation difference usually also can't explain worker_threads or why the event loop isn't parallel. It's the foundation under 2.6.1's event loop and 2.6.2's memory model.
**Prerequisites:** 2.6.1, 2.6.2.

**Key concepts**
- **Process = isolated memory; thread = shared memory, separate stack.** That single distinction produces almost every practical consequence.
```text
Process A                          Process B
┌──────────────┐                   ┌──────────────┐
│ code + heap  │  ← separate →     │ code + heap  │
│ ┌──────────┐ │                   │ ┌──────────┐ │
│ │ thread 1 │ │                   │ │ thread 1 │ │
│ │ thread 2 │ │                   │ └──────────┘ │
│ └──────────┘ │                   └──────────────┘
└──────────────┘
```
- **Threads within a process share the heap, so communication is cheap and races are possible.** Separate processes need IPC — pipes, sockets, or shared memory — and pay serialisation or setup cost for it.
- **A process crash is contained; a thread crash usually isn't.** The OS protects processes from each other's memory; within one process a segfault takes everything down. That's why a native addon crash in Node kills the whole server.
- **Concurrency vs parallelism:** concurrency is dealing with many things at once (interleaving), parallelism is doing many at once (literally simultaneous). A single-threaded event loop is concurrent, not parallel.
- **The event loop is single-threaded for your JavaScript,** so a long synchronous function blocks everything on that thread — including timers and I/O callbacks. Nothing else in that process runs until it returns.
```js
// Blocking: nothing else happens during this, on this thread
const big = JSON.parse(hugeString);          // seconds of frozen UI
crypto.createHash('sha256').update(hugeBuffer).digest('hex');  // sync hashing, same problem

// Non-blocking: the event loop stays free
const hash = crypto.createHash('sha256');    // incremental, interleaved
fs.createReadStream(path).on('data', chunk => hash.update(chunk));
await asyncFunctionThatDoesIo();             // yields instead of blocking
```
- **`Promise.all` gives concurrency, not parallelism** — awaiting several promises interleaves I/O but runs the callbacks on one thread. CPU-bound promises still queue behind each other.
- **`worker_threads` add real parallelism in Node,** each with its own V8 isolate and heap, communicating by `postMessage` (structured-clone copy) or shared `SharedArrayBuffer`.
```js
import { Worker } from 'node:worker_threads';
const worker = new Worker('./hash-worker.js', { workerData: hugeBuffer });
worker.on('message', digest => console.log(digest));   // off the main thread
```
- **Offloading to a worker, a child process, or a job queue** are three different answers to the same problem, chosen by cost: worker for CPU in-process, child process for isolation or a different runtime, queue for durability and horizontal scale.
- **Data races appear where shared memory is shared.** Two threads writing the same object can interleave a read and a write, producing torn or partially-updated state — the reason web workers use structured clone or a `SharedArrayBuffer` with `Atomics` instead of handing over live objects.
- **Deadlock is threads waiting on each other,** and it's rare in JS because the event loop's single thread rarely holds two locks at once — but multi-worker designs reintroduce it.
- **Threads need explicit synchronisation** (mutexes, semaphores, atomics) whenever memory is shared; processes don't, because isolation is the synchronisation.

**Questions**
1. What's the difference between a process and a thread?
2. What's the difference between concurrency and parallelism?
3. Why does the Node event loop block on heavy CPU work?
4. Does `Promise.all` run things in parallel?
5. How would you handle a CPU-heavy task in Node without freezing the server?
6. When would you choose a child process over a worker thread?
7. Why can't you just share an object between threads?
8. What's a data race?

**Model answer, Q1:** "A process is an independent running program with its own memory space; a thread is one of several independently scheduled execution streams inside a process, sharing that process's memory. So threads are cheap to create and can talk without serialising, but they need locks; processes are expensive and isolated, so one crashing doesn't take down the others and communication costs more."
**Model answer, Q3:** "Because the event loop is a single thread, and a synchronous CPU-bound call never yields. Until `JSON.parse` of a 200MB string returns, that thread can't run any other callback, so timers and I/O completions all sit in the queue. I/O is fine because the kernel does the work and the loop stays free; it's only work that occupies the JS thread itself — parsing, sorting, hashing, compression — that stalls everything."
**Model answer, Q4:** "No. It gives concurrency: the loop interleaves the promises' I/O so the total time is roughly the slowest, not the sum. But the continuations still run one at a time on the main thread, so CPU-bound work inside those promises serialises and adds up. For actual parallelism I need a worker thread or a separate process."
**Model answer, Q5:** "Depends on the library. If it exposes an async or streaming API I'd use that so it yields between chunks and the loop stays free. If it's genuinely synchronous with no async option, I'd move it to a worker thread so it runs on its own thread with its own heap, and post the result back. For long jobs I'd push it to a queue with a worker process, because that survives a deploy and can scale horizontally — an in-process worker dies with the server."
**Model answer, Q6:** "When I want fault isolation, a different runtime or native dependency, or a hard memory boundary. A worker thread shares the process lifetime, so an out-of-memory crash in it can still take down the process; a child process doesn't. Child processes cost more — spawn time, serialised messages, their own memory footprint — so for pure CPU work on big data with no crash risk, a worker is the better default."
**Model answer, Q7:** "Because if they shared the live object, both threads could mutate it at once, and any operation running in two steps could be interrupted halfway by the other thread — that's a data race. JavaScript avoids it by copying: passing to a worker uses structured clone, so the other side gets its own snapshot. When you genuinely need shared memory you use `SharedArrayBuffer`, and then you're responsible for synchronising access with `Atomics` yourself."

**What they evaluate**
- **Stating the isolation difference first,** because that's the answer that generalises.
- **Distinguishing concurrency from parallelism without hedging.**
- **Knowing a real remedy** (worker thread, streaming, or a queue) rather than just describing the problem.
**Probe:** "This endpoint does four 500ms `await`s on independent HTTP calls. What's the wall-clock time, and why isn't it 2 seconds?" (Expect: ~500ms, because I/O overlaps on the event loop; then: "and if those four were CPU hashes instead?")
**Common wrong answers:** "Node is multithreaded so it handles requests in parallel" (the loop is single-threaded; libuv has a thread pool for *some* I/O, not your JS); `Promise.all` described as parallel; "threads are faster than processes because there's no overhead" (ignoring lock costs and the risk of races); "use `setImmediate` to make it non-blocking" (it only reorders the queue); "Node can't do parallel work at all".
**Trade-offs:**
- **Worker thread vs child process:** workers start faster and share the address space, but share fate and memory pressure; child processes are slower and cost serialised messaging, but give isolation and independent memory — the right call when untrusted code or a native crash is involved.
- **Sharing memory vs copying messages:** shared memory avoids copying large buffers and is much faster, but every access must be synchronised with atomics and reasoning gets hard; structured-clone messages are safe and simple, at a real cost for large data.
- **In-process work vs an external queue:** a worker thread is simple and fast for single-machine work, but dies with the process and caps you at one machine; a queue gives retries, durability and horizontal scaling, and costs you a service to run.
**Mistakes:** Blocking the event loop with a sync hash, parse or large `JSON.stringify` and wondering why p99 latency spiked; assuming `Promise.all` gives parallelism for CPU work; passing a live object to a worker and expecting shared mutations; sharing a plain object across workers without `SharedArrayBuffer`; unbounded `Promise.all` over thousands of requests; assuming a thread can be "killed" safely.
**Mini task:** Write a server that hashes a large buffer synchronously and measure the latency of every other route while it runs. Then switch the hash to a worker thread and to the streaming API, and tabulate the p99 of the other routes across all three. It demonstrates the concept better than any explanation.
**Resources:** Node's official `worker_threads` and `Atomics` docs; MDN's Web Workers guide and the same-origin policy that forces copying; the "Workers and the Event Loop" chapter of *Node.js Design Patterns*; `flamegraphs`/perf tooling docs for finding which synchronous call is blocking.
**Self-check:** Can you explain in one sentence why a heavy `JSON.parse` freezes an otherwise idle server, and name two ways to move that work off the main thread?

## Interview Questions (2.6) — CS Basics for Web
**Definition:** The group-level practice set for the computer-science foundations a web interview will assume. These are the questions that get asked as "why does this behave this way?" — so the answer needs a *mechanism*, not a rule.

**Questions**
1. Why is `0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3`? How do you handle money?
2. What's the largest safe integer in JS, and what happens beyond it?
3. Why do we send 64-bit IDs as strings?
4. Stack vs heap? What's a stack overflow?
5. What causes a memory leak in JS, and how do you prove one?
6. Is JavaScript compiled or interpreted? What is a JIT?
7. Why do TypeScript types not exist at runtime, and where do you validate instead?
8. Process vs thread? Concurrency vs parallelism?
9. How does Node handle thousands of connections on one thread?
10. What breaks if you run a CPU-heavy loop in a request handler?
11. What's a race condition? Give a web example and the fix.
12. ASCII vs Unicode vs UTF-8?
13. Why is `'😀'.length === 2`?
14. Is Base64 encryption?
15. What can JSON not represent?
16. Why is `JSON.parse(JSON.stringify(obj))` not a deep clone?
17. What's the Norway problem?
18. Why does MySQL's `utf8` reject emoji?

**Model answer, Q1:** "Both `0.1` and `0.2` are stored as the nearest binary fraction to those decimals, so adding them leaves a residue — that's IEEE 754, not a JS bug. For general comparisons I'd use an epsilon tolerance. For money I wouldn't use a float at all: integer minor units — cents — with all arithmetic in integers and formatting only at the display boundary. The error compounds, so reconciliation becomes a real job."
**Model answer, Q4:** "The stack holds call frames and locals and is fixed-size, so unbounded recursion overflows it in milliseconds. The heap holds dynamically allocated objects and is GC-managed, so it fills over hours when objects accumulate and stay reachable. So the stack overflow is a depth problem — fix the recursion or use an explicit stack — and the OOM is a size problem — find the retainer. Say 'conceptually' when you describe where variables live, because engines optimise."
**Model answer, Q9:** "By delegation rather than parallelism. When a handler awaits a database call, Node hands the work to the OS or libuv's thread pool and returns to the event loop to serve the next request. So one thread keeps thousands of connections *concurrent* — but no two run at the same instant. That's why it's excellent for I/O-bound work and why CPU-bound work is catastrophic: it blocks the one thread, which stalls every request including health checks."
**Model answer, Q13:** "JS strings are sequences of UTF-16 code units and `.length` counts code units. `U+1F600` is above the Basic Multilingual Plane, so it needs a value above `0xFFFF` and is stored as a surrogate pair — two code units, one character to a human. Iterating with `for...of` or spreading uses the string iterator, which steps by code point, so `[...'😀'].length` is 1. The consequence is that slicing by `.length` can cut a pair in half and leave a lone surrogate."
**Model answer, Q15:** "No dates, no `undefined`, no `NaN` or infinity, no comments, no trailing commas, no types. `stringify` *drops* `undefined` properties and functions with no warning, turns `NaN` and `Infinity` into `null`, converts `Date` to an ISO string that stays a string after parsing, and throws on `BigInt`. So the pattern is: parse as `unknown`, validate with a schema, and explicitly decide what each date and numeric edge case should be."
**What they evaluate**
- **A mechanism behind every answer** — the follow-up is where the mark is.
- **Distinguishing depth from size, concurrency from parallelism, catalog from encoding.**
- **Silence as the recurring theme** in floats, JSON, and encoding.

## Group 2.6 Wrap-Up — CS Basics for Web

**Common mistakes across 2.6:** floats for money; assuming `.length` equals characters; treating Base64 as encryption; ignoring event-loop blocking; non-UTF-8 databases; `JSON.parse` without `try/catch`.

**Mini tasks**
1. Write a function that safely adds two dollar amounts as integer cents and formats them.
2. Reproduce and fix a memory leak: a Node server that stores per-request data in a global array. Observe it with `process.memoryUsage()`, then fix it.
3. Block the event loop with a CPU loop, measure the latency of another endpoint, then fix with `worker_threads`.
4. Encode and decode "héllo 😀" in UTF-8 bytes, Base64, and URL-encoded forms, and write down the byte lengths.

**Resources:** *Code: The Hidden Language of Computer Hardware and Software* (Petzold), CS50 (Harvard, free), javascript.info (Numbers, Strings, Memory), "The Absolute Minimum Every Software Developer Must Know About Unicode" (Joel Spolsky), MDN (`TextEncoder`, `BigInt`, `structuredClone`), Node.js docs (Event Loop guide).

**Self-check:** Can you explain event-loop blocking, why `'😀'.length === 2`, and why floats are wrong for money, in 90 seconds each?

# PHASE 2 RAPID-FIRE (answer aloud in under 30 seconds each)

| # | Question | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | What happens when you type a URL? | URL parse → DNS → TCP → TLS → HTTP → server → render |
| 2 | 401 vs 403? | Unauthenticated vs unauthorized |
| 3 | A vs CNAME record? | IP vs alias; CNAME can't be at the apex |
| 4 | Why lower TTL before migration? | Caches expire sooner, so faster cutover |
| 5 | `localhost` vs `0.0.0.0`? | This machine only vs all interfaces |
| 6 | `chmod 755`? | rwxr-xr-x |
| 7 | `kill` vs `kill -9`? | SIGTERM allows cleanup, SIGKILL doesn't |
| 8 | `>` vs `>>`? | Overwrite vs append |
| 9 | Find who's using port 3000? | `lsof -i :3000` |
| 10 | Merge vs rebase? | Preserve history vs linear rewritten history |
| 11 | Undo a pushed commit? | `git revert` |
| 12 | `reset --soft` vs `--mixed` vs `--hard`? | Discard changes vs keep staged |
| 13 | How to recover lost commits? | `git reflog` |
| 14 | `fetch` vs `pull`? | Download only vs download and integrate |
| 15 | `--force-with-lease`? | Force push that refuses to overwrite others' work |
| 16 | Committed a secret? | Rotate first, then clean history |
| 17 | Value vs reference? | Primitives copy, objects share a pointer |
| 18 | `==` vs `===`? | Coercion vs strict |
| 19 | `\|\|` vs `??`? | Falsy vs nullish |
| 20 | Pure function? | Same input → same output, no side effects |
| 21 | Recursion needs? | Base case + progress toward it |
| 22 | Why is naive fib slow? | Exponential recomputation → memoize |
| 23 | OOP's four pillars? | Encapsulation, abstraction, inheritance, polymorphism |
| 24 | Composition vs inheritance? | Has-a vs is-a; prefer composition |
| 25 | `dependencies` vs `devDependencies`? | Runtime vs build/test |
| 26 | `npm install` vs `npm ci`? | May update lockfile vs exact reproducible install |
| 27 | `^1.2.3` means? | `>=1.2.3 <2.0.0` |
| 28 | Linter vs formatter? | Bugs/quality vs style |
| 29 | Why `0.1 + 0.2 !== 0.3`? | IEEE 754 binary floats; use integer cents |
| 30 | `'😀'.length`? | 2 (UTF-16 code units) |
| 31 | Stack vs heap? | Call frames/locals vs dynamic objects |
| 32 | Is JS compiled or interpreted? | JIT-compiled in modern engines |
| 33 | Process vs thread? | Isolated memory vs shared memory |
| 34 | How does Node handle many connections single-threaded? | Event loop + non-blocking I/O |
| 35 | Is Base64 encryption? | No, it's encoding |

# PHASE 2 FINAL READINESS CHECKLIST

**How the web works**
- [ ] I can narrate the URL-to-render flow in 2 minutes, then go deeper on any layer
- [ ] I can explain DNS record types, TTL, and recursive resolution
- [ ] I know the status codes and methods, and when to use each
- [ ] I can run the outside-in troubleshooting ladder

**Terminal and Linux**
- [ ] I can navigate, search, and process logs with pipes, `grep`, `awk`, `sort`, `uniq`
- [ ] I understand permissions (numeric and symbolic), processes, and signals
- [ ] I can find and kill a process by port, and use SSH keys
- [ ] I can write a safe bash script with `set -euo pipefail`

**Git and GitHub**
- [ ] I can explain staging, commits, branches, and HEAD as a model
- [ ] I can merge, rebase, resolve conflicts, and clean up history interactively
- [ ] I can recover from any common mistake (reset, revert, reflog, stash, bisect)
- [ ] I know trunk-based vs GitHub Flow vs Gitflow, and I write conventional commits
- [ ] I have a working GitHub Actions CI file

**Programming fundamentals**
- [ ] I can explain types, value vs reference, coercion, and falsy values
- [ ] I can write recursive and memoized solutions and explain stack depth
- [ ] I can discuss OOP vs FP trade-offs with code examples
- [ ] I handle errors deliberately (custom errors, async, no swallowing)
- [ ] I solved the 10 warm-up problems cleanly

**Developer environment**
- [ ] I use VS Code shortcuts, debugger, and DevTools fluently
- [ ] I understand lockfiles, semver ranges, `npm ci`, and dependency hygiene
- [ ] My repo has ESLint, Prettier, hooks, typecheck, and CI
- [ ] I have a clear, honest stance on using AI assistants

**CS basics**
- [ ] I can explain floats, safe integers, and BigInt, and I use integer cents for money
- [ ] I can explain stack/heap, GC, and common leaks
- [ ] I can explain JIT, transpilation, and why TypeScript types vanish at runtime
- [ ] I can explain event-loop blocking and process vs thread
- [ ] I can explain UTF-8 vs UTF-16 and Base64 vs encryption
- [ ] I know JSON's limits and alternatives
